This is worth noting because a "remove a feature" task naturally focuses on the code that *implements* the feature, while the breakage risk concentrates in the code that merely *reads* it — and reads are scattered wider. The check that actually caught it was a plain grep for the field name across the whole of `backend/src` and `frontend/src` before changing anything, which also turned up a now-inaccurate response-shape comment in `frontend/src/api/stock.js` and three stale references in `04_API_SPEC.md`. Grepping for the removed identifier — rather than reasoning about which files "should" be involved — is what made the blast radius factual instead of assumed.

### Why the color-loading gate is phrased positively ("has settled") rather than negatively ("is not loading")
The minimal patch for the pre-fetch gap would have been `useState(true)` on the loading boolean — one character-ish change, and the first render would say "Loading…" correctly. It was rejected because it fixes the symptom at exactly one point in the lifecycle. The screen doesn't just load once: `resetLookup()` clears everything between articles, and a boolean initialised `true` is only `true` at mount, so the second article looked up in the same session would hit the identical hole again. The three-state version fixes it everywhere by construction, because "nothing has been asked about this article yet" has a name (`idle`) that `resetLookup` can return to.

The gate also had to be written as `colorListsSettled` (all required lists are `'loaded'`) rather than `!anythingLoading`, and those are not equivalent — they differ precisely at the `idle` moments, since an un-started fetch has nothing in flight either. Phrasing it negatively would have silently reintroduced the bug while looking like it had been fixed. Which lists count depends on the source: in bootstrap mode the picker is populated from `globalColors`, so `resolvedColors` having settled says nothing useful on its own. That detail matters because `colorBootstrapMode` flips true in the same batch that `resolvedColors` settles — one render *before* the `globalColors` request even starts — which is why the old code briefly claimed "No colors exist in the system yet" while a color did exist. The measurement caught that second, previously unnoticed instance in 10/10 bootstrap runs, and the same gate closes both.

### Why POST /api/products defaults an omitted categoryId to "Others" instead of requiring it
Round 12 made `Product.categoryId` a required foreign key, but the task was explicitly scoped to schema-and-migration only — no category-picker UI, no `GET /api/categories` endpoint. Left alone, that combination is a contradiction: the schema demands a value the frontend has no way to supply, so `createProduct` (the same endpoint Receive Stock's "Create article" flow already calls) would 500 on every single article creation, discovered empirically by running the exact request the existing frontend already sends and watching it crash with a raw Prisma error. Because "patch it to not crash" and "leave it broken until a follow-up task" are genuinely different, consequential outcomes — one keeps a currently-working, 52-check-tested feature alive, the other breaks it in a way only a future task fixes — this was raised as an explicit question rather than decided unilaterally, unlike the backfill-to-"Others" call for the two pre-existing rows (a one-time historical default, lower stakes than a decision that governs every future create).

The chosen shape matters: `categoryId` stays *optional* in the request, honored as-is when a real one is sent, defaulting to "Others" only when absent — not forced to "Others" unconditionally, and not validated-then-rejected when missing. This keeps today's untouched frontend working exactly as before while leaving the door open for a real category picker to start sending genuine values later with zero further backend change. The default is looked up by name (`findFirst({ where: { name: 'Others' } })`) each time, not a hardcoded id, because nothing about a growing, editable list (`Category.isActive`, rename support) guarantees a fixed id stays valid indefinitely — the same reasoning already applied to Color/Location/Factory lookups elsewhere in this codebase.

### Doc drift, again: 03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md's Round 12 revision was missing two already-shipped Round 11 fixes
For the second consecutive schema-revision task, the doc's incoming version turned out to be missing state that was live, migrated, and tested in the real database — this time `ProductSize.@@unique([productId, sizeLabel])` and `User.partyStockReturns` were both absent again (the exact same two things the Round 11 task had to restore after the same thing happened then), and the `@@check` comment had reverted to its pre-verification wording. Rather than silently re-fixing this a second time with no comment (which would make it look like nothing had happened, when the same drift recurring twice is itself worth noticing), it's now flagged explicitly in both the response to the user and inline in schema.prisma's own comments, and the live constraints were kept rather than deferring to the doc. The general lesson: when a source-of-truth doc gets revised by an external process between tasks, diffing it against the CURRENTLY LIVE schema (not just reading the new doc in isolation) is what catches silent regressions in the doc itself — treating the doc as unconditionally authoritative would have reintroduced a real bug (the duplicate-size pieces-per-set inflation) a second time.

### Why the Kids-toggle default re-applies on every OFF→ON transition, instead of only the first time
The instruction was "smart-default the picker to Kids without locking it — still changeable," which leaves open exactly *when* the default fires: only the very first time the toggle is switched on, or every time. Chose every time — the check is `if (!isKids)` (i.e. "about to turn on") at the moment of the click, not a one-shot flag that gets consumed and never fires again. The reasoning: each OFF→ON flip is the person actively saying "this is a kids garment" right now, which is exactly the moment "Kids" is the best guess again, regardless of whether they'd previously wandered off to a different category earlier in the same form. The OFF direction deliberately does the opposite — it never touches the category, even though it could "revert" to nothing. Clearing it on OFF would mean a manual pick (e.g. "Hoodie", made *because* the person decided that was more accurate than "Kids") gets silently destroyed by an unrelated toggle flip, which is a real value the app has no business discarding. This asymmetry — re-apply going one direction, never touch going the other — is what makes the field a genuine *default* rather than a rule that fights the user: it offers a guess exactly when a guess is newly relevant, and never overwrites an actual decision.

### Why the two lines that turned out to be genuine bugs in the new test were worth chasing down to the exact character, not worked around
The first hang (two clicks with nothing awaited between them) and the second (a `"pieces"` vs `"piece"` string mismatch) could both have been "fixed" by inserting a longer blind sleep or a looser substring check that happened to match both plurals. Neither shortcut was taken, for the same reason: this project's tests exist to catch real regressions, and a test whose passing condition has been loosened until it stops noticing the exact thing it was checking is worse than no test — it reports green while proving nothing about the singular-vs-plural rendering, or about whether a genuinely stale click ever gets sent. The fix in both cases was to make the assertion match reality exactly (the real rendered string, the real point where a render is guaranteed to have committed), not to make reality's mismatch stop mattering to the assertion.

### Why deactivate/reactivate is gated per-entity by CREATION rules, not by each entity's own PATCH rules or a single blanket policy
Factory and Product both already have an existing PATCH `/:id` endpoint for editing fields (GST/contact, category/price), and both of those are OWNER-only. It would have been easy to make deactivate/reactivate OWNER-only too, by analogy to "editing a record" in general. That would have been wrong: the instruction was specifically to match each entity's *creation* gating, and Factory/Product/Color all let any authenticated role create one — staff add factories and articles constantly as part of normal Receive Stock work. Deactivation is a genuinely different kind of action from "edit this GST number," much closer in spirit to "un-create it" — the same class of action as the creation it reverses, not the same class as field-level editing. Location breaks the pattern the other direction: its own creation is already OWNER-only (unlike the other three), so its deactivate/reactivate stayed OWNER-only too, for the same "match creation" reasoning, not because deactivation is inherently more sensitive for Location specifically.

Party had no precedent to match at all — no create/list endpoint exists yet, so there was nothing to mirror. Rather than default to "any role" just because most of the other four landed there, this was raised as an explicit question, since it's a real security-relevant judgment call with no established answer in the codebase, precisely the kind of ambiguity CLAUDE.md says to ask about rather than assume. Landed on OWNER-only, reasoning that a Party is a customer/shop relationship record — closer to Location (a bounded, deliberately-managed list) than to Color (a low-stakes lookup that grows freely with daily use).

### Why the lockout guards from deactivateUser don't appear anywhere in these five
`deactivateUser` has two guards: never let someone deactivate themselves, and never let the last active OWNER be deactivated. Both exist because of one specific structural fact — the API's own OWNER-only routes require *at least one active OWNER to exist*, or nothing gated behind `requireRole('OWNER')` could ever be reached again, including the reactivate endpoint that would undo the mistake. That's a genuine irreversible-lockout risk unique to User/OWNER. None of Factory/Product/Color/Location/Party have anything analogous — deactivating every single Factory in the system doesn't prevent anyone from creating a new one or reactivating an old one, because `POST /api/factories` isn't gated behind "a Factory must already be active." The guards are protecting against a specific self-referential trap (the permission needed to reverse the action requires the very state the action is destroying), not deactivation in general — so porting them to entities without that trap would have been solving a problem that doesn't exist there, not extra safety.

### Why the Transfer screen selects a Stock ROW, instead of copying Receive Stock's article-then-colour cascade
Receive Stock's selection is two dependent dropdowns: look up an article (creating it if absent), then pick a colour (creating it if absent), scoped to a Factory. Copying that shape onto Transfer would have been the path of least resistance — the components already exist and the screens superficially rhyme ("pick an article and a colour and a quantity"). It would also have been wrong, because the two screens answer different questions.

Whether an article+colour can be *received* depends on almost nothing — that's the point, Receive Stock brings things into existence. Whether it can be *transferred* depends on four facts simultaneously: the Product exists, the Colour exists, a Bundle links them, AND the source Location currently holds `qtySets > 0` of it. A cascade evaluates those one at a time, so it can lead someone through two valid-looking steps into a combination with nothing to move — article 6056 is real, Navy is real, Delhi holds zero Navy. The dead end is only discoverable at the end, after the user has already committed to a path.

Collapsing it to a single flat list of concrete in-stock rows (article + colour + available quantity, filtered to the chosen source) makes that unrepresentable: every visible option is transferable by construction, because the list is built from Stock rows rather than from Products. It's also one decision instead of two, and it carries the available quantity along for free — which is what caps the stepper, so the `INSUFFICIENT_STOCK` error becomes a genuine race-condition backstop rather than something the UI regularly walks people into. This is the same "make the bad state unrepresentable rather than merely unlikely" reasoning behind the three-state colour-loading fix, applied to selection instead of loading.

The deliberate absence of any "+ Create new" affordance is load-bearing, not an omission: a Transfer that could create an article would be a Transfer that moves stock which never existed. That's commented in the file itself precisely because the *lack* of something is invisible to a future reader, who might otherwise "fix" the inconsistency by pattern-matching Receive Stock's `CreatableSelect`.

### Why POST /api/transactions still rejects TRANSFER_OUT/TRANSFER_IN even now that transfers are fully built
The two enum values had been rejected by that endpoint on the grounds that their stock-movement semantics weren't designed yet — a temporary state, same as `DEFECT_RETURN`/`PARTY_RETURN` still are. Now that transfers work, the obvious tidy-up would be to add them to `VALID_TYPES`. That would quietly destroy the invariant the whole feature rests on: a transfer is only ever coherent as a *pair* (out at the source, in at the destination), created together atomically. `POST /api/transactions` creates exactly one Transaction per call, so accepting a lone `TRANSFER_OUT` there would let stock leave Delhi and arrive nowhere — inventory silently vanishing, with a perfectly valid-looking audit row explaining where it went. So the rejection stays, permanently, and the comment now says *why it's permanent* rather than "not built yet" — otherwise the next person to read it would reasonably assume it's stale and remove it. The general shape: when an operation's correctness depends on two writes happening together, the API needs exactly one door for it, and every other door needs to stay locked on purpose.

### Why rule 95 was neither confirmed nor reverted — it doesn't exist, and none was written to fill the gap
A follow-up handoff document from a separate review conversation asserted, as verified fact, that rule 95 in `05_BUSINESS_RULES.md` states custom-composition orders trigger at packing/shipping rather than at order time, and that this is "structurally dependent on Phase 2's Order/Pack Order screens." Asked to re-examine that rule and either justify the dependency against the real schema or revert it to an open question. Checked the actual document first: it ends at rule 94. There is no rule 95 — not a different version, not a stale one, nothing. A full-text search for "trigger," "packing," "shipping," and "order time" turns up only unrelated existing rules (22, 27, 55, 56, 58, 63, 64) about the already-designed Order/Pack Order screens in general.

This means the premise of "confirm the reasoning or revert it" doesn't apply — both options assume a stated conclusion exists to be judged. The correct response to a citation of nonexistent content is to say so, not to manufacture a rule 95 in either form (a confirmed dependency or an "open question" placeholder) just to give the task's request somewhere to land. Writing *either* version would mean inventing new project decisions under the cover of "fixing a doc," which is a materially different (and much riskier) act than restoring content that demonstrably exists elsewhere, like the schema-drift fix below. `05_BUSINESS_RULES.md` is unmodified by this task, and this entry exists so a future reader who goes looking for "the rule 95 decision" finds an explanation instead of silence.

### Why rule 95 was later added anyway (2026-08-12), with honest provenance instead of the entry above being reversed
A follow-up investigation traced the earlier "rule 95" claim to a real file: six stray docs sitting at `/Users/aadityaarora/Desktop/` (one directory above the actual project root), all written within an 8-second window on 2026-08-11 — outside the git repo entirely, never merged. Five of the six turned out to be genuine, independently-verifiable corrections to already-stale canonical content (matched against already-existing rules 50/84, or against the live, running code) and were merged separately. Rule 95 was different: nothing outside that single stray batch corroborates it — no commit, no `LEARNING_LOG.md` entry, no schema field that requires it, no reference from any other already-decided rule. Repetition of the rule-95 text across multiple stray files isn't independent evidence, since all six files share the same 8-second creation window — that's one generation event, not several agreeing sources.

Asked directly whether to add it anyway, on the stated basis that it was "a confirmed real decision made in planning." That framing couldn't be verified and wasn't added to the log as fact — see the entry above, which still accurately describes what could and couldn't be established at the time. What changed here is different: it's the owner deciding, in this conversation, that this is now the rule going forward — an act within the owner's own authority to make, regardless of where the stray draft came from. Rule 95 is added dated 2026-08-12, with an inline provenance note, precisely so the two things stay distinguishable: "we can independently verify this was already true" (rules 50, 84, and everything merged alongside this) versus "the owner is deciding this right now" (rule 95). Collapsing that distinction — writing rule 95 in as if its origin had been confirmed rather than simply decided — would repeat the exact mistake the entry above was written to avoid.

### Why the Party.name uniqueness race was accepted rather than closed with a migration
`Party.name` has no DB-level unique constraint, unlike `Color.name`/`Factory.name` — `POST /api/parties`'s case-insensitive uniqueness check is application-layer only (a pre-check plus a `P2002` catch that currently can't fire, since there's no index to violate). Two near-simultaneous POSTs for the same name could both pass the pre-check and both succeed, creating duplicate Party rows. Deliberately not fixed: Party creation is OWNER-only, an infrequent, deliberate action, not a public-facing form under real concurrent load, and the failure mode is a harmless duplicate row (rename/merge later, if it ever actually happens) — not data corruption or a security issue. Same reasoning already accepted for the owner-deactivation TOCTOU race (rule 78): closing it needs machinery (a DB migration here; row-level locking there) this codebase doesn't otherwise use, for a risk that's real but scale-inappropriate to engineer around.

### Why Category was missing from rule 85's archiving list even after its endpoints existed
The Desktop-root stray-file review (the six-file batch that later produced the rule 95 saga) had already drafted this correctly — its version of rule 85 included Category, with a note that the endpoint itself was missing. That got caught and merged as a bucket-(a) correction to `06_ROADMAP.md`'s Category gap, but the rule 85 wording change itself wasn't part of that merge — the later reconciliation task was scoped narrowly around rule 95 specifically (verify it, decide its provenance, split the commits), and rule 85's own text was never revisited once the immediate Category archive endpoints were actually built. A real, already-identified gap slipped through anyway, despite the surrounding saga catching several other things correctly — a reminder that "this was flagged once, somewhere" isn't the same as "this got applied," even a few tasks later. Closed now: Category added to rule 85's list.

### Why POST /api/factory-payments went from role-only to PIN-gated mid-task, reversing an earlier explicit decision
`POST /api/factory-payments` originally shipped OWNER-role-only, no PIN — the controller's own comment said so explicitly, and `04_API_SPEC.md` documented it that way, with real reasoning: recording a payment isn't itself a cost/selling-price read or write, so it didn't get the same gate as `PATCH /api/products/:id`. That was a deliberate, already-reviewed call, not an oversight.

Building the Factory Payables screen's Record Payment flow surfaced a conflict: the screen spec called for an inline PIN prompt on this exact action, but the backend it would submit to never checked the PIN at all — a `pin` field the frontend collected and sent would have been silently ignored. Shipping that would mean an owner sees what looks like a real security gate on a live financial action, when in fact the request succeeds regardless of what's typed, or with no PIN at all. Flagged this before writing any frontend code rather than either quietly building the decorative prompt or quietly dropping the PIN requirement to match the existing backend. Given the choice, added `requirePin` to the route (`backend/src/routes/factoryPayments.js`) — the same shared middleware and lockout behavior already used for price edits, no schema change needed since it reads the existing `priceEditPinHash` fields. The original "no PIN" reasoning wasn't wrong on its own terms; it just stopped being the right call once a real screen made "an owner can log a payment against role alone" into something a screen was about to visibly promise not to allow.

### Why the color-picker escape hatch is now permanently available, not a regression fix — the original design only ever solved the zero-color bootstrap case
Receive Stock's color picker had two sources it could draw from: `resolvedColors` (an article's own real, bundled colors) and `globalColors` (every color in the system), the latter reachable only while `colorBootstrapMode` was true. That flag existed to solve one specific problem — an article with zero colors of its own has nothing in `resolvedColors` to pick from, so it needs to fall back to the full list for its very first color. It was never designed to solve the general case: once an article had even one real color, `colorBootstrapMode` turned off and stayed off, including on every future re-search of that same article. There was no code path back to the global list after that point — not a missing button, a genuinely unreachable state. Confirmed with a rendered-component test: creating an article, staging two colors, committing them, then re-searching that same article showed a color picker with no "+ Create new color…" option at all, for either a freshly-created or a previously-existing article.

The fix removes the mode distinction entirely rather than patching around it: `globalColors` is now fetched on the same condition `resolvedColors` already uses (any matched/justCreated article, unconditionally), the picker's option list is the deduplicated union of both, and `canCreate` is no longer gated on anything — it's just always true. `colorBootstrapMode` itself is gone; nothing needed it once the picker stopped having two different sourcing behaviors to choose between. Verified a color that exists globally but was never bundled to a given article (a third color, `Green`, alongside two the article already had) is directly pickable, not just creatable-by-retyping-its-name — confirming this is a genuine fallback to the full list, per the PRD's "owner/staff can add new colors as needed," not a narrower "create a new one if you must" patch.

Framed as a design gap closure, not a regression, because the original zero-color bootstrap behavior was never wrong on its own terms — it correctly solved the one case it was built for. It just never covered the more general case the PRD actually describes, and nothing about how it was built made that omission visible until someone tried to add a second color to an already-received article.

### Why the CRITICAL "staging bug" report didn't get a code fix — it didn't reproduce
Reported as: picking a new article's first color and setting a quantity was said to immediately commit the whole article to the receipt, without ever pressing "Add to receipt." Diagnosed the same way the `colorBootstrapMode` bug was — not by reading the code and guessing, but by actually rendering the real component (a temporary vitest + happy-dom + `@testing-library/react` harness, removed after this task) and driving the exact reported sequence, then checking real DOM state before any explicit commit action. Result: nothing commits. `receiptItems` is written to in exactly one place in the whole file — `handleAddToReceipt`, wired only to that button's `onClick` — and tracing every other handler confirmed none of them reach it. Went further and staged two colors end-to-end (switching colors without ever clicking "Add to receipt," confirming the first stayed staged and nothing committed, then committing once and confirming both landed correctly grouped), which is the exact scenario the report says doesn't work.

Rather than either declaring the report wrong and doing nothing, or inventing a fix for a defect with no evidence behind it, connected it to the REAL GAP bug above: "Add to receipt" is a genuine one-click commit, correct for a single color but not visually distinguished from a "save this color, let me add another" action — someone could reasonably click it after one color expecting to still be able to add more, then hit the REAL GAP when trying to re-search that article to do so. That combination is a completely reasonable way to describe "I picked one color and the article was just done" without there being a commit-logic defect anywhere. Fixed the actual gap (above) and added an explicit `+ Add another color` action (`handleStageAnother`) so staging a second color is a visible, first-class step rather than something only discoverable by already knowing that switching the color dropdown silently finalizes whatever was active — the same standing rule already applied elsewhere in this project (a passing test proving an interaction works is not the same as that interaction being discoverable). No change was made to the commit logic itself, because nothing about it was found to be wrong.

### Why Transfer's staged batching was built as a new feature, not "finished" as a spec-restoration — §5.9 didn't exist
Asked to add staged multi-item batching to Transfer on the premise that `07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md`'s §5.9 already documented it and a prior task must have left it unbuilt. Checked before building anything, the same way the earlier "approved Claude Design reference mockup" claim was checked rather than trusted: the brief has no §5.9 — it ends at §5.8 (Factory Payables), and Transfer isn't in the original §5 list at all. `Transfer.jsx`'s own header comment already explained why: "transfers were an accepted Phase 1 limitation (rule 46) when that brief was written, so this screen follows the app's established conventions rather than a spec'd layout." `git log` shows exactly one commit touching the file — the large `f7f0085` squash snapshot that introduced it already in single-row-per-submission form — so there was no earlier batching attempt to have partially completed or reverted. `06_ROADMAP.md` marks Transfer "✅ COMPLETE" in that same single-row shape, and this file's own "Why the Transfer screen selects a Stock ROW" entry documents the single-decision, no-dead-end reasoning as deliberate, not incomplete.

None of that made the feature request wrong on its own merits — staged batching is a reasonable ask, mirroring Receive Stock/New Order's already-established "Staged batching, precisely" pattern (§7). It meant treating it honestly as a new design decision being made now (2026-08-13), the same provenance discipline already used for rule 95: `07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md` got a genuinely new §5.9 entry, dated in its own text as added now, rather than the batching behavior being silently folded into the existing single-row description as if it had always been the spec.

### Why Transfer's staged batching is one flat list, not Receive Stock's two-tier stagedColors → receiptItems structure
Receive Stock needs two tiers because a single commit unit (one article) can legitimately contain several colors, so colors have to be staged *within* an article before the article itself gets added to the receipt. A Transfer line has no such grouping need — one Stock row + one quantity + one destination is already a complete, self-sufficient thing to move, and requirement 2 explicitly asked for staged lines to grow across different articles, not to be committed one article-group at a time. Copying Receive Stock's two-tier shape here would have added a grouping step with nothing real for it to group, so `stagedLines` is a single flat array instead, and `finalizeActiveLine`/`handleStageAnother` mirror Receive Stock's `finalizeActiveColor`/`handleStageAnother` mechanism (fold the active selection into the staged array, make "stage this and let me pick another" an explicit, visible button rather than a side effect of just clicking a different row) without mirroring the two-tier structure the mechanism happened to sit inside there.

### Why a staged line's cap accounts for what's already staged against the same row, not just the last-fetched qtySets
Requirement 6 asked for each staged line's quantity to be capped by "that row's real available qtySets." Read literally against only the raw, last-fetched `row.qtySets`, that cap would be fictional the moment the same row gets staged more than once in one batch: a 5-available row staged for 5 sets still *shows* 5 available (nothing has actually been submitted yet), so a second staging pass against the same row could push the batch to a locally-staged total of 10 against 5 truly on hand. `remainingForRow()` subtracts what's already staged for that `bundleId` from the raw figure, so the stepper's max — and the number shown in the picker itself — reflects what's really still stageable, not a number that's already gone stale the moment the first line for that row exists. This is a UI-level convenience only; the backend's guarded UPDATE inside `POST /api/transfers` remains the actual enforcement, exactly as the pre-existing per-row cap comment already said before batching existed.

### Why each staged Transfer line snapshots its own destination instead of reading `toLocationId` live at submit time
Only a source-Location change resets the staged list (requirement 5); nothing resets it when the destination dropdown changes, so it's possible — not the common case, but possible — to stage a line while "To" is Gurgaon, then change "To" to Mumbai before staging a second line. Reading `toLocationId` live at submit time would silently redirect the first line to Mumbai too, changing where already-staged stock is headed without the person ever having decided that. Each staged line snapshots `toLocationId`/`toLocationName` the moment it's finalized instead — the same snapshot-not-live-read principle Receive Stock's rule 52 already established for Factory/Location/price — and the staged list shows each line's own destination explicitly (`→ {toLocationName}`) so a genuinely mixed-destination batch is visible rather than hidden behind an assumed single "to."

### Why the in-flight-submission race was fixed with both a UI disable AND a merge-not-replace safeguard, not just one
Found while tracing an unrelated edge case (the fully-staged-row re-select question): nothing stopped a user from staging a new line while a previous batch was still submitting, because `canStage` never checked `submitting` and the stock-row buttons had no `disabled` prop at all. The real damage wasn't that the new line could be double-submitted — it was silent data loss: `handleConfirmedSubmit` snapshots `stagedLines` into a local `lines` var at the very start, then at the end does `setStagedLines(failedLines)`, which is a flat *replace*. Anything staged into `stagedLines` after the snapshot was taken but before that final `setStagedLines` call would simply never appear in `failedLines` (it was never part of `lines`, so it was never submitted, never failed, never retried) and would vanish the instant the replace landed — no error, no trace, nothing in the outcome banner.

Fixed both layers deliberately, not just the more visible one. Disabling the stock-row buttons and "+ Add to transfer" (`canStage` now includes `!submitting`) closes the gap for every real user going through the actual UI — that's the primary fix, and it's what the empirical test exercises with a genuinely held-open `POST` promise (not a fixed timeout) so there's a real window to attempt the blocked interaction during. The merge fix — computing `submittedIds` from the original `lines` snapshot, filtering current `stagedLines` down to whatever *isn't* in that set, and only then prepending `failedLines` — is deliberately kept as a second, independent layer rather than treated as redundant once the UI is disabled: a `disabled` prop is a rendering-layer guarantee, not a data-layer one, and the two have different failure modes (a future edit could easily drop `!submitting` from `canStage` without touching `handleConfirmedSubmit` at all, silently reopening the exact same hole). The merge logic was verified as a pure computation against both outcomes (submitted line succeeds vs. fails) rather than only through the UI test, specifically because the scenario it protects against — the UI block being bypassed — is by definition not reachable through the UI test that proves the first layer works.

### Why grouping Transfer's stock list by Factory → Article doesn't reverse the earlier "flat list, no cascade" decision — it's a real reconsideration of presentation, not of validity
The entry earlier in this file ("Why the Transfer screen selects a Stock ROW, instead of copying Receive Stock's article-then-colour cascade") argued that a flat list of concrete Stock rows makes every visible option transferable *by construction*, because the list is built from Stock rows rather than from Products — a cascading Factory→Article→Colour picker built from Products/Colours independently could walk someone into a combination with nothing to move (article exists, colour exists, this Location holds none of it). That argument is about where the DATA comes from — Stock rows vs. independently-matched Product/Colour records — not about how many rows are shown on screen at once or how they're visually grouped. A real screenshot surfaced the actual cost of the flat form once the catalog grew: ~18 ungrouped rows at a single Location, unreadable at a glance, the exact kind of problem Live Stock's own Factory→Article accordion (rule 57) already exists to solve for a structurally identical browsing problem.

The fix grouping rows into a Factory dropdown + collapsible Article accordion changes nothing about which rows are valid or how validity is determined — every row still comes from the same already-fetched, already-filtered (`qtySets > 0`, remaining-after-staged > 0) Stock-row list the flat version used, just grouped for display afterward. The one place this could have quietly reintroduced a dead end is the Factory dropdown's own options: if they'd come from a separate `listFactories()` call (matching every Factory in the system), picking a Factory with zero stock at this Location would produce an empty accordion — not the same *kind* of dead end the original entry warns about (no invalid Product+Colour combination is being constructed), but the same *symptom* (a selectable option that leads nowhere). Avoided by deriving `factoryOptions` from the Factories actually present in the already-loaded row set instead of a separate fetch — the same "every visible choice is real" guarantee the flat list always made for individual rows, just extended one level up to the new grouping control. `productId`/`factoryId` already rode along on every Stock row from the original design (needed because article numbers are only unique per Factory, never globally — 05_BUSINESS_RULES.md rule 1), so no new join was needed to know which Factory each row belongs to; `factoryName` was the only field GET /api/stock didn't already carry, added as a same-query field on the existing Product join rather than a second endpoint or a client-side join against a separately-fetched Product/Factory list (the way LiveStock.jsx does its own factory grouping) — this endpoint already had the relation in hand.

### Why back-to-Home navigation was built as a shared component, not a snippet copied onto all 8 screens — and why it was a genuine original gap, not a regression
A prior investigation task confirmed this directly before any code was written: no shared header/layout component ever existed in this codebase (`frontend/src/components/` had no `Header.jsx`/`Layout.jsx`/`Nav.jsx`), all 8 screens independently hand-built the identical icon-mark/eyebrow/title markup, `App.jsx` wraps each route with no persistent chrome around it, and `git log --oneline --all` across every commit in the repo showed nothing ever touching navigation specifically — the pattern was present, unchanged, since the initial scaffold commit. `07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md` §3.2 had described the screen-header *pattern* in prose from its first draft ("small rounded-square icon mark... present on every screen"), which is almost certainly why later task prompts referred to "§3.2" as if a component already backed it — but the section only ever documented what the pattern should look like, never that one real component implemented it. Worth recording plainly because it would have been easy to mistake "no back button anywhere" for a regression (something that broke) when it was really a gap that had simply never been closed.

Chose extraction over patching all 8 headers individually for the same reason `productSelect`'s hardcoded-`'OWNER'` bug is logged above: once the same logic needs to hold across several places, nothing about copy-pasted or independently-maintained code enforces that a change to the pattern — or an addition like this back arrow — actually lands everywhere it needs to. That earlier bug was one call site whose assumption silently went stale when a condition elsewhere changed; the risk here was the same shape at larger scale — 8 independent copies, and a 9th screen someday, each free to drift from the other 7 the moment anyone edited just one. `ScreenHeader.jsx` makes that structurally impossible rather than merely currently-consistent: there is exactly one place the icon-mark/eyebrow/title/back-arrow markup exists, so every screen necessarily renders whatever that one component renders.

Extraction surfaced two real, non-cosmetic differences among the "identical" 8 headers, not just icon/tone/eyebrow/title as assumed going in. First, Home's title uses a dedicated `greeting` class (21px, per the brief) instead of the standard `screen-title` (18px) — handled with a `titleClassName` prop rather than hardcoding one class, so the exception stays visible and intentional instead of silently lost. Second, and more consequential: Login is unauthenticated and isn't wrapped in `ProtectedRoute`, so a back-to-Home link there would resolve to `/`, hit `ProtectedRoute`'s auth check, and redirect straight back to `/login` — a "back to Home" control that would functionally just reload the same screen. Flagged this rather than assuming the task's literal wording ("every screen except Home") was exhaustive, since the task itself asked to confirm every real difference before assuming uniformity; the owner confirmed excluding Login too. `ScreenHeader`'s `showBackLink` prop defaults to `true` and is set to `false` on exactly the two screens where "go to Home" isn't a coherent action — Home (nothing to go back to) and Login (not somewhere Home is reachable from yet) — rather than being a Home-specific special case baked into the component itself.

### Why `FactoryDebit` exists — a real factory, in real use, showing an impossible negative Amount payable
Rule 81 defined `totalOwed` as purely derived from this system's own STOCK_IN transaction history. That's correct for any factory whose stock has always been received through the app — but a factory onboarded with real, pre-existing debt, whose receiving history predates the app entirely, has `totalOwed = 0` by that rule no matter how much is actually owed. Any `FactoryPayment` recorded against that factory (a real payment, for a real debt) then pushed `amountPayable` negative — not a display bug, a genuinely wrong number, since "amount payable" went negative for a factory that was, in fact, still owed money. This is a live-data problem, not a hypothetical: it's what using the already-built Factory Payables screen against a real factory actually surfaced, which is why rule 96 and this feature exist now rather than at the original Factory Payables build.

`FactoryDebit` is deliberately shaped identically to `FactoryPayment` — same fields, same Owner+PIN gate, same "one form, no separate confirm modal" pattern on the frontend — because it's the same kind of event in the opposite direction: a manual, logged adjustment to what's owed, not a new category of financial record needing its own rules. Folding it into `totalOwed` itself (`transactionOwed + SUM(FactoryDebit.amount)`), rather than introducing a third top-level figure alongside `totalOwed`/`totalPaid`, keeps `amountPayable = totalOwed − totalPaid` true as a formula without every caller needing to know a debit exists — `getFactoryPayable`'s response shape barely changes (one new `debits` array, `totalOwed`'s number is just now bigger when debits exist), and the frontend's `amountPayable` display needed no changes at all.

### Why the two "record" actions share one form and one `activeForm` enum, not two independent booleans
The original screen had a single `formOpen` boolean for the one action that existed. Adding a second action as a second boolean (`paymentFormOpen`/`debitFormOpen`) would let both be `true` at once with nothing stopping it — a state the UI has no way to render correctly, since there's one shared amount/date/note/PIN field block below, not two. `activeForm` (`null | 'payment' | 'debit'`) makes "both open" unrepresentable instead of merely unlikely, the same reasoning already applied to Receive Stock's three-state colour loading and Transfer's `locationGroups`. `handleSubmitForm` branches on it once (which endpoint to call, which success message to show) rather than existing as two near-duplicate handlers — the same "don't let identical logic drift across two places" reasoning just applied to `ScreenHeader`, immediately above, applied here to a payment-vs-debit submit handler instead of a header.

### Why Article Pricing needed zero backend changes — and why that was confirmed by reading the code, not assumed from the task description
Before writing anything, checked `productController.js`/`routes/products.js` directly rather than assuming a new endpoint or a new query param was needed. `GET /api/products` already accepted `?factoryId=` (`listProducts` has always built `where.factoryId` from `req.query.factoryId` — this predates this task entirely, from whichever earlier task built Receive Stock's article lookup). `productSelect(role)` already omits `costPrice` from the Postgres query itself for non-OWNER roles — the exact structural "select, don't strip" guarantee the earlier `costPrice`-leak incident (`productSelect('OWNER')` hardcoded once, then a live leak the moment the route's access loosened) exists to protect. `PATCH /api/products/:id` already requires OWNER **and** PIN the instant `costPrice`/`sellingPrice` appear in the body, via `requirePinForPriceEdits`. Every piece this screen needed already existed, already correctly gated, already tested by whatever built Receive Stock and that PATCH route originally. The only new backend artifact from this whole task is the OWNER-only route guard on the frontend (`ProtectedRoute requireRole="OWNER"`), which is defense-in-depth on top of the real, structural, server-side guarantee — never the guarantee itself. Writing a second, screen-specific product-select or a new PATCH endpoint "just for this screen" would have reintroduced exactly the two-copies-of-the-same-logic risk the original leak came from, for zero actual benefit — the existing endpoints already do precisely what §5.10 asks for.

### Why Article Pricing's pending-price badge says "Pending," not the brief's literal "New · pending price" copy
`07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md` §4/§3.1's token table both name the exact status text "New · pending price" — but that copy was written for a context where the badge appears once, next to an article name, telling you *that the article* is pending. Article Pricing's table has three separate cells (Cost/Selling/Margin) that all need the same treatment per requirement 5.10's own wording ("renders its Cost/Selling/Margin cells with the ... badge treatment instead of blank cells") — repeating "New · pending price" three times in one row would be noise the column headers already make redundant (a reader looking at the "Cost Price" column doesn't need the badge to also tell them it's about price). Used the shorter "Pending" instead, keeping the exact same `badge-warning` class/color role §3.1 assigns to "new-article-pending-price status" — the visual language is identical, only the copy is shortened for a context where it's said three times in one row instead of once. Worth noting this is also the *first* real implementation of this badge anywhere in the app — `grep`ing the whole frontend for "pending" before starting turned up zero matches; the brief has described it since its first draft (including one direct line in §5.3: a pending article "has no price until the owner sets one (owner-only, separate screen, not shown here)"), but no screen ever actually built it until this one, which is that exact "separate screen" the brief was pointing at.

### Why the `updatedAt` migration backfills from each row's own `createdAt`, not the migration's execution time
Adding `updatedAt DateTime @updatedAt` to `FactoryPayment`/`FactoryDebit` failed outright against both the dev and test databases — 5 and 2 existing rows respectively, and Postgres can't add a `NOT NULL` column with no default to a non-empty table. The tempting one-line fix, `ADD COLUMN "updatedAt" TIMESTAMP(3) NOT NULL DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP`, would have backfilled every pre-existing row's `updatedAt` to the moment the migration ran — potentially minutes or hours after that row's real `createdAt`. That's exactly the signal the frontend's "edited" label looks for ("`updatedAt` meaningfully after `createdAt`"), so every untouched historical row would have shown as edited the instant this migration landed, before a single real edit had ever happened. Used `--create-only` to write the migration by hand instead: add the column nullable, `UPDATE ... SET "updatedAt" = "createdAt"` on both tables, then set it `NOT NULL`. An untouched historical row now has `updatedAt == createdAt`, identical to how it would look if `@updatedAt` had been on the table since the row was created — only a genuine future edit can make the two diverge.

### Why the "edited" label uses a 1-minute threshold instead of checking `updatedAt !== createdAt`
`createdAt` is `@default(now())`, evaluated database-side at insert time; `updatedAt` is `@updatedAt`, which Prisma's client sets by computing `new Date()` itself at query-build time, on the application server. These are two different clocks (Postgres server time vs. the Node process), read at two slightly different moments even within the same `create()` call — so a brand-new, never-touched row can legitimately show a few hundred milliseconds of drift between the two fields. A strict inequality check would false-positive "edited" on rows nobody ever edited. `isEdited()` instead checks for more than 60 seconds of drift — comfortably clears any realistic clock/latency skew, while still catching every real edit (which, in actual usage, never happens within the same minute a row was first created).

### Why editing/deleting an existing entry got entirely separate state from the creation forms, duplicating some field names
This task was explicit: don't touch the creation flow. The cleanest single-state design — generalizing `activeForm` from `null | 'payment' | 'debit'` into something like `null | 'create-payment' | 'create-debit' | 'edit-payment' | 'edit-debit'` and reusing the same `amount`/`date`/`note`/`pin` fields for both creating and editing — would have meant renaming `activeForm`'s values and touching every `activeForm === 'debit'` check throughout the existing, working creation code, even though its actual behavior wouldn't change. Chose the more literal reading instead: `handleSubmitForm`, `activeForm`, and every line of the creation form's JSX are untouched, byte-for-byte, from before this task. Edit/delete got their own parallel state (`entryAction` — `null | { kind, mode, entry }` — and its own `entryAmount`/`entryDate`/`entryNote`/`entryPin`/etc.), which does duplicate four field names that already exist for creation. This is a deliberate exception to the "don't duplicate the same logic in two places" principle applied everywhere else in this session (`ScreenHeader`, the payment/debit create-handler merge, Article Pricing reusing `productSelect`) — here, the explicit instruction not to touch working, tested creation code outweighed the usual drift risk, since the two field sets serve genuinely different mutations (`POST` a new row vs. `PATCH`/`DELETE` an existing one) that only happen to share a visual shape.

### Why this is the first hard DELETE in the whole app, and why that's safe here specifically
Every other deletable-feeling entity in this schema — Factory, Product, User, Location, Party, Category — uses soft-deactivate (`isActive: false`) because other rows reference them by foreign key and must stay resolvable forever (a `Transaction` tracing back through a deactivated `factoryId` still needs that Factory row to exist). Checked the schema before writing `DELETE /api/factory-payments/:id`/`DELETE /api/factory-debits/:id`: nothing anywhere references `FactoryPayment` or `FactoryDebit` by foreign key — they're leaf nodes, referenced by nothing. A hard `prisma.factoryPayment.delete()` can't orphan anything, so the soft-delete machinery this schema uses everywhere else would be solving a problem that doesn't exist here. `04_API_SPEC.md` had no `DELETE` endpoint documented anywhere before this task — worth recording plainly since the next hard-delete candidate in this schema won't have a precedent to blindly copy from; it needs the same check (does anything reference this by FK?) run fresh, not "well, Factory Payments does it."

### Why `GET /api/factories/:id/payable` needed one small change despite the task saying "no additional changes needed"
The task's claim was specifically about `totalOwed`/`totalPaid`/`amountPayable` correctness — verified true (see the "verified explicitly" entry below) without touching that computation at all. But the endpoint's `payments`/`debits` `select` never included `createdAt`/`updatedAt` — nothing before this task needed them. The new "edited" label requires both fields to be present on each entry so `isEdited()` has something to compare. Added them to both `select` clauses; the aggregate math itself is byte-for-byte what it was before this task.

### Verifying `GET /api/factories/:id/payable` reflects edits/deletes correctly, rather than assuming it
Read `getFactoryController.js`'s `getFactoryPayable` before writing PATCH/DELETE: it runs `prisma.factoryPayment.findMany`/`prisma.factoryDebit.findMany` fresh, from live rows, on every single call — no cache, no stored aggregate, no denormalized total anywhere in the schema. Confirmed with a real probe rather than trusting that reasoning alone: created a payment, fetched `/payable` (`totalPaid` reflected it), edited the same payment's amount via `PATCH`, fetched `/payable` again (`totalPaid` updated to the new amount, `amountPayable` recalculated correctly), then deleted it via `DELETE` and fetched a third time (`totalPaid` and the `payments` array both back to their pre-creation state). All three reads hit the same endpoint with no special-casing — it "just worked" precisely because it was never doing anything clever enough to need updating.

### Why the 1-minute "edited" threshold was replaced with an explicit `wasEdited` flag, not tuned to a smaller number
The two entries above explain why a threshold existed at all — `createdAt`/`updatedAt` are two different clocks (Postgres server time vs. Prisma's client-side `new Date()`), so a naive `updatedAt !== createdAt` check false-positived on every freshly-created row. But the fix for that false-positive problem was never actually the right fix for the underlying question ("was this ever edited") — it was a workaround for a signal (timestamp comparison) that was never designed to answer that question precisely, only approximately. The threshold's own honestly-documented tradeoff ("an edit within the same minute as creation won't show the label") was a real, reachable gap, not a hypothetical edge case — testing/correcting an entry immediately after creating it is an entirely normal thing to do, and the label would have silently failed to appear for exactly that case. Tuning the threshold smaller doesn't fix this category of problem, it only shrinks the window where it's wrong; the clock-drift false-positive risk and the same-minute false-negative risk pull in opposite directions no single threshold value can satisfy for both. `wasEdited` sidesteps the whole tradeoff: it's not inferred from anything, it's set directly, exactly once, by the one code path that actually knows an edit happened (`updateFactoryPayment`/`updateFactoryDebit`) — unambiguous regardless of how many milliseconds or minutes separate creation from correction. The two entries above stay in the log as an accurate record of what was tried first and why it seemed reasonable at the time, not deleted or rewritten now that something better replaced it.

### Why the new admin password-reset endpoint checks the requester's OWN PIN, not the target account's
`PATCH /api/users/:id/password` needed to answer "who is allowed to do this," and the natural-sounding wrong answer would have been to require the *target's* current password or PIN — that's backwards for an admin reset: the entire reason this endpoint exists is to help someone who doesn't know (or never knew) their own password. Instead it reuses `requirePin`/`verifyPin` exactly as every other PIN gate in this app does — looked up against `req.user.id`, the person making the request. This proves who's performing the reset, not that they know anything about the account being reset, which is the correct question for an admin action.

### Why resetting another OWNER's password required extending rule 74, not a fresh restriction invented from scratch
Before writing `resetUserPassword`, checked whether an existing precedent already covered this shape of problem rather than designing a new one. Rule 74 (`POST /api/users` restricting OWNER-creation to `isPrimaryOwner`) is the closest: both are "OWNER acting on OWNER-level access." Password reset is actually the more dangerous of the two — creating a new OWNER account grants a fresh privilege, but resetting an *existing* OWNER's password can seize an account that's already in use, including the primary owner's own. Applied the identical `isPrimaryOwner` check rather than inventing a separate rule, so the two restrictions can't drift apart later — a change to one without noticing the other would leave a real asymmetric hole. Verified with a real probe against the dev DB (not just reasoning about the code): a non-primary OWNER (`aadi123`) was confirmed blocked with `403 FORBIDDEN_ROLE` attempting to reset the primary owner's password, confirmed able to reset a STAFF account's password normally, and the primary owner's password was re-confirmed still logging in correctly after the blocked attempt — proving the rejection actually rejects, not just returns an error status while quietly still writing the row.

### Installing `@testing-library/jest-dom` violated the exact engines rule it exists to enforce — caught immediately, not after the fact
Setting up the throwaway test harness for Manage Users' Reset Password feature, ran `npm install vitest happy-dom @testing-library/react @testing-library/jest-dom` in one command out of habit, without checking each package's `engines` field first — CLAUDE.md's standing rule, restated after Prisma 7/Vite 7/jsdom already broke this exact way. The install itself printed `npm WARN EBADENGINE ... required: { node: '>=22' }, current: { node: 'v20.12.2' }` for jest-dom specifically. Caught it from the install output before writing a single assertion against it, uninstalled just that one package, and used the Chai-native `getByText`/`queryByText` presence checks already established in an earlier session (logged further up) instead of `toBeInTheDocument()`. Worth logging even though nothing broke: the rule says check *before* installing, and this was a check *during* — it worked out because the warning was impossible to miss, but a noisier install log could have buried it.

### `npm uninstall <one package> --no-save` silently pruned two OTHER unrelated `--no-save` packages from node_modules
After the jest-dom mistake above, ran `npm uninstall @testing-library/jest-dom --no-save` expecting it to remove exactly that one package. It also removed `vitest` and `happy-dom` — both installed moments earlier in the same `--no-save` batch — because none of the three were ever recorded in `package.json`, so npm's dependency resolution treated all of them as extraneous once uninstall triggered a tree re-resolution, not just the one named package. Only noticed because the very next `npx vitest run` command fell back to downloading a fresh, unpinned `vitest@4.1.10` from the registry instead of using the local install — which then failed outright on an unrelated native-binding error from its bundled `rolldown`, on a machine that had nothing wrong with the `vitest@2.x` that had just been removed. Fixed by reinstalling all three packages together again (`vitest@^2 happy-dom @testing-library/react`, explicitly pinning the vitest major version this time) and, critically, deferred any further `npm uninstall` calls on `--no-save` packages until the very end, removing them all in one batch via `rm -rf node_modules/<pkg>` instead of trusting `npm uninstall` to touch only what's named. `rm -rf` on the specific package directories is the correct fix here, not a workaround, because it does exactly what was intended (remove only the named directories) without invoking npm's tree-resolution logic at all — the actual mechanism that caused the unwanted side effect in the first place.

### Why "add a Change PIN entry point" required touching SetPin.jsx despite an explicit instruction not to
The task's premise was that `PATCH /api/users/me/pin` already fully supports changing an existing PIN (true — `currentPin` is required and verified whenever `priceEditPinHash` is already set) and that the gap was purely reachability — no link anywhere pointed at that capability once a PIN existed. Read as written, the instruction was to add a "Change PIN" link and leave `SetPin.jsx` alone. But `SetPin.jsx` itself, read before writing anything, turned out not to match that premise: it collects only `newPin`/`confirmPin` (no `currentPin` field exists anywhere in it), and it opens with `if (user.hasPinSet) return <Navigate to="/" replace />` — an explicit guard that redirects away the moment a PIN already exists, with its own comment stating "Changing an EXISTING PIN is a different, not-yet-built screen." A "Change PIN" link routed at this component, left untouched, would have been a real link to a real screen that immediately bounces back to Home every time — passing a glance at the code (a link exists, it points somewhere real) while failing the actual feature outright, and specifically failing the task's own stated test ("successfully changes it given the correct current PIN"), which is structurally impossible without a `currentPin` field. Flagged this before writing any code rather than either silently overriding the "do not touch" instruction or silently complying with it in a way that would ship a dead link — the same "verify a premise against the real code before building on it" discipline as the Transfer sort-order-fix task and the missing-files-in-a-commit-list catch, just caught at read time instead of during implementation. Given the go-ahead, `SetPin.jsx` became one screen with two modes chosen by `user.hasPinSet` at load time (no `currentPin` field/no guard when false; `currentPin` required, wrong-PIN `attemptsRemaining` surfaced, when true) rather than a second near-duplicate screen — Home's "Set PIN"/"Change PIN" rows are exact inverse conditions on the same flag routing to the same component, so whichever mode the component picks always matches how it was actually reached.

### Reconciling the Phase 2 Order/OrderLineItem/OrderAdjustment draft against rules 21–26, 59, 63–65, 95
The draft in `03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md` had been sitting untouched since Phase 2 was first sketched, before several of the rules it was supposed to satisfy existed in their current form — the same drift Party's own Phase 2 draft already showed once (stale `runningDueBalance`/`tier` fields predating rule 83, caught in an earlier task). Checked line by line rather than assumed correct: `OrderAdjustment`'s append-only shape (rule 23) and the four-stage `OrderStatus` enum (rule 59) were already right, so left alone rather than rewritten for its own sake. Three real gaps: no `qtySetsPacked` field on `OrderLineItem`, so rule 64's packing clamp and shortfall-messaging had nothing to compare against `qtySetsRequested`; no `reason` field on `OrderAdjustment`, despite rule 65 requiring a structured reason (not free text) for correcting entries — flagged as genuinely required but the actual category values left undecided, since rule 65's own example categories (Miscount, Wrong colour, Wrong customer) are Receive-Stock-specific and don't transfer to order-level changes like a Party reducing a quantity; and every relation field across all three models was missing (FK scalar present, no paired `@relation`), which every other model in this schema has. *(Update, same day: the category set was finalized as a proper `OrderAdjustmentReason` enum — Quantity reduced by Party / Order cancelled / Return after delivery / Miscalculation / Other — rather than left as an unconstrained `String`, once the actual values were confirmed.)*

Two decisions worth recording explicitly, since a future task could reasonably second-guess either: (1) deliberately did NOT add `packedById`/`billedById`/`shippedById` next to the existing timestamp fields — a status transition is just another value change, already fully expressible as an `OrderAdjustment` row (`field: "status"`, `changedById`), and splitting "who" across two audit mechanisms would be redundant, not more complete. (2) Kept `OrderLineItem.priceAtOrder`'s name exactly as-is, despite it being ambiguous (`Product` has both `costPrice` and `sellingPrice`) — checked first and found it's already cross-referenced by name in two other places (`Transaction.costPriceSnapshot`'s own comment, and rule 82 itself), so renaming it for clarity would have broken existing references for a cosmetic gain. Tightened its comment instead to state which price it snapshots.

### The Owner Dashboard's KPIs surfaced a units bug that had been shipped in two directions at once

The task specified "Stock value = SUM(Stock.qtySets × Product.costPrice)". Written out, that reads fine. In the schema it doesn't: `Product.costPrice` had no documented unit anywhere, and the codebase was already using it as **two different units in two shipped calculations**.

- `factoryController.getFactoryPayable` computes `qtySets × piecesPerSet × costPriceSnapshot` — treating price as **per piece**. Rule 81, `03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md`, and `04_API_SPEC.md`'s own wording ("the actual cost price per piece") all agree with it.
- `orderController.listOrders` computes `qtySetsRequested × priceAtOrder` with no piece factor — treating price as **per set**. Rule 86 and `PartyStockReturn`'s schema comment agree with *that*.

Both were live. Neither was flagged. The two fields are clearly the same unit as each other — the real data runs 550→700, 475→625, 750→1050, consistent 25–40% markups — so they can't be per-piece on one screen and per-set on another and both be right.

**Why I stopped instead of picking.** The two readings of Stock value on real data were ₹27,070 and ₹1,03,780 — a 3.8× spread on a headline number the owner would act on. There was no evidence inside the repo that could settle it, because the repo contained both answers. This is the difference between an ambiguity you resolve by reading more carefully and one you resolve by asking: more reading would only have produced a more confident wrong guess. The task had pre-authorised exactly this ("stop and ask rather than guessing — these are financial figures the owner will actually rely on"), and it was right to.

Answer: **per piece**. So every money figure derived from either field is `qtySets × piecesPerSet × price`, and rule 69 now says so once, for both fields, instead of each screen re-deriving it.

**What I deliberately did NOT do.** The answer means the order/return side (`listOrders`' `totalValue`, Bill/New Order totals, `priceAtReturn`'s documented total) is under-counting by the pieces-per-set factor. That is a real bug, in shipped code, on money. I recorded it in rule 69 and left it — it spans several screens plus rule 86's wording, and quietly rewriting order values inside a task scoped to "build a dashboard" would have buried a significant correction in an unrelated diff. Surfacing a bug and fixing a bug are different units of work, and the second one deserves its own review.

**On the two extractions.** `piecesPerSetFor` moved out of `factoryController` into `utils/piecesPerSet.js`. It had already been *exported* from that controller — which is the tell that it had outgrown its home; a controller exporting a domain rule for other controllers to import is a shared module wearing the wrong hat. `computeRevenue`/`periodToRange` went into `utils/revenue.js` rather than living inside the dashboard controller, because rule 98 requires the future Parties page's per-party sales summary to be the same arithmetic. The `partyId` parameter is wired and tested now, with no caller, precisely so the next task can't accidentally write a second version — the seam is cheaper to build while the reasoning is fresh than to retrofit around two divergent implementations later.

**A smaller call worth recording:** `billedAt` buckets revenue, not `shippedAt` or `createdAt`. Rule 98 mandates month-bucketing but never names the field, and a SHIPPED order carries both timestamps. Billing is when the money is claimed, and it's the stable choice — an order shipping in a later month must not silently move revenue out of the month it was billed in. Stated in the code and the API spec as an assumption rather than left for someone to reverse-engineer from a query.

### Pack Order's checklist-first redesign: replacing a two-variable workaround with a state shape that makes the bug unrepresentable

The task was, on its face, a UI change — tap-to-confirm instead of a pre-filled stepper. But the actual fix was upstream of any component: the old screen represented "packed in full" and "nobody has looked at this yet" using overlapping facts (`packingQty` defaulting to the ordered quantity, `touchedLines` as a bolted-on correction), and the file's own header carried a 20-line comment whose entire job was reconciling the two. That comment was the tell. A correct model shouldn't need a paragraph explaining why a value that LOOKS confirmed isn't.

The replacement is one map, `confirmed: { [lineId]: qtySetsPacked }`, where **key absence is the unconfirmed state** — not a derived reading of a number that happens to coincide with one. This is the same idiom CLAUDE.md already mandates for async status (`idle`/`loading`/`loaded`, never a bare boolean whose default aliases onto "found nothing"), just applied to a manual-confirmation status instead of a fetch status. Once state is shaped this way, "was this line ever touched" stops being a question that needs its own tracked fact — it falls straight out of `in`.

**The one place the old default still had to survive:** the backend's coverage check (`orderController.js`) requires exactly one entry per live line, so "mark packed anyway" still has to submit *something* for a line nobody confirmed. That value is still the ordered quantity — unchanged from before, and required to be, since the task's own constraint was zero backend change. What's different isn't the number, it's that staff are now told, by name, that it's about to happen, instead of it being folded silently into a default they never saw. Getting this specific number wrong (e.g. defaulting to 0 for "confirm anyway") would have been a real regression dressed as a UI polish — it would retroactively short-pack every line nobody touched and write a false `SHORT_PACKED` adjustment for each one.

**Where the new interaction reused, and deliberately did NOT reuse, existing app patterns.** The unconfirmed-lines warning reuses `ConfirmModal` and the exact "inform clearly, let the person decide" shape Bill Order's insufficient-stock warning already established — no new warning idiom invented. The per-article collapsed-header progress badge reuses `.accordion-subtitle`/`.accordion-low-badge`, the identical placement Bill Order uses for its own blocked-line count, found by checking that screen directly rather than inventing a new position. What's genuinely new is the two-sibling-controls row shape (tap-target + separate Adjust button) — nothing in this app needed "one tap does X, an adjacent smaller control does something narrower" before, so this had no existing pattern to borrow.

### Good Returns: three design calls worth recording, because each one had a plausible wrong answer

**1. Why the return `Transaction` carries `costPriceSnapshot: null`, and why that mattered more than it looked.** The task specified type `STOCK_IN` for the stock movement, which is correct — goods genuinely arrive. But `GET /api/factories/:id/payable` computes what we owe a factory by summing exactly those rows (`qtySets × piecesPerSet × costPriceSnapshot`). Had this endpoint snapshotted `costPrice` the way a real receipt does, every customer return would have quietly increased what the app claims we owe the factory the article came from — money that was never owed, appearing in a real financial figure, with nothing anywhere to flag it. Setting it null is right because null already means "unknown/not applicable" to that sum (it contributes 0, per that controller's own `!= null` check), which is exactly the correct contribution for a movement that creates no factory debt. `transferController` had already made this same call for its two legs, for the same reason. Tested directly rather than asserted: the payable for the fixture factory stays 0 across three returned lines.

**2. Why the shared `applyStockMovement` helper was extracted rather than the increment re-typed.** Receive Stock's increment logic lived in `transactionController.buildStockUpdate`, and `transferController` had already written a second inline copy of the same upsert-then-guarded-update. Adding a third copy for returns would have made it three. The non-obvious part being shared is the **upsert**, not the increment: a bundle/location pairing with no prior activity has no `Stock` row at all, so a plain `stock.update` throws. That's not a hypothetical — returned goods legitimately go back to a location that has never held that bundle, and the test for it (`return into a never-used location`) would have failed against any hand-rolled "just increment it" version. `transactionController` was refactored onto the shared helper too, so this genuinely reduced the number of implementations rather than adding a fourth; `transferController` was deliberately left alone, since its paired-leg shape and source-specific error message aren't the same operation.

**3. Why reason and note attach to the staged GROUP rather than each colour.** A Party sending back three colours of one article is nearly always sending them back for one reason, and asking the same question three times per return would make a common case tedious to serve a rare one. The rare case isn't lost: each "Add" captures its own reason, so staging the same article twice with two different reasons works exactly as expected. This is the same judgement New Order's own per-group staging already makes.

**A consequence worth stating plainly:** because the reason lives on the staged group, removing the last colour from a group removes the reason with it — which is correct, but means the staged-list rows each display their own reason rather than a single group-level heading. Without that, two groups of the same article with different reasons would render identically and be impossible to tell apart.

### Finalizing `PartyStockReturn`: why a *per-set* price replaced a stored total, and why cost-based was actually a bug in the spec
Rule 86 had said `PartyStockReturn` carries "a computed cost-based value," and the schema matched it with `valueSnapshot Decimal`. Both halves of that were wrong, in two independent ways, and it's worth separating them because they'd be easy to conflate as one change.

**Cost-based → selling-based.** A return is a *party-facing* record. The question it answers is "what is this worth against what this Party owes us," and what they owe was set by `sellingPrice` — nobody asks what we paid the factory when a customer sends goods back. So a cost-based figure was answering a factory-side question on a party-side record. It was also unsafe rather than merely imprecise: rule 10 keeps `costPrice` away from STAFF entirely, and a cost-derived *total* displayed on a staff-visible screen is trivially divisible by a visible `qtySets` straight back into the unit cost. Redacting a field from an API response is worthless if a number computed from it is printed next to its own divisor. This is the same reasoning that already made `OrderLineItem.priceAtOrder` snapshot `sellingPrice`, now applied to the other party-facing record.

**Stored total → per-set snapshot.** The subtler change. `valueSnapshot` stored a *product* (`qtySets × price`) in a column sitting directly beside one of its own inputs. Correct `qtySets` from 3 to 2 later and the stored total is instantly, silently wrong — nothing recomputes it, nothing flags it, and the row still looks perfectly well-formed. Storing `priceAtReturn` (per set) removes that failure mode entirely, because the per-set price is the only part that genuinely *can't* be recovered later — `Product.sellingPrice` moves over time, while `qtySets` is right there on the row. Total value becomes `qtySets × priceAtReturn`, computed fresh at every display site. The general shape: snapshot the input that decays, never the output you can derive from it.

**Why `reason` is `NOT NULL` while `OrderAdjustment.reason` is nullable.** These look like the same call made two different ways, so the difference is worth stating. `OrderAdjustment.reason`'s nullability was *earned* by a real case — a routine `Placed → Packed` row is forward progress, not a correction, and forcing it into one of five correction categories would have recorded a falsehood. No such case exists for returns: a `PartyStockReturn` row only comes into existence because goods physically came back through the door, and that always has a cause. So the constraint went into the database itself rather than being left to controller discipline. Nullability should track whether a genuine "legitimately absent" case exists, not be applied as a uniform house style.

**Why `note` is still optional in the schema despite being required for `OTHER`.** "Required when `reason = OTHER`" is a conditional constraint, and Prisma has no way to express one (the same limitation already logged for `@@check`). Rather than fake it by making `note` unconditionally required — which would force meaningless text onto the five self-explanatory reasons — it stays `String?` with the rule enforced at the write endpoint and recorded in `03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md` §1.1, the section that exists precisely for rules the schema can't hold.

The migration itself was documentation-and-DDL only, on a table with a confirmed 0 rows before and after. Prisma generated a `DROP COLUMN` + `ADD COLUMN` rather than a `RENAME`, and that was deliberately left alone rather than hand-edited into a rename: the column's *meaning* changed, not just its name, so carrying any old value across would have been wrong — a cost-based total silently reinterpreted as a per-set selling price is exactly the kind of corruption that looks like valid data forever after. With 0 rows the two are equivalent in effect; the drop is the more honest record of what happened.

One gap surfaced that this task's own scope doesn't cover: `Transaction` (already migrated, Phase 1) has no field linking it back to an `OrderLineItem`. Rule 95 confirms the custom-composition toggle belongs on the *fulfilling* `Transaction`, not the order line — correctly, `OrderLineItem` has no such field of its own — but without `Transaction.orderLineItemId`, a packing-time `STOCK_OUT` row (and any `TransactionSizeBreakdown` hanging off it) has no way back to the `Order` it fulfilled. Documented as a required addition for the actual migration task rather than silently fixed now, since `Transaction` isn't one of the three models this task was scoped to reconcile — and rather than silently left for someone to discover later.

Also removed the Phase 2 draft's stale re-declaration of `Party` (`runningDueBalance`, `tier`, a `PartyTier` enum, no `isActive`) rather than leaving it untouched next to a freshly-corrected `Order` block. Leaving an actively-wrong duplicate model sitting one paragraph away from the real fix would have been worse than removing it — replaced with a forward-note pointing at the real, migrated `Party` in §1 and what the eventual migration needs to `ALTER` onto it.

### Running the Phase 2 Order migration surfaced one real ambiguity the doc's literal spec couldn't survive contact with: `Party.tier`
The reconciled doc (previous entry, commit `56477cf`) specified `tier PartyTier` — required, no default — as the literal, final field. Implementing it exactly as written would have meant a Postgres `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN "tier" "PartyTier" NOT NULL` with no default, against a `Party` table that, by migration time, already had 9 real rows — including a genuine party, "SAI," actually in use. That's not just a Prisma quirk to route around: someone (me) would have had to invent a business classification for a real customer that no document anywhere had actually decided. Checked the real row count directly before writing any schema rather than assuming the table was still empty (`git log`/doc dates alone wouldn't have surfaced this — only a live query would).
