Flagged it explicitly and asked rather than picking a default unilaterally; the two live options were "make it nullable, deferring the real classification decision" or "default everyone to REGULAR, including SAI, silently." Chose nullable (`PartyTier?`) — deviates from the doc's literal non-nullable spec, but doesn't invent a fact about a real customer that nobody actually decided, and is consistent with rule 83's own framing that `tier` depends on Order history that doesn't exist yet. Updated `03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md` itself to reflect this as the actual final decision (not just the schema) — the same discipline this project has applied every other time an implementation detail forced a doc correction, so the two never quietly diverge. Verified afterward with a direct query: all 9 existing parties, SAI included, kept every original field byte-for-byte and simply got `tier: null`, `runningDueBalance: 0` — no invented data, nothing silently overwritten.

### The test database branch (`TEST_DATABASE_URL`) doesn't inherit migrations run against the real dev database — has to be brought up to date separately
Went to write automated tests for `POST/GET /api/orders` against `start:test` (the existing `NODE_ENV=test` mechanism, a separate Neon branch specifically so test runs never touch real dev data) and hit `prisma migrate status` reporting the Phase 2 Order migration as unapplied there — even though it had already been run against the real `DATABASE_URL` in the previous task. Obvious in hindsight (two separate Postgres databases have two separate migration histories; nothing keeps them in sync automatically), but easy to miss if a task assumes "the migration already ran" means "everywhere" rather than "on the one database it was pointed at." Checked `prisma migrate status` directly against `TEST_DATABASE_URL` before assuming the test branch was ready, rather than writing tests first and getting a confusing "table does not exist" failure. Fixed with `prisma migrate deploy` (not `migrate dev`) against `TEST_DATABASE_URL` specifically — `deploy` only applies existing migration files and never tries to generate a new one from schema drift, which is the correct tool once a migration already exists and just needs applying to a second database. Worth remembering for any future task that adds a migration and then tests against `start:test`: the test branch needs its own explicit `migrate deploy` every time, it's not automatic.

### Building New Order: two places where the task's own premise didn't match reality, checked directly rather than trusted
The task instructed "check how Receive Stock already implements [factory-disambiguation] and reuse it" (rule 54). Read `ReceiveStock.jsx` in full first: it has no such thing — Factory is a session-level dropdown chosen *before* any article lookup there, so a search is always already scoped to one Factory and never needs to disambiguate. Grepped the whole frontend for "disambiguat" to be sure — the only other hits are Live Stock's unrelated Location sub-grouping. Built the factory-disambiguation chips fresh (rule 54's text, §7's copy convention: "{article name} — {factory name}" per chip) rather than either inventing a fake "reuse" or silently building something different from what was asked. What *did* genuinely exist and got reused directly: the multi-colour staging mechanic (rule 53) and its `.chip`/`.stepper`/`.staged-list` CSS.

Separately, the task said the low-stock badge should be amber; rule 56 (which the task itself cited) says red, explicitly "unified... consistently across Live Stock, Pack Order, and New Order," explicitly replacing "earlier looser/inconsistent thresholds," and explicitly names New Order as in scope. Went with red (`badge-danger`, the same class Live Stock already uses at the same ≤2 threshold) rather than the task's literal wording, flagged prominently before writing any code rather than silently picking either. If amber was actually wanted, this is a one-line class change — but introducing it silently would have recreated the exact inconsistency rule 56 says it fixed.

### Moving stock deduction from Pack to Bill — putting the irreversible step at the only transition that can't be repeated (2026-08-17)

**The real problem this solves.** Packing originally performed the FIFO stock deduction: `PATCH /:id/pack` decremented `Stock` and wrote the `STOCK_OUT` `Transaction` rows. That reads fine for a single clean pass, but packing is fundamentally a *counting* step — a physical recount against the shelf, exactly the kind of thing a warehouse legitimately needs to redo (staff miscounted, a box was reopened, a colour was swapped). The moment you allow packing to happen twice, or allow an order to be edited and re-packed, you have an irreversible inventory deduction hanging off a repeatable action. Nothing in the schema or the endpoint prevented the second deduction from running; the only reason it hadn't bitten yet is that no re-pack path had been built.

That's the wrong shape for the problem. Guarding it (a `hasBeenDeducted` flag, an "already packed?" check, reversing the old deduction before re-deducting) all amount to bolting single-execution semantics onto a step that is *supposed* to be repeatable. Every one of those adds a new way to be wrong.

**Why Bill is the right home instead.** `Billed` is already rule 23's hard lock: once an order reaches it, nothing can edit it, no further adjustments land against it, and the only forward move is `Ship`. That property already existed for business reasons entirely independent of stock — and it's precisely the property a once-only irreversible operation needs. Putting deduction there means it can only ever run once *by construction*, not by a guard someone has to remember to maintain. Verified this directly rather than assuming: after billing, `pack` returns `409 ORDER_NOT_PLACED` and `bill` itself returns `409 ORDER_NOT_PACKED`, with stock confirmed unchanged by direct query — the double-deduction is structurally unreachable, not merely defended against.

The lifecycle now reads honestly: **Placed** = intent recorded, **Packed** = physically counted (staging, zero side effects), **Billed** = committed (stock moves, order locks), **Shipped** = dispatched. Each stage means one thing.

**One real design decision inside the move:** billing deducts each line's `qtySetsPacked`, not `qtySetsRequested`. Packing is what established what physically went in the box; billing commits against that reality, not the customer's original ask. A short-packed line therefore moves only what was really packed, and needs no second adjustment row — the `SHORT_PACKED` entry written at pack time already explains the gap.

**A consequence worth stating plainly:** `INSUFFICIENT_STOCK` is now genuinely reachable in normal operation, where before it was near-theoretical. Stock can legitimately move between packing and billing (another order bills the same stock first, a transfer runs, a correction lands), so billing can find the shelf emptier than packing did. That's not a regression — it's the honest failure mode of committing later, and it rejects the whole request atomically with nothing partially deducted rather than silently overselling. Tested explicitly by draining stock out from under a packed order before billing it.

**Migration note.** The real dev database was checked before any code changed (the task required it, and it mattered): it held 3 real orders, not 0. Two were already `PACKED` with 11 real `STOCK_OUT` rows against them from the old model — meaning under the new code, billing them would have deducted the same stock a *second* time. Confirmed by querying every affected bundle that all 11 lines would have succeeded in double-deducting rather than failing safely, including 6 that would have driven real stock to zero. Stopped and reported rather than guessing; on Aadi's decision those two orders were deleted and their 11 deductions reversed exactly (incrementing the same `stockId` each transaction had drawn from), restoring stock to its true pre-pack values. The third order was `PLACED` with nothing deducted, so it needed no intervention and flows correctly under the new model. This is the whole reason the "check the real DB first" step exists — the code change was trivially correct in isolation and would still have corrupted real inventory.

### Transaction Corrections — reversing rule 70, and why the Factory Payable query needed to change too (2026-08-21)

**The task assumed two things that turned out to be false, and both changed the design.**

First, it assumed `GET /api/history` already merged "Transactions" as one of its sources. Reading `historyController.js` directly showed only four real sources: Order events, `OrderAdjustment`, Transfer, Good Returns — Receive Stock receipts had no History presence at all. A correction can't be "surfaced in History, linked to the original" when the original has nowhere to appear, so adding `STOCK_IN` receipts as a fifth source became a genuine prerequisite of this task, not scope creep: it's also the only place an OWNER can find a specific receipt to correct in the first place, since no screen anywhere lists raw receipts (`ReceiveStock.jsx` only ever creates transactions, never lists them).

Second, the task's own gating instruction ("OWNER only, no PIN") directly contradicted an existing rule: rule 70 said *"Owner's view of History is read-only — no correction affordance on the owner surface, even though staff's History screen has one."* Checked staff's `History.jsx` before treating either side as authoritative — it had no correction UI at all, meaning rule 70 was forward-looking policy written before this feature existed, not a description of something already shipped that the task would be breaking. Flagged the conflict and asked rather than silently picking a side, since it determines who gets the button and which of two real screens it lives on. Landed on: OWNER-only placement (as the task asked), but PIN required specifically when a correction touches cost price — mirroring rule 71's existing "OWNER+PIN the moment a real price appears in the request, no exception for how it happened" rather than treating a correction as a loophole around that rule. Rule 70 updated in `05_BUSINESS_RULES.md` to record the reversal explicitly (struck through, not deleted) rather than left silently contradicting the code.

**The Factory Payable double-count risk was real, not hypothetical.** `getFactoryPayable` sums `type: 'STOCK_IN'` transactions unconditionally. The natural-looking correction implementation — reverse the wrong receipt with a `STOCK_OUT`, add a corrected `STOCK_IN` — would have left the payable figure *wrong in a new way*: `STOCK_OUT` is deliberately excluded from that sum by type (an ordinary sale/shipment shouldn't reduce what's owed to the factory), so the reversal contributes nothing, the old wrong `STOCK_IN` keeps counting forever, and the corrected replacement adds a second amount on top. Fixed by adding a `TransactionCorrection` link (`Transaction.correctionAsOriginal`, a one-to-one) and excluding any `STOCK_IN` that has one from the sum (`correctionAsOriginal: null`). This is why a plain `STOCK_OUT` reversal was never going to be sufficient on its own — the mechanism needed a way to say "this original no longer counts," and type alone couldn't express it.

**Why the replacement inherits the original's `costPriceSnapshot` by default, not today's live price.** A quantity or location correction doesn't mean the receiving event happened at a different time — it means one detail was mis-recorded. Re-deriving the price from `Product.costPrice` at correction time (the way an ordinary new `STOCK_IN` does) would silently re-price a months-old receipt against whatever the article costs today, which is a different and wrong thing to fix. The snapshot only changes when the correction explicitly says price was the mistake (`costPrice` present in the request body).

**Why `WRONG_FACTORY` is a reason value but not a separate code path.** `Transaction` has no Factory field — Factory is only ever inherited via `Bundle -> Product.factoryId`. Confirmed by reading the schema directly rather than assuming a dedicated field existed. "Wrong factory" and "wrong article, same factory" are mechanically identical (re-point at a different `bundleId`); the reason enum keeps them as distinguishable audit-trail labels anyway, since which kind of mistake it was is real information for whoever reads the log later.

**Scope was deliberately cut here.** Transfer corrections were part of the original ask but are a separate follow-up task — same mechanism in spirit (reverse both legs, reapply corrected legs), but building both resources in one pass would have meant validating the shared plumbing against two different reversal shapes at once. `TransactionCorrection`'s schema doesn't preclude a Transfer correction reusing it later; the model is keyed by transaction IDs, not receipt-specific fields.

---

### Transfer Corrections — why a dedicated model beat reusing TransactionCorrection, and the "invisible by construction" trick that made it work (2026-08-21)

**Reuse vs. a dedicated model.** The prior task's own note said `TransactionCorrection`'s schema "doesn't preclude" reusing it per-leg for a Transfer, but never committed to it. Tried reasoning it through before writing any schema: a Transfer produces *two* `Transaction` rows (`TRANSFER_OUT`/`TRANSFER_IN`), so reusing `TransactionCorrection` would mean two separate correction rows per Transfer correction — with no field linking them back into "these two are actually one event," fighting against `GET /api/history`'s own existing convention of reading Transfers from the `Transfer` table directly as one collapsed entry, never reconstructed from their legs. Worse, `TransactionCorrection.reason` is typed `TransactionCorrectionReason`, which has `WRONG_FACTORY`/`WRONG_PRICE` values that don't apply to a Transfer at all, and no way to distinguish "wrong FROM location" from "wrong TO location" the way a Transfer genuinely needs. A dedicated `TransferCorrection` — `originalTransferId`/`reversalTransferId`/`replacementTransferId`, all pointing at real `Transfer` rows, plus its own `TransferCorrectionReason` — mirrors `TransactionCorrection`'s original→replacement shape at the right level (Transfer, not Transaction) and produces exactly one History entry per correction with no reconstruction needed.

**The reversal needed to be a real `Transfer`, not bare `Transaction` rows — this was the actual hard part.** First instinct, copying the receipt correction's pattern directly: represent the reversal as two bare `STOCK_OUT`/`STOCK_IN` `Transaction` rows, same generic types the receipt correction used for its own reversal. This breaks specifically because `GET /api/history`'s `RECEIPT` source queries `type: 'STOCK_IN'` unconditionally — a bare `STOCK_IN` reversal leg (restoring stock at the original's source) would masquerade as a fake "X sets received at Y" entry, a real corruption of the audit trail (nothing was received from a factory; stock just moved back internally). Reusing `STOCK_IN`/`STOCK_OUT` for a Transfer's reversal collides with a rule that was invisible until this specific case was worked through by hand.

The fix: give the reversal real `TRANSFER_OUT`/`TRANSFER_IN` legs (a genuine `Transfer` row, fromLocation/toLocation swapped from the original — literally "transfer it back"), reusing the exact same paired-legs mechanism `POST /api/transfers` already uses. `TRANSFER_IN` is a different `TransactionType` value than `STOCK_IN`, so it's automatically excluded from the `RECEIPT` query with zero extra code — the same "invisible by construction" property the receipt correction's `STOCK_OUT` reversal had for a different reason (wrong *type*, not wrong *value*, but the same shape of correctness: pick the representation that's structurally incapable of colliding, don't add a filter to patch around a collision after the fact).

That traded one problem for a smaller one: a real `Transfer` row IS picked up by `GET /api/history`'s ordinary Transfer listing (which shows every row unconditionally, unlike `Transaction`'s type-filtered sources) — so the reversal would show up as a confusing extra "X sets transferred back" entry unless something excluded it. Solved with the same pattern already established for the receipt correction's originals: a reverse relation (`Transfer.correctionAsReversal`) and one filter (`correctionAsReversal: null`) on the ordinary `TRANSFER` query. The original stays visible (marked `corrected: true`) and the replacement stays visible as an ordinary-looking fresh Transfer — only the reversal, which is pure bookkeeping nobody asked to see, gets hidden.

**No PIN branch at all, unlike the receipt correction.** Confirmed directly rather than assumed: `costPriceSnapshot` is `null` on both of a Transfer's legs by design (`createTransfer`'s own comment: "an internal transfer... creates no such debt"), so there is no price field this endpoint could ever touch, and therefore no conditional PIN wrapper to build — `routes/transferCorrections.js` is `requireRole('OWNER')` alone, nothing else.

### PATCH /api/users/:id — reusing resetUserPassword's isPrimaryOwner check verbatim (2026-08-21)

**Why this endpoint gets no PIN, unlike password reset next to it.** The task was explicit that name/username editing sits at the same gating tier as deactivate/reactivate (role + `isPrimaryOwner`, no PIN) rather than the password-reset tier (role + `isPrimaryOwner` + PIN). Worth noting *why* that split makes sense rather than taking it on faith: a PIN in this app always marks "this specific write could let someone impersonate or lock out another person's access" (price, password) — renaming an account changes how it's labeled, not who can log into it or what it costs, so it doesn't cross that line.

**The isPrimaryOwner check is copied, not reimplemented.** `resetUserPassword` already has the exact restriction this endpoint needs (rule 97: a non-primary OWNER can freely act on any STAFF account and on themselves, but not on a *different* OWNER account without `isPrimaryOwner: true`). Wrote `updateUser`'s guard as the identical condition — `target.role === 'OWNER' && target.id !== req.user.id && !req.user.isPrimaryOwner` — rather than deriving it independently, specifically so the two endpoints can't quietly drift into different definitions of "who counts as untouchable" over time.

**Username uniqueness deliberately has no separate pre-check.** `createUser` doesn't pre-query for a collision — it attempts the `prisma.user.update`/`create` and catches the DB's own `@unique` constraint violation (`P2002`) as a `409`. `updateUser` does the same rather than adding a `findFirst`-style existence check first: a second, hand-written case-sensitivity rule would only be a duplicate of the DB's own collation to maintain and could drift out of sync with it (e.g. if the constraint's collation ever changed), where relying on the same constraint both endpoints already share can't.

**Partial-body semantics.** Body is `any subset of { name, username }`, not a full replace — only keys actually present in `req.body` get written (`data.name = name` guarded by `name !== undefined`, not `if (name)`, since an update that only touches `username` must leave `name` completely alone rather than defaulting it to something falsy).

### PATCH /api/orders/:id/lines — a false premise in the enum, and an investigation that reversed its own starting assumption (2026-08-21)

**The task's `OrderAdjustmentReason` premise was wrong, same shape of gap as Transaction Corrections' History assumption.** It said `QUANTITY_CHANGED` and `LINE_ADDED` "already exist in OrderAdjustmentReason." Grepped the schema and the whole codebase to be sure before writing anything: neither exists. The real enum has `QUANTITY_REDUCED` (decrease-only by name — every comment near it only ever describes a decrease — and, checked separately, not used by any live code path yet, since `qtySetsRequested` had never been written to anywhere before this endpoint), plus `ORDER_CANCELLED`/`RETURN_AFTER_DELIVERY`/`MISCALCULATION`/`SHORT_PACKED`/`OTHER`, none of which fit "quantity went up" or "a line was created." Added `QUANTITY_CHANGED` and `LINE_ADDED` as real new enum values via migration rather than force-fitting the existing ones — same resolution as the History-merge gap: a false premise about what already exists doesn't change the actual intent, it just means the intent needs a small structural addition to become buildable. A third value, `ORDER_EDITED`, was also added for the order-level `status` adjustment this endpoint writes when it reverts a `PACKED` order back to `PLACED` — `OrderAdjustment` has no `note` field to fall back on `OTHER` with an explanation (unlike `TransactionCorrection`/`TransferCorrection`, which do), and 03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md §2's own "routine forward transition" carve-out for a null `reason` explicitly only covers forward transitions — this one goes backward, so by the schema's own stated rule it needed a real, named reason.

**The investigation question was answered NO, and that reversed which approach actually helps.** The task asked whether Pack Order's checklist (the `confirmed` state in `PackOrderDetail.jsx`) genuinely re-derives from real `qtySetsPacked` in the database on load, since that determines whether a touched-line-only `qtySetsPacked` reset is actually safe versus a whole-order reset. Read the component directly: it does **not** — the file's own block comment says so explicitly ("Deliberately NO seeding of `confirmed` here... nothing is confirmed until a human taps it"), a deliberate redesign from 2026-08-19 that replaced an earlier version which *did* pre-fill from stale state. Checked further whether raw `qtySetsPacked` leaks through anywhere else while an order is `PLACED`: `dashboard/Orders.jsx` explicitly special-cases it (`status === 'PLACED' ? 'Not yet packed' : ...`), and `PATCH /:id/pack` itself always requires full coverage of every live line on its next real submission, overwriting every line's `qtySetsPacked` regardless of what was left there. So a touched-line-only reset (chosen) is safe for a subtler reason than the task's own framing suggested: not because the frontend rehydrates confirmed state and would show it correctly, but because *nothing anywhere shows the DB's raw value for a PLACED order at all*, so an untouched line's real historical count can be preserved risk-free — it just has no visible effect either way until the order is genuinely re-packed, at which point it's superseded regardless. Whole-order reset would have thrown away real audit data (what was actually counted on a shelf) for zero corresponding safety benefit.

**Nulling `packedAt` on reversion wasn't asked for explicitly but followed from the field's own doc comment.** `Order.packedAt`'s existing schema comment reads "set when status transitions to PACKED" — once status reverts, that stops being true, so leaving a stale timestamp there would contradict the field's own stated meaning. This is the first backward transition this schema has ever needed to represent; every other stage timestamp (`billedAt`, `shippedAt`) only ever moves forward, so there was no existing precedent to check against, just the field's own comment to stay consistent with.

**Testing note.** One test run hit a `Transaction already closed` error (Prisma's default 5000ms interactive-transaction timeout) with ~19.6s elapsed — a real cold-start/network stall against the Neon Postgres connection, not a logic bug (the transaction itself only performs a handful of small sequential writes, the same shape every other order-mutating endpoint here already uses). Confirmed by simply retrying: it passed clean on the next attempt with no code change.

### Pack Order tone pass — a shared-class trap the task's own scope rule was written to catch, and why the CSS quiets more than it recolors (2026-08-21)

**`.line-cancel-link` and `.order-cancel-button` turned out to be shared with Bill Order, not Pack-Order-exclusive despite the "pack-" naming pattern nearby.** Grepped every `.jsx` page for the classes actually named in the task before touching any of them, rather than assuming from the file scope alone — `BillOrderDetail.jsx` uses both verbatim (its own per-line cancel link and order-level cancel button reuse them outright, plus `.pack-line-color-chip` for the colour-name chip). Editing either in place would have quietly muted Bill Order's cancel affordance too, exactly the "changing them here would silently change every other screen too" risk the task's own scope rule named for the explicitly-listed global tokens — it just didn't occur to name these two, since they don't look global from their class names. Resolved the same way the task said to handle a genuine shared-token need: didn't touch `.line-cancel-link`/`.order-cancel-button` at all, and instead added a new, real Pack-Order-only class (`.pack-line-cancel-link`) applied only in `PackOrderDetail.jsx`'s own JSX, muted by default and red only on `:hover`/`:focus-visible`. `.pack-line-row`, `.pack-line-row-confirmed`, `.pack-line-status-*`, `.pack-line-tap`, `.pack-line-adjust-btn` were all confirmed genuinely exclusive to this one file before being edited directly.

**Quieted the short-pack row too, not just the confirmed row the task named explicitly.** The task's own bullet list only called out "the confirmed-line highlight (solid green fill)," but `.pack-line-row-short` had the identical problem — a full solid `--warning-bg` fill — for the identical reason. Leaving one saturated full-fill state while muting the other would have undercut the whole point: a several-line order with two short-packed lines would still show two loud amber cards competing with everything else. Extended the same whisper-tint-plus-left-border treatment to both, on the reasoning that "apply restraint... extend that instinct further" was describing a principle to apply consistently, not a literal two-item checklist.

**The status badge and low-stock badges were left completely untouched, correctly, and that wasn't actually a hard call.** The task asked to "check whether their saturation/weight can soften" but also explicitly forbade touching `.badge-warning`/`.badge-danger` (shared global tokens). No real tension: both are already small pills, not full-row fills — they already satisfy the "small flag, never a fully-tinted row" bar this whole task was extending, so there was nothing to soften and nothing to justify a shared-token exception for.

**Screenshot methodology.** Needed a real multi-state comparison (confirmed / short-pack / unconfirmed / low-stock, several lines at once), not an empty screen — `git stash` (scoped to just the two changed files) produced a true "before" against the unmodified CSS/JSX without losing the in-progress work, screenshotted a real order via Playwright, `git stash pop`, then re-ran the identical tap/adjust/hover sequence against the same order for "after." Re-running the interaction sequence twice was necessary, not just convenient: Pack Order's `confirmed` state is deliberately ephemeral per-page-load (see the Order-Lines-Edit entry above), so there was no DB state to simply reload against for "before" — each screenshot is a fresh sequence of real UI taps.

**A stale `.env` value blocked the browser entirely, unrelated to this task but discovered by it.** `frontend/.env`'s `VITE_API_BASE_URL` pointed at `192.168.1.12`, this machine's LAN IP from a previous network; the current one is `192.0.0.2`, so every request failed with `ERR_ADDRESS_IN_USE` and login silently hung. Fixed locally to `localhost` for the duration of testing (Vite only reads `.env` at server start, so this needed a dev-server restart, not just HMR), then restored the original LAN value byte-for-byte afterward — `.env` is gitignored, machine-specific, and not this task's to permanently change, so the fix was scoped to the test session and the stale value was left for Aadi to refresh himself (the file's own comment already documents how).

### Pack Order tone pass, part 2 — the bottom action bar and a focus-ring bug the first pass never looked for (2026-08-21)

**The blue outline was confirmed to be exactly what it looked like: an entirely unstyled default `:focus-visible` ring.** Grepped `index.css` for any existing `:focus`/`:focus-visible`/`outline` rule touching `.pack-line-tap` before assuming — there was none (the only pre-existing focus rule anywhere near this screen was the one added for `.pack-line-cancel-link` in the first tone pass). Verified directly in a real browser rather than by inspection alone: `document.activeElement.matches(':focus-visible')` returned `true` and `getComputedStyle(...).outline` reported Chromium's own UA default (`auto` style, `#005fcc`-family blue) — not a leftover custom rule, not a browser quirk, genuinely nothing styling it. Reproducing this needed *real* keyboard `Tab` presses, not a programmatic `el.focus()` call — the first attempt used `.focus()` and Chromium happily reported `:focus-visible` as matching in computed-style checks but never actually painted a visible ring in the screenshot, which would have been a false negative if trusted. Real `page.keyboard.press('Tab')` navigation was the only way to get a screenshot that actually showed the bug before fixing it, and actually showed the fix afterward.

**The fix is deliberately NOT any of the app's semantic role colours.** `07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md` §3.1 reserves success/danger/warning/accent for MEANING ("each role has a fixed meaning, applied consistently") — a focus ring communicates "this is where your keyboard input currently is," not a status, so recolouring it accent-blue (still blue, just softer) or any other role tint would have been quietly claiming a meaning it doesn't have. Used `--text-primary` instead, the same neutral-dark tone the design tokens already use for "Selected chip" — reusing an existing "this is deliberately highlighted" convention rather than inventing a new one.

**`.btn-primary`/`.btn-danger` were kept in the className, not replaced — only overridden.** Both are shared base-layout rules (width, min-height, border-radius, type scale) reused by every button in the app, grouped together in one shared selector (`.btn-primary, .btn-secondary, .btn-danger { ... }`) — dropping them from the className to write a fully independent button class would have meant re-declaring all of that structural CSS a second time for no reason, and risked drifting out of sync with the shared button contract later. Added `.pack-mark-packed-btn`/`.pack-order-cancel-btn` alongside them instead, overriding only colour — same "additive scoped class, not a modification of the shared one" pattern the first tone pass already established for `.pack-line-cancel-link` vs. `BillOrderDetail.jsx`'s shared `.line-cancel-link`. Relies on source order for the override (both new rules are declared later in the file than `.btn-primary`/`.btn-danger`), same mechanism, not anything more fragile than what the first pass already shipped.

**"Cancel this order" default state deliberately does NOT follow §3.1's "background/border/text share the same role tint" rule to the letter.** An outlined button with a transparent background isn't one of the tri-tint combinations that section describes (background/border/text all danger-tinted) — it's background=transparent, border+text=danger-tinted, escalating to the full tri-tint fill only on hover/focus. Treated as a deliberate, narrow exception rather than a violation: §3.1 is describing how a component should look when it IS actively signalling its role, and this button's whole point is to NOT signal "danger" at rest — the moment it actually matters (hover/focus, about to be pressed), it snaps to the exact same solid fill `.btn-danger` uses everywhere else, so the role-tint rule is honoured right at the point it exists to protect.

### Owner Dashboard Article Pricing — a factory PICKER on mobile became a factory COLUMN on desktop, and why PinPrompt forced a real two-step (2026-08-21)

**Confirmed, not assumed: pending articles are in mobile's fetch, and sorted first on purpose.** Read `pages/ArticlePricing.jsx` before building anything — `listProducts({ factoryId })` is unfiltered by price, and its own `sortedProducts` comment says surfacing pending rows first "is the whole point, not a cosmetic sort choice." Included them here too, on the same reasoning restated for this specific page: an owner pricing-management tool with pending articles hidden would be actively worse at the one job it exists to do.

**The desktop table does NOT mirror mobile's factory-picker structure, and that was a deliberate departure, not an oversight.** Mobile filters to one Factory at a time (`GET /api/products?factoryId=`) because on a phone that's the only way to keep a list short enough to use. This page fetches unfiltered and makes Factory a real, sortable COLUMN instead — confirmed the right call against real data after building it: the dev DB has genuine same-`articleNo`-different-Factory collisions (`6099` exists under both Comeco and RDX, `6002` under both Jyoti Creations and RDX) that a factory-picker would hide one at a time but a Factory column disambiguates in place, visible in the same screenshot. `GET /api/products` itself only ever selects `factoryId` (a scalar), never a `factory: { name }` relation — the name-lookup Map (`factoryNameById`, built from a separate `listFactories()` call) exists specifically to bridge that gap, not because the backend needed changing.

**PinPrompt's own `<form>` is why this couldn't be mobile's one-step combined form, structurally, not just by preference.** Mobile's PIN field sits inline in the SAME `<form>` as cost/selling price and submits all three together in one call — exactly the "hand-copied inline PIN form" PinPrompt's own header comment names as one of the three not-yet-refactored copies. PinPrompt renders its own `<form>` internally; nesting a `<form>` inside a `<form>` is invalid HTML, so using it at all forces a genuine two-step reveal (price fields staged first, PinPrompt only once they validate) — the same shape Parties.jsx's Record Payment and History.jsx's price corrections already use for the identical reason. This is not extra scope invented for this task; it's the direct structural consequence of the task's own instruction to use PinPrompt directly.

**A `<form>` can't span multiple `<td>` cells in one `<tr>`, which shaped the two-step's actual markup.** First instinct was to keep the two-step entirely within the editing row's own cells. That doesn't work: a `<form>` isn't a valid direct child of `<tr>` (only `<td>`/`<th>` are), so a single form can't wrap the Cost Price cell + Selling Price cell + an Action-cell button together. Step 1 sidesteps this by not being a form at all — plain `<input>`s with `onChange` and a plain `type="button"` "Continue" (an `onKeyDown` Enter handler on each input recovers the submit-on-Enter convenience a real form would have given for free). Step 2's `PinPrompt` gets its own dedicated sub-`<tr>` with one `<td colSpan>` beneath the editing row — a single cell can validly contain a `<form>`, and `colSpan` lets that one cell still visually span the full row width.

**Deliberately tightened one thing beyond mobile's own behaviour: editing a second row while one is already open is disabled, not silently allowed.** Mobile's single combined form has nothing granular to lose by switching rows mid-edit (unsaved price fields, at worst). This page's two-step form can be sitting at the PIN step with a real staged price when a different row's Edit is clicked — silently abandoning a PIN-step-in-progress edit is a worse experience than mobile's own looser behaviour, so `editingId !== null && editingId !== product.id` disables every other row's Edit button while one is open. Called out explicitly here since "same PIN-gated flow as mobile" was the instruction and this is a real, intentional divergence from mobile's actual behaviour, not an oversight.

**Testing confirmed the real endpoint, not just a passing UI.** Intercepted every `PATCH /api/products/:id` network call during the Playwright test rather than trusting the UI's success banner alone — confirmed exactly one PATCH fired for the wrong-PIN attempt (rejected with the real `INVALID_PIN` + attempts-remaining message, not a client-side simulation) and exactly one more for the successful save, both hitting the literal same URL and carrying `{ costPrice, sellingPrice, pin }` in the body — the same shape and the same endpoint `updateProduct` in `api/products.js` already sends for mobile, confirming no parallel price-write path was created.

### OWNER Home tile reorganisation — a 5-tone palette, two tiles that needed new colours, and why `TILES` had to split by role (2026-08-22)

**Investigated the starting state directly rather than trusting the task's framing.** Read `Home.jsx`'s `TILES`/`MORE_ITEMS` arrays before changing anything — confirmed Article Pricing and History were genuinely in `MORE_ITEMS` (not tiles), and confirmed Change PIN, Manage Users, and Manage Parties were already in `MORE_ITEMS` (nothing to move for those three, just to leave alone).

**The colour-clash fix ran into a real palette constraint the task's own phrasing didn't anticipate.** "Give Bill Orders and Ship Order two NEW, distinct colours... from role-tints already established" assumes at least two unclaimed tones exist. Checking §3.4 directly showed the app has exactly five: Success, Danger, Warning, Accent, Purple. Four were already spoken for by tiles once Live Stock keeps Accent (Success→Receive, Purple→New Order, Warning→Pack Order), leaving exactly **one** free tone — Danger — for **two** tiles. Reusing an already-claimed tone would just relocate the clash; Danger already carries a real meaning elsewhere in this same app ("Low stock/Damaged/Remove"), so handing it to a routine action risks the wrong signal. Surfaced this as a real constraint conflict via `AskUserQuestion` rather than picking silently — the user chose to add one genuinely new token (Teal, `--teal-*` in `index.css` + §3.4) for Ship Order, and let Bill Orders take Danger (closer semantic fit for money-owed than Ship has). Documented in both `index.css` and `07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md` §3.4 as an explicit, dated departure from the table's own "Ship/Billed under Accent" grouping — not a bug, a deliberate one-off.

**A second gap the task left genuinely unspecified: what colour do the two *promoted* tiles (Article Pricing, History) get?** The instruction only addressed the Bill/Ship/Live-Stock clash. By the time that was resolved, all five original tones plus the new Teal were already claimed by the six pre-existing tiles — nothing was left for two more, and inventing a 6th/7th semantic tone wasn't in scope. Rather than ask a second question mid-task, used existing precedent: §3.4 already defines "Neutral card/border" tokens, just never applied to a tile. Added `.tile-neutral` reusing those verbatim (no new hex) and reasoned that Article Pricing/History are being promoted for *convenience*, not elevated to the "core, frequent, semantically-distinct" status §5.1 reserves colour for — a plain neutral tile reads as exactly that distinction visually. Flagged clearly in the code comment and this entry for review rather than presented as obviously correct. *(Superseded the same day — see the entry directly below: specific colours, Rust and Lavender, were requested once this was reviewed, and `.tile-neutral` was removed as dead code once nothing referenced it any more.)*

**Why `TILES` became two separate arrays (`STAFF_TILES` / `OWNER_TILES`) instead of one array with per-item `show(user)`, which was the existing pattern.** That pattern can vary *visibility* and, with more work, *order* per role — but not a tile's own *tone* for the same route rendered to different roles. Ship Order needed exactly that: STAFF keeps it on Accent blue (untouched, since STAFF must see zero change), OWNER moves it to Teal. One shared object can't hold two different `tone` values for the same entry depending on who's looking at it without inventing a second per-role field anyway — at which point two explicit arrays reads more honestly than one array pretending to be role-agnostic. `STAFF_TILES` is deliberately left as a flat, ungated array (STAFF is the only role that ever reads it now), so there was nothing left to accidentally regress. *(STAFF's Ship Order tone specifically was superseded the same day — see below — but the two-array split itself is still the right shape and got MORE necessary, not less, once STAFF gained its own extra tile.)*

**MORE_ITEMS needed asymmetric handling for the two promoted items, not identical treatment.** Article Pricing was already owner-only there, so it could just be deleted outright — STAFF's filtered view never included it, so removing the row changes nothing STAFF-visible. History was unconditional (`() => true`, both roles saw it in More), so deleting it outright would have been a real STAFF-facing regression; instead its `show` became `(user) => user.role !== 'OWNER'`, which is behaviourally identical to `true` for STAFF (byte-for-byte unchanged) while correctly hiding it from OWNER now that OWNER sees it as a tile instead.

**Verified STAFF's "zero change" claim with real Playwright output, not by reading the diff and assuming.** Logged in as a fresh throwaway STAFF probe account and dumped the actual rendered tile hrefs/labels/classes and More-list hrefs/labels — confirmed the exact same 5 tiles in the exact same order with the exact same tones (Ship Order still `tile-accent`), and the exact same 5 More rows in the exact same order, against the pre-task array contents. Also confirmed OWNER's rendered output matches the specified order and tone list exactly, and that no console errors fired on either login. *(The "zero change" claim itself was true at the time and stayed true for four of STAFF's five original tiles — but see below for the two deliberate, confirmed exceptions added the same day: Ship Order's colour, and a sixth tile.)*

### Same-day follow-up to the Home tile reorganisation — real colours for the neutral placeholder, Ship Order unified across roles, and a 9th tone for a STAFF-only promotion (2026-08-22)

**Built directly on the uncommitted state from the entry above, not a fresh read of history.** All four changes in this task modify arrays/tokens the previous entry had just introduced the same day — Article Pricing/History's tones, STAFF's Ship Order tone, and MORE_ITEMS' Transfer/Good Returns rows. Confirmed the actual current file contents first rather than assuming the prior entry's description was still literally true of the code (it was, but that's a check worth doing every time state has been building up across several same-day tasks).

**Article Pricing → Rust, History → Lavender — replacing the neutral placeholder, not layering a new decision on top of it.** The placeholder existed specifically because the original task never specified a colour; once real colours were requested, `.tile-neutral` had exactly zero remaining references (confirmed with a grep across the whole frontend before removing it) — genuinely dead code, not speculatively unused, so it was deleted outright rather than left as an orphaned rule nobody would ever clean up later.

**Ship Order unifying to Teal for both roles is a deliberate, explicit reversal of the previous entry's "STAFF stays byte-for-byte unchanged" framing — confirmed by the user, not inferred.** The original task was explicit that STAFF's Home screen must see zero change; this task explicitly named Ship Order's colour as the one deliberate exception to that rule. Worth naming as a genuine reversal rather than glossing over it, since a future task reading only the *first* entry above would otherwise get a wrong, outdated picture of STAFF's Ship Order tile — which is exactly why that entry now carries an inline superseded-note pointing here instead of being silently left to contradict this one.

**The Transfer Stock promotion is the first time this app has needed a genuinely 9th tile colour, and it surfaced a real "same colour, two meanings" risk that hadn't come up before.** Every previous colour decision in this app only had to avoid clashing *within one screen*. This one had to also avoid clashing *across* the two role-specific Home screens, because one person — the owner — sees both. Danger was ruled out for the now-familiar "borrowing the app's own problem-signal tone for a routine action" reason. Rust and Lavender were ruled out for a *new* kind of reason specific to this cross-screen situation: they don't clash with anything on STAFF's own screen, but they'd silently mean "Article Pricing" and "History" to the one viewer who'd see both meanings side by side over time. Landed on a new Indigo token rather than force a reuse, same "add a token when there's a genuine need, not by default" precedent Teal/Rust/Lavender already set — flagged clearly rather than picked silently, since this is exactly the kind of palette-scarcity tension worth surfacing each time it recurs, not just the first time.

**MORE_ITEMS' Transfer row needed the *mirror image* of History's earlier asymmetric-show fix, not a new pattern.** History went from unconditional to STAFF-only when it was promoted for OWNER. Transfer goes from unconditional to OWNER-only now that it's promoted for STAFF — same `(user) => user.role === EXCLUDED_ROLE` shape, just the excluded role flipped, because the tile it's duplicating moved to the opposite role this time.

**Confirmed Good Returns' exact existing label before changing it, rather than assuming the wording from memory.** Read the literal string out of `MORE_ITEMS` (`'Good Returns'`) before writing the replacement, per the task's own explicit instruction not to assume — trivial in this case (the wording matched expectation) but worth doing as a matter of habit, since the cases where a remembered label turns out to be stale or slightly different are exactly the cases silent assumption would get wrong.

**Re-verified both roles from scratch in a real browser rather than trusting the diff.** Fresh throwaway probe accounts (created and cleaned up the same way as the entry above), confirmed OWNER's 8 tiles/order/tones unchanged from the reorganisation, confirmed STAFF now shows 6 tiles with Ship Order on `tile-teal` and Transfer Stock appended in `tile-indigo`, confirmed Transfer no longer duplicated in STAFF's More list, confirmed the "GR - Goods Return" label renders for both roles, and confirmed zero console errors on either login.

### Receive Stock color search — a task premise that assumed chip grids for something that's actually one shared `<select>`, and a controlled-input bug the fix had to guard against (2026-08-22)

**The task's own framing ("colour chip grid(s)", "likely two different kinds" of color list) didn't match what's actually in `ReceiveStock.jsx`.** Read the file directly before writing anything, rather than building toward the described chip-grid UI. There are no color chips anywhere in this file — chips exist only for SIZE selection (adult/kids). Colors are picked through a single shared `CreatableSelect` (a real `<select>`, extracted to its own component and reused by Location/Factory/Color pickers across several screens). More importantly, the "two different kinds of list" the task hypothesized — an article's own valid colors vs. a broader growing system-wide list — used to be two separate things in this codebase, but an earlier task (see `colorPickerSource`'s own header comment, "the REAL GAP fix") had already unified them into one deduplicated union, rendered by exactly one `CreatableSelect` instance that's identically reused for both a matched article and a just-created one (both hit the same JSX branch). Reporting this rather than silently forcing a two-chip-grid design that doesn't exist in the code was the point — same "investigate, state which is true, then build on what's actually there" approach this project has needed several times before when a task's premise didn't survive contact with the real file.

**Added the search as a state/filtering layer in `ReceiveStock.jsx` itself, not as a feature on the shared `CreatableSelect` component.** `CreatableSelect` is reused by Factory and Location pickers on other screens that don't have a growing-list problem — adding search there unconditionally would be scope creep onto screens nobody asked to touch. Instead, `ReceiveStock.jsx` computes a `filteredColorOptions` array from its own already-fetched `availableColors` and hands that to `CreatableSelect`'s existing `options` prop, exactly the same way it already computes and hands off `availableColors` today. The shared component needed zero changes.

**A real correctness trap: filtering `options` can silently "lose" a controlled `<select>`'s own displayed value.** A native `<select value={selectedColorId}>` falls back to showing nothing meaningful if `selectedColorId` isn't among the currently rendered `<option>`s. Once a color is picked (but not yet staged), typing an unrelated search term into the new filter would have — without a fix — visually made the picker look like the selection had vanished, even though `selectedColorId` (and the Sets stepper hanging off it) were still fully intact. Fixed by always keeping the actively-selected color's option present in `filteredColorOptions` regardless of whether it matches the current search text, while keeping the *separate* "no matches" message driven by the real, un-padded substring-match count — so the message stays honest about the search itself even when the select is quietly keeping the active pick visible underneath it.

**Verified the "only one spot needs this" finding empirically, not just by reading the code.** Ran the fix against a real matched article (6002/RDX, 6 existing colors), confirming the `<select>`'s own options narrow correctly ("bl" → Black + blue), clearing restores the full list, and a no-match search ("zzzznotarealcolor") shows the message while "+ Create new color…" stays present and clickable. Then separately walked a brand-new article all the way through creation (name, category, one size, Create article) and confirmed the identical search box and identical filtering behavior appear there too ("green" → Olive Green + Green) — empirical proof the shared block really is shared, not just plausible from reading the JSX once.

### A real live-filtering Combobox, and a task premise that assumed New Order/Good Returns were CreatableSelect when they're genuinely something else (2026-08-22)

**Built `components/Combobox.jsx` as its own component, not a further patch on the search-box-plus-select shape from the entry directly above.** That shape (a plain `<input>` filtering a native `<select>`'s options) was a reasonable minimal fix for its own task, but it structurally can't deliver what this task asked for — live narrowing per keystroke with no separate "search step," arrow-key navigation through the results, Enter-to-select, a "+ Create new" row living inside the same list — because a native `<select>`'s own option list isn't something React can keep redrawing against on every keystroke while the browser owns its open/closed state and its own keyboard handling. Superseding it needed a real component, not an incremental extension.

**Prop shape deliberately mirrors `CreatableSelect`'s own** (`fieldLabel`/`value`/`onChange`/`options`/`disabled`/`placeholder`/`canCreate`/`onCreate`) even though the implementation shares nothing with it — this is what let Receive Stock's color picker become a near drop-in swap (same `selectedColorId` state, same `handleColorChange`/`handleCreateColor` handlers, same `availableColors` list already computed for the old picker), matching the task's own "don't change what gets stored, only how it's picked."

**The "already-selected stays valid" guarantee needed a genuinely different mechanism than the native-`<select>` version did, because the failure mode itself is different.** The earlier entry's bug was a native `<select>` silently rendering its placeholder instead of the real value when that value wasn't among the currently-filtered `<option>`s — a browser-level fact about controlled `<select>` elements. A text input has no equivalent failure: its displayed text is its own state, not derived from which dropdown rows are currently visible, so it can't "go blank" that way. What it CAN do instead is drift — showing stale or partial typed text that no longer matches the real `value` once the dropdown closes without an explicit pick. Fixed with a `useEffect` that re-syncs the input's displayed text from `value`/`options` whenever the dropdown is closed (guarded so it never fires *while* someone is actively typing/browsing) — the same underlying promise ("the picker never visibly disagrees with the real selection"), delivered by resyncing state instead of by keeping a stale option artificially present in a list.

**Chose one-shot creation over porting `CreatableSelect`'s two-step name-reveal.** `CreatableSelect`'s "+ Create new…" swaps the whole picker into a separate name-input-plus-Create-button step. The task's spec for this component never mentions a second step, and explicitly asks for "no separate search step, no second click into anything" — so the create row instead reuses whatever's already typed in the combobox's own input as the new option's name directly (`+ Create new color "Turquoise"`), one click or Enter and it's created, selected, and the dropdown closes — no retyping the name a second time into a different field.

**Investigated New Order and Good Returns before touching either, per the task's own explicit instruction, and the premise was wrong for both.** Grepped both files for `CreatableSelect`/`chip`/`<select` rather than assuming they matched Receive Stock's now-fixed pattern. Neither uses `CreatableSelect` for color at all — both use a genuinely different, MULTI-select checkable colour-chip grid (`toggleColorChip`/`checkedColors`, several colors checked at once, each revealing its own inline Sets stepper, one "Add selected" commits every checked color as a batch) — Good Returns' own file header comment confirms this was deliberately copied from New Order's pattern. `CreatableSelect` does appear in Good Returns, but for the Location field ("Return into"), not color. This is a real, load-bearing distinction the task itself anticipated as a possibility but didn't resolve: the combobox as specified is a strictly single-pick widget (one click/Enter fills the input and closes), while New Order/Good Returns need to let someone pick several colors in one pass before committing anything — swapping either screen's picker for a literal single-select combobox as specified would remove real, documented functionality (07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md §5.3's "one 'Add selected' button adds ALL currently-checked colors together"), not just restyle it. Flagged this back to the user rather than guessing which way to resolve it, before writing anything for either screen.

**Resolved by checking the actual data source, not just the rendering pattern — and it confirmed neither screen needed this fix at all.** Both screens' chip grids are fed by `GET /api/products/:id/valid-colors`, which the backend controller's own comment describes precisely: "a Color only appears here if a Bundle actually links it to this specific Product." That's a bounded, per-article list — typically a handful of real variants for one garment style — structurally incapable of growing the way Receive Stock's `globalColors` (the entire system-wide catalog) does, and with no "create new color" escape hatch feeding it either. The "list keeps growing" premise this whole task exists to solve genuinely doesn't apply here. Confirmed this against the real endpoint before concluding, then left both screens untouched per the user's explicit instruction once confirmed — Receive Stock's Combobox migration is the complete scope of this task.

### "Others" collapsible group on Home's More list — reusing the nested accordion outside actual nesting, and a role split that had to widen an existing precedent (2026-08-25)

**Confirmed the real starting state before touching anything, per the task's own instruction.** Read `Home.jsx`'s actual `MORE_ITEMS` directly rather than working from memory — confirmed all four named items (Manage Users, Set PIN/Change PIN, Manage Parties, Factory Payables) were genuinely present with those exact labels, and confirmed Manage Parties specifically was the ONE of the four still unconditional (any role) — the other three were already OWNER-only.

**Reused `.accordion-section.nested`/`.accordion-header.nested`/`.accordion-body.nested` (Live Stock's Factory→Article second tier) rather than the outer/top-level accordion variant, even though "Others" isn't literally nested inside another accordion.** The outer variant (`.accordion-section` without `.nested`) expects its own `.card` shell — Live Stock's own top-level Factory sections render as `className="card accordion-section"`. The More list has no card wrapper; every row in it (`.secondary-link-row`) is a plain divider-separated row instead. The nested variant is styled for exactly that visual situation (a plain top-divider, no card of its own) regardless of whether it's technically nested inside a parent accordion — reused it for what it visually does, not for where it's normally found in the DOM.

**Set PIN and Change PIN both had to move into `OTHERS_ITEMS`, even though the task named only "Change PIN."** They're a single conceptual slot — exact inverse conditions on the same `hasPinSet` flag, never both visible, never neither (documented at their original `MORE_ITEMS` home). Moving only "Change PIN" and leaving "Set PIN" stranded as a flat row would have split one slot across two different UI locations depending on whether an owner had a PIN set yet, which isn't what "group the PIN row" can have meant. Moved both, kept their exact existing mutually-exclusive condition unchanged.

**Manage Parties needed the same asymmetric `show(user)` flip History and Transfer already used in the two prior same-week Home tasks, not a new mechanism.** It was the one item of the four that STAFF could actually see (unconditional, not owner-gated) — moving it into an OWNER-only collapsible group meant its `MORE_ITEMS` entry had to become STAFF-only (`role !== 'OWNER'`) while an identical-content entry was added to `OTHERS_ITEMS` for OWNER. Directly reused the pattern already established for History (moved into a tile for OWNER, `MORE_ITEMS` twin flipped to STAFF-only) and Transfer (moved into a tile for STAFF, `MORE_ITEMS` twin flipped to OWNER-only) — third occurrence of the same shape in as many tasks, at this point a settled convention rather than a one-off.

**Positioned "Others" last in OWNER's flat list — a judgment call, flagged as such per the task's own request.** No order was specified. Appending after every remaining flat row (Transfer Stock, Low Stock, GR - Goods Return, Owner Dashboard) reads as a settings/admin catch-all tucked at the bottom, the same "append, don't renumber" precedent already used for Locations, Article Pricing, and Transfer Stock's own tile placement — rather than replacing one of the four removed items' old positions, which would have left the group's placement depending on which specific item used to sit there, a fact with no real meaning going forward.

**Verified both roles with real Playwright output, not by reading the diff.** Fresh throwaway probe accounts: confirmed OWNER's flat rows are exactly the four survivors (Transfer Stock, Low Stock, GR - Goods Return, Owner Dashboard) plus a collapsed "Others" that expands to exactly the four named items and re-collapses on a second click; confirmed STAFF's flat rows are byte-for-byte what they were before this task (Low Stock, History, GR - Goods Return, Manage Parties) with no "Others" element present in the DOM at all for that role. Zero console errors either login.

### Bill Order discount/GST capture — finding the REAL "existing correct total" turned out to require reading three different files' comments, not one (2026-08-25)

**Investigated `billOrder()`'s "reuse the existing correct calculation" instruction directly, and it pointed somewhere non-obvious.** There are actually TWO different "order total" calculations already living in this codebase, computing genuinely different numbers: `listOrders`' own `totalValue` (`qtySetsRequested`-based, used for every list-view row across Pack/Bill/Ship Order lists and the dashboard) and `BillOrderDetail.jsx`'s own per-article `group.total` (`qtySetsPacked`-based, with its own comment explicitly justifying the difference: "this is the PRE-BILLING screen and packed quantity is the authoritative figure before billing"). Rule 100 (added 2026-08-20, `utils/revenue.js` vs `utils/locationRevenue.js`) turned out to independently confirm the same distinction from the opposite direction — `qtySetsRequested` and actually-billed quantity only agree when a line was never short-packed. Since this task is specifically about the moment of BILLING, the correct basis is unambiguously `qtySetsPacked` — reusing `listOrders`' `totalValue` formula instead (the more "generic" of the two, and the one dashboard/Orders.jsx's own header comment calls "already correct") would have been reusing the WRONG existing calculation, silently overcharging a Party on any order that was ever short-packed. Confirmed this with three separate pieces of existing evidence (BillOrderDetail's own comment, dashboard Orders.jsx's own comment distinguishing its `lineValue()` from BillOrderDetail's basis, and rule 100) before writing a single line of the new calculation, rather than picking whichever "total" was easiest to find first.

**Extracted the qtySetsPacked-based formula into a shared frontend util (`utils/orderBilling.js`) instead of duplicating it a third time.** Both real billing entry points (`BillOrderDetail.jsx`, dashboard `Orders.jsx`) need the identical live preview, computed from the identical `getOrder()` line-item shape — this is exactly the "already a closed unit, reused verbatim by more than one caller" case this project's own precedent (`piecesPerSetFor`'s consolidation history, `CreatableSelect`'s extraction) says to extract rather than copy-paste. `preBillingTotal(lineItems)` and `computeBillingAmounts({...})` are the two exports; the backend's own `billOrder()` computes the authoritative version independently (same formula, different codebase — frontend/backend already don't share a constants package, same reasoning `piecesPerSetFor`'s own duplication comment gives), and nothing computed client-side is ever trusted as the stored value.

**The dashboard's "Mark billed" button is reachable before a row's line-item detail has ever been fetched, which the live preview genuinely needs.** `dashboard/Orders.jsx` only lazily fetches full order detail (`qtySetsPacked`, `priceAtOrder`, product size shape) when a row is expanded (`toggleOrder`) — but "Mark billed" sits on the collapsed header itself and doesn't require expanding first. Extracted the existing fetch-if-not-cached logic out of `toggleOrder` into a shared `ensureDetail(orderId)`, called from both places, so clicking "Mark billed" on a never-expanded row still gets a real `preTaxAmount` to preview from. The confirm button stays disabled (`billDetailReady`) until that fetch resolves — showing a live preview computed from incomplete data, for a financial and irreversible action, would be worse than a brief "Loading order total…" state.

**Extended `ConfirmModal` with optional `children` and `confirmDisabled` props rather than building a second modal shape.** The task was explicit about this ("extend the existing confirm flow — don't build a new modal shape"). `children` renders between the existing `body` paragraph and the action buttons; every existing caller passes neither prop and is completely unaffected (`children` defaults to `undefined`, rendering nothing; `confirmDisabled` defaults to `false`). The CSS for a disabled confirm button (`.btn-modal-confirm:disabled`) already existed, unused for validation until now — added for "in flight" submitting states by an earlier task, reused verbatim rather than adding a second disabled treatment.

**A genuinely non-obvious ordering bug the validation had to prevent: an owner checking "Apply a discount?" and then pressing confirm before typing a percent.** `computeBillingAmounts` treats an empty-string percent as "not yet computable" (returns 0/`hasDiscount: false`), distinct from a real `0`, the same idle-vs-loaded discipline this project already applies to async status elsewhere — applied here to "a checked box with no real value yet." `billingInputIncomplete` (checked-but-empty on either question) disables the modal's confirm button on both screens, so the destructive, irreversible bill action can't fire on a half-answered question that would otherwise have silently sent `Number('') = 0`, a valid-looking discount of 0% nobody actually chose.

**Tested end-to-end with five real orders across all four discount/GST combinations, plus one deliberate cross-screen comparison.** Same article (RDX 6099, 6 real colors, 2 sets each at ₹675/piece, 4 pieces/set → ₹5,400 preTaxAmount per order) to keep the arithmetic checkable by hand: discount-only via mobile (₹540 off → ₹4,860, no GST fields populated), GST-only via dashboard (₹972 on the full ₹5,400 → ₹6,372), both via mobile AND an identical order billed via dashboard with the same 10%/18% inputs (₹540 off → ₹4,860, then 18% of ₹4,860 = ₹874.80 → ₹5,734.80 — confirmed byte-identical `actualPayable` between the two screens by direct Prisma comparison, not just eyeballing the UI), and neither (`actualPayable === preTaxAmount` exactly, ₹5,400). Confirmed no PIN prompt anywhere in either flow. All deducted stock restored via real compensating `STOCK_IN` transactions through the shared `applyStockMovement` utility (never a raw `Stock.qtySets` update), then the now-redundant cleanup transactions and the throwaway orders/probe account were deleted once the real qtySets values were confirmed back at their originals.

**Adding `productName` to both Low Stock screens meant editing `GET /api/stock`'s shared select, not a second endpoint.** Confirmed first (rather than assuming from the earlier "no `name` field" investigation was still current) that `stockController.js`'s `Product` select really did still omit `name` — {id, articleNo, factoryId, factory.name} only. Since both Low Stock screens, Live Stock, Transfer, Pack Order, and Bill Order all already share this one endpoint via `listStock()`, adding `name: true` to the existing select and flattening it to a new `productName` field on the response (same field name Pack/Bill/Ship Order already use for the identical "ArticleNo — Name" header, so no new naming convention introduced) covers every consumer in one additive change rather than adding a parallel per-screen fetch. Checked `Product`'s schema directly to confirm `name` sits as its own independent field, not nested under anything price-adjacent — `costPrice`/`sellingPrice` remain untouched and unselected, so no role-gating question was in play here.

### Repeated sizes in a set (M, L, L, XL) — a schema change that multiplies into every money figure in the app (2026-08-25)

**Chose `qty` on the existing row over allowing duplicate rows, which keeps the safety constraint that already existed.** The obvious way to represent "2 of size L" is to drop the `@@unique(productId, sizeLabel)` constraint and write two L rows. That would have worked, and it would have been wrong: that constraint exists specifically so an *accidental* duplicate can't silently inflate pieces-per-set, and dropping it to express a *deliberate* repeat would have thrown away the protection against the accident in order to permit the intent. A `qty` column on the single row expresses the intent explicitly while leaving the accident still unrepresentable at the database level. It also made the migration purely additive — one `ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN qty INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1` — where a constraint drop would have been a structural change to a table every money figure depends on.

**`SUM(qty)` and the old `COUNT(rows)` are provably identical for every pre-existing article, and that was verified rather than reasoned about.** `qty` defaults to 1, so the two formulas agree whenever nothing repeats — which is true of every article predating this change. That's an easy argument to make and a dangerous one to trust, because this number multiplies into every piece count, stock valuation, factory payable and order total in the system: a silent regression would show up as slightly-wrong money everywhere rather than as an error anywhere. So I snapshotted all 20 real articles' computed pieces-per-set *before* migrating, recomputed with the actually-shipped util *after*, and compared article by article — 20/20 identical, including the Kids article (6 → 6) and the 3-size and 2-size ones. Then a second, stronger check on the aggregate: the whole-system "pieces in stock" figure moved by exactly the amount attributable to the one new repeated-size article and no more, proving no other article's valuation shifted.

**`piecesPerSetFor` sums `(s.qty ?? 1)`, and the `?? 1` is load-bearing, not defensive noise.** Eleven separate Prisma selects across the backend feed this one function, and every one of them had to be updated to include `qty: true`. A bare `s.qty` on a select that forgot it yields `NaN` — and a single `NaN` doesn't corrupt one article, it poisons the entire aggregate it's summed into, turning a whole factory payable or dashboard KPI into `NaN`. Falling back to 1 instead degrades to exactly the pre-`qty` `COUNT` behaviour: wrong only for articles that actually repeat a size, and wrong in one visible place rather than catastrophically across a total. This is the same reasoning the function's existing `?? 0` for unrecognised Kids labels already used — for a money figure, prefer the failure mode that under-reports one row over the one that destroys the sum it belongs to.

**That safety net creates its own testing obligation, which changed how I verified.** Because a missed select now degrades *quietly* instead of exploding, "no errors and no `NaN` anywhere" is not evidence the selects are right — it's exactly what a missed select would also look like. So the verification had to be positive rather than negative: confirm the qty-bearing article is actually valued at 4 pieces/set at each surface, not merely that nothing broke. The Receive → Pack → Bill cycle is the sharpest version of this — Rs.4000 is correct, Rs.3000 is what a missed `qty` would have produced, and those are distinguishable numbers rather than an absence of symptoms.

**Grepping for computation sites found one the task description didn't list, and it was already broken.** The task named the likely places but warned they might not be exhaustive. Grepping `sizes.length` (rather than only `piecesPerSetFor`) turned up `LiveStock.jsx:106` computing `piecesPerSet: product.sizes.length` directly, bypassing the shared util entirely — the one site the 2026-08-20 consolidation missed. It would have ignored `qty`, but more importantly it was **already wrong before this task**: a Kids article stores exactly one `ProductSize` row, so Live Stock has been reporting every Kids article at 1 piece per set instead of its real 4/5/6. Fixed by routing it through `piecesPerSetFor` like every other caller. Worth noting the search that found it: grepping for the *util's name* would have missed it by construction, since the bug is precisely that it doesn't call the util — the productive grep was for the raw expression the util was meant to replace.

**Verified the "Orders need no changes" assumption by running it, not by asserting it.** The task's premise was that Orders only ever deal in whole sets, so redefining what a set contains should require zero Order-side changes. That's the kind of claim that's easy to state and expensive to be wrong about, so I ran a full real cycle on a genuinely repeated-size article: received 3 sets, ordered 2, packed 2, billed with 10% discount + 18% GST. Every stage independently landed on the 4-pieces-per-set figure — `listOrders.totalValue` Rs.4000, `preTaxAmount` Rs.4000, `finalAmount` Rs.3600, `actualPayable` Rs.4248 — against Rs.3000/Rs.2700/Rs.3186 if any stage had still been counting rows. The assumption held, and `git diff --stat` confirms no order-side controller logic was touched to make it hold.


### Screens showed pre-tax money because rule 101 added a new source of truth and nothing was pointed at it (2026-08-25)

**The bug's shape is worth naming: nothing was broken, something was merely never connected.** Rule 101 (the same day) added `Order.actualPayable` — a real, server-computed, already-correct snapshot of what a party owes. Every calculation in the app kept working exactly as before, no test failed, no error appeared anywhere — and every display site kept rendering the pre-tax line-item sum, because none of them had any reason to know a better answer now existed. SAI's Amount Due read ₹99,675 against a true ₹1,02,735. This is the failure mode of adding a source of truth without auditing its consumers, and it is completely silent by construction: the old code is still *correct code*, it's just no longer the *authoritative* answer.

**Fixed at the two producers, not the six screens.** Every list-view rupee figure in the app — Owner Dashboard Orders, Party Payables' per-order list, and the mobile Pack/Bill/Ship Order lists — reads one field, `GET /api/orders`'s `totalValue`. Every aggregate — Party Payables' `totalBilled`/`amountDue`, the Overview revenue KPI, the per-party sales summary — flows through one function, `computeRevenue`. Correcting those two, rather than patching each screen, means no consumer can drift back onto the pre-tax number later, and a screen added next month inherits the right behaviour without knowing this rule exists.

**The single most important property: it goes DOWN as well as up.** The obvious mental model — "billed orders need GST added" — is wrong, and an implementation built on it would pass the headline test and still be broken. Order `cmt4gw3xa002lbtfocfyvoeyy` carries a 5% discount and 5% GST: pre-tax ₹18,200, real `actualPayable` ₹18,154.50. Applying the discount and GST in the right order yields *less* than the pre-tax figure. This is why the rule is "prefer the real stored value", never "recompute a tax on top", and why both directions were verified on screen rather than just the one the bug report led with.

**Made the decision per-order via a shared helper, because a single view legitimately mixes both kinds.** `orderValueOf(order, fallback)` takes the fallback as a *value*, deliberately not computing it — the two callers' fallbacks are genuinely different calculations, both already correct for the orders that need them, and this function's only job is deciding which source wins. SAI's payables list is the proof it has to be per-order: ₹64,260 (snapshot) + ₹29,025 (fallback) + ₹9,450 (fallback) sit in one list and sum to one aggregate, and any per-screen or per-endpoint toggle would have to get all three wrong together to be consistent.

**Selecting the column is load-bearing, and forgetting it fails silently.** `orderValueOf` reads `order.actualPayable`; if a Prisma select omits it, the field is `undefined`, the helper falls back forever, and the aggregate stays quietly pre-tax with nothing at all to indicate a problem — the exact bug being fixed, reintroduced by omission. Both call sites therefore needed `actualPayable: true` added to their selects, and both got a comment saying why, since the failure has no symptom to trace back from. Same family as the `?? 1` reasoning in the previous entry: a graceful fallback is right, but it converts a loud failure into a silent one, so the thing it guards must be verified positively rather than by absence of errors.

**Fixing the total created an obligation to show the arithmetic.** `lineValue()` in the dashboard's expanded order row carried a comment stating its own invariant — that a line's value and the order's total share one basis, "rather than mixing two different bases on one screen." A snapshot-based header broke exactly that: ₹64,260 above lines summing to ₹61,200. Rescaling each line would invent per-line numbers that were never stored; reverting the header would restore the bug. So the expanded row now renders the real stored breakdown (pre-tax → discount → GST → amount billed) only when a snapshot exists, which makes the header's figure explained rather than contradicted, and leaves snapshot-free orders rendering nothing new and summing exactly as before.

**Two exemptions confirmed structurally, not assumed.** The Overview "open orders value" KPI queries only `PLACED`/`PACKED` — statuses that exist *before* billing — so every order it can reach has a null snapshot by construction; adding the preference would select a guaranteed-null column and imply billed orders belong in that KPI. Per-location revenue works at `Transaction` level because location lives on `Stock`, never on `Order`, so a whole-order `actualPayable` cannot be attributed to a location without inventing an allocation rule for discount and GST — a real design decision, deliberately not smuggled in under a display fix. Factory Payables was checked directly rather than waved off: it reads `Transaction.costPriceSnapshot` on `STOCK_IN` rows and never touches `Order` at all, so a customer-side selling figure is structurally irrelevant to it.

**The task's own expected total didn't match its own components, and the components were right.** It asked to confirm ₹1,02,675 "(₹64,260 + ₹29,025 + ₹9,450)" — but those three add to ₹1,02,735. The pre-fix figure it quoted (₹99,675) matched the live database exactly, which is what established the components were trustworthy and the stated sum was a typo rather than a sign my reading of the data was wrong: ₹99,675 − ₹61,200 + ₹64,260 = ₹1,02,735. Worth flagging rather than quietly matching the stated total, since "make the number equal the expected value" would have meant breaking a correct calculation to satisfy an arithmetic slip.

**Capped `gstPercent` at 0–5, and checked the existing data before assuming a ceiling was safe to add (2026-08-26).** `billOrder()` validated `gstPercent >= 0` with no upper bound, so a mistyped `18` on a ₹61,200 order would have been accepted and silently overcharged by roughly ₹8,000 — the kind of slip that's invisible precisely because the arithmetic is correct for the number entered. Before adding the ceiling I queried for any already-billed order with `gstPercent > 5`: none exist (both real billed orders are at exactly 5%), so no historical snapshot is retroactively contradicted by the new bound. That check mattered because rule 101 snapshots are written once and never recomputed — a validation change can't corrupt them, but it *could* have left stored data that the current rules would now reject, which is worth knowing about rather than discovering later.
