**Verified the rejections without touching production data, by reading the control flow rather than trusting it.** `billOrder()` validates the percent bounds *before* it looks the order up, which means an invalid `gstPercent` cannot reach any database write regardless of which order is targeted. Confirming that in the controller let me run every rejection case against a deliberately non-existent order id — provably write-free — instead of billing a real order and trying to unwind it. The two accepted boundary values (0 and 5) do perform a real billing with real stock deduction, so those ran against a throwaway order and had their stock restored through the shared `applyStockMovement` helper as a genuine compensating `STOCK_IN`, never a raw `Stock.qtySets` update. A `gstPercent: 5` request against the same fake id returning `404 ORDER_NOT_FOUND` rather than `400` is what proves the rejections came from the new cap and not from the bad id — without that control the whole rejection suite would have passed for the wrong reason.

**Lowered the low-stock threshold from ≤2 to ≤1 sets (2026-08-26), and grepped for a second copy before touching only the obvious one.** The task named `frontend/src/utils/lowStock.js` as the known constant, but this system has a well-established pattern (`utils/piecesPerSet.js`) of the same domain rule being duplicated once per codebase since frontend and backend share no constants package — so I checked for that pattern here too rather than assuming this rule was the exception. It was: `dashboardController.js` keeps its own `LOW_STOCK_THRESHOLD = 2`, used both to filter the Overview KPI's count query and to report `lowStockThreshold` in the API response. Both were changed together, each now cross-referencing the other in its own comment so a future change to just one is harder to do by accident.

**Grepped for hardcoded numeric thresholds before trusting that the constant was the only path.** Searched for any `qtySets`/`totalStock` comparison against a bare `2`, and any Prisma `lte`/`gte: 2` — none exist; every low-stock check in the codebase already goes through one of the two named constants. This matters because a hardcoded copy would have silently kept the old threshold on whatever screen held it, the exact kind of drift rule 56 was written to prevent in the first place.

**Found and fixed a grammar bug the number change exposed, rather than assuming display copy was safe just because it was correct before.** The Overview KPI's subtitle interpolated the threshold directly into hardcoded plural text — `lines at or below {threshold} sets`. That read correctly at 2 and reads as "1 sets" at 1. Not something the task asked for, but it's a direct, visible consequence of the exact change requested, caught by actually looking at the rendered page rather than assuming a copy string with a variable in it is immune to the variable's own value changing category (plural to singular).

**Made factory-level collapse consistent across Live Stock and both Low Stock screens (2026-08-26), starting from an audit rather than the assumption baked into the task description.** The task only had one confirmed fact per screen — Live Stock has factory collapse, the Owner Dashboard's Low Stock page doesn't — and explicitly flagged the staff-facing Low Stock List's state as unconfirmed. Reading all three files before touching anything found that `LowStockList.jsx` was in the *same* missing-collapse state as the dashboard version, not the same state as Live Stock: both Low Stock screens' own header comments explicitly documented "factory heading, plain and non-collapsible" as a deliberate prior decision (reasoning: a factory rarely has more than a handful of already-filtered low articles). That comment is evidence of a real design call, not an oversight — but the task's own instruction to make the layer consistent everywhere means that call is being deliberately reversed now, which is exactly why it's called out here rather than silently overwritten.

**Reused Live Stock's exact factory-accordion shape rather than inventing a Low-Stock-specific one, including for the collapsed summary line.** Live Stock's collapsed factory header shows article count + sets + pieces + a low-count badge; neither Low Stock screen has "pieces" as a meaningful figure (every row is already pre-filtered to low stock), so the direct copy would have been wrong. Instead of dropping the badge or inventing new copy, the same "count + combined total across the group" idea already used one level down for each Low Stock screen's own *article* header (colour count + combined low-set total) was lifted up one level to the factory header (article count + combined low-set total across the whole factory) — consistent with both the sibling screen (Live Stock) and the existing convention on the very page being changed.

**Deleted `.dash-lowstock-group`/`.dash-lowstock-group-heading` from index.css instead of leaving them stranded.** Both classes existed only to render the old plain, non-interactive factory heading being replaced. Grepped for other usages first (there were none — both Low Stock screens were the only callers) before removing, rather than assuming they were dead and finding out later they weren't.

**Made History actor-scoped by role (rule 104, 2026-08-26), and chose a live join over a snapshot — the deliberate exception to this project's own strongest convention.** Every other "must stay historically accurate" value in this codebase is snapshotted at action time (`priceAtOrder`, `costPriceSnapshot`, rule 101's billing amounts), and the reflex here would have been to add a `actorRoleAtAction` column to all seven source tables. That would have been wrong, and the reason is specific rather than a matter of taste: those fields are snapshotted **because they are editable later**, so a live read would let a present-day edit rewrite the past. `User.role` is not editable. I verified that rather than assuming it, by reading every write to an existing `User` row in the codebase — there are exactly eight (`deactivate`/`reactivate` write `isActive`; `updateOwnPin` and `requirePin`'s two lockout writes touch PIN fields; `resetUserPassword` writes `passwordHash`; `updateUser` destructures only `{name, username}` from the body so a `role` key in a request is silently ignored; `seed.js` backfills `priceEditPinHash`/`isPrimaryOwner`) — and `role` appears in a `data` payload **only** inside `prisma.user.create`. Since role is write-once at creation and users are never hard-deleted (only `isActive`-deactivated, so an actor always resolves), role-at-action-time and role-now are necessarily the same value. A snapshot column would have been a second copy of an immutable fact whose only possible behaviour is to drift wrong.

**That finding is also what made the task's "flag how existing entries should be handled" question dissolve rather than need answering.** The task anticipated a backfill dilemma — default old entries to visible-to-both, or hidden-from-staff? — which only exists if role has to be reconstructed after the fact. Because the join resolves correctly for every historical row already in the database, there is no class of entry with unknown provenance, so there is nothing to default. Worth stating explicitly rather than silently not backfilling, since "no backfill" looks identical to "forgot to backfill" from the outside.

**Scoped by ACTOR, never by entry type, and tested precisely that distinction.** The tempting shortcut is to hide entry *types* that feel owner-ish (corrections, billing). That's wrong: a receipt recorded by a staff member is floor activity staff should see, and the same receipt recorded by the owner is not. The sharpest available test is therefore two receipts created through the *same endpoint* minutes apart, differing only in who called it — if the filter keyed on type, both would behave identically. Staff saw the staff-performed one and not the owner-performed one, which no type-based implementation could produce. The two correction types do end up invisible to staff, but as a *consequence* of their routes being OWNER-gated, not as a rule of their own.

**Filtered in all seven queries AND added a fail-closed backstop, and the allowlist-vs-denylist choice there is the load-bearing part.** The `where` clauses are the real enforcement (owner rows are never fetched for a staff request). The backstop exists because that enforcement is spread across seven separate queries and the realistic failure is adding an eighth source later and forgetting one. It filters `actorRole === 'STAFF'` (allowlist) rather than `!== 'OWNER'` (denylist) — the two differ in exactly the case that matters: a new source whose actor `select` omitted `role` yields `actorRole: undefined`, which a denylist treats as safe and **shows to staff**, the precise leak the backstop is there to prevent. An allowlist turns that same mistake into a missing entry instead of a disclosure.

**Added a Factory Payables entry point to the Owner Dashboard sidebar (2026-08-26), and the investigation changed what "wrap it in the shell" had to mean.** Factory Payables lived only at `/factory-payables` — an OWNER-gated route *outside* the `/dashboard` shell — so an owner working in the dashboard had no way to reach it at all. At 1440x900 it measured 480px wide with 480px of dead space either side, drew its own `ScreenHeader` whose back arrow points at `/`, and had no sidebar: not *broken* (no overflow, no console errors, legible), but unmistakably a mobile screen someone had navigated away from the app to reach.

**The decisive finding was that only the OUTER WRAPPER conflicts with the shell, not the content.** Enumerating every CSS class the populated screen actually renders showed them all to be shared app-wide vocabulary — `.stat-row` (flex, `flex: 1` children), `.stat-hero` (block), `.card`, `.payment-history-row`, `.sticky-action-bar` (`position: sticky`) — every one of which already sizes off its container. The narrow column was `.page`'s `max-width: 480px`, nothing intrinsic to the content. That is what made this a small wrapping fix rather than the substantial rework the task said to stop and report: swap the wrapper, touch nothing inside. Had the content itself carried fixed widths or mobile-only positioning, the honest answer would have been that the entry point and the redesign cannot be cleanly separated.

**Chose one component with an `inDashboard` prop over a second `pages/dashboard/FactoryPayables.jsx`, deliberately against the local precedent.** Article Pricing, Parties and Locations each have a separate purpose-built dashboard copy, so a second copy was the "consistent" move. It was the wrong one here: those pages were *rewritten* for the dashboard (different layouts, `dash-*` classes, genuinely different interactions), whereas Factory Payables needs the identical behaviour in both places — the same payable calculation, the same per-request PIN on every write, the same edit/delete flows. A 691-line duplicate would be two places to fix every future bug, and writing it *is* the deferred redesign the task explicitly scoped out. The prop defaults to `false`, so the existing mobile route keeps working untouched — verified, not assumed.

**Appended the nav item rather than slotting it beside the other money page.** Factory Payables sits next to Article Pricing thematically, which argued for inserting it mid-list. The nav's own comments overrule that: both Locations and Article Pricing record "append beyond the original list, don't renumber it," explicitly to avoid reshuffling positions someone already has muscle memory for. Appending happens to land it adjacent to Article Pricing anyway, and after the operational pages (Orders, Low stock, Parties, History) an owner opens far more often — so the convention and the thematic argument agreed once the convention was followed.

**Confirmed the two PIN mechanisms are genuinely different rather than assuming one unlock covers both.** The dashboard's lock is a *screen* unlock (`POST /api/users/me/verify-pin`, issues no token, grants no access). Factory Payables sends `pin` in the body of every mutating request, enforced server-side by `requirePin` on POST/PATCH/DELETE. So unlocking the dashboard correctly does *not* satisfy a write, and Record payment still prompts for its own PIN inside the shell — not a double-prompt bug but two different gates doing two different jobs. There is no double-prompt for *viewing*, which is the case that would have been annoying.

**Flagged rather than fixed: the content is now full-width and looks stretched.** At 1222px the history rows put Edit/Delete a long way from the date they belong to, and the stat cards are wider than their content needs. That is real and worth fixing — with a `max-width` on the content or a genuine two-column layout — but it is exactly the deferred UX pass, so it was left alone.

**Added article-level grouping to the dashboard Orders page's expanded line view (2026-08-26), and the investigation overturned half the task's own premise.** The task named two places this rendering supposedly lived — the Orders page and Party Payables' "Orders and bills" — and asked which one component covered both. Reading `dashboard/Parties.jsx` directly showed its order list has no expand affordance at all: no click handler, no `expandedOrder` state, and it calls `listOrders()` only, never `getOrder(id)` — there is no line-item detail to group because none is ever fetched. So the honest answer wasn't "one shared component" or "two separate ones," it was "only one of the two named places actually has the feature being asked about." Reporting that finding before touching code (rather than silently building only in Orders.jsx and letting the summary imply both were checked) is what made it a defensible answer instead of a guess that happened to be right.

**Matched two different established conventions at two different levels, and kept them from colliding.** BillOrderDetail.jsx supplied the GROUPING shape (article header with a nested colour-row body, `accordion-section nested`/`accordion-header nested`). Transfer.jsx's `article.rows.length === 1` check (skip the accordion wrapper entirely when there's nothing to disambiguate) supplied the COLLAPSE-SKIPPING rule — but Transfer applies that rule one level down (per-article, based on colour count), while this task needed it one level up (per-order, based on article count). Applying it correctly meant checking `groups.length <= 1` — the count of DISTINCT ARTICLES on the order — not reusing any per-article line count from either reference screen. Getting the level right here was the actual work; the accordion markup itself is a direct copy.

**Verified byte-identical output for the single-article case by keeping its exact code path alive, not by eyeballing before/after screenshots.** The single-article branch calls the exact same `renderLine(li, { showArticle: true })` the pre-existing flat map called, with `showArticle` always `true` in that branch — so SAI's real order (9122, six colours) runs through literally unchanged markup. This was worth stating explicitly because "looks the same in a screenshot" is a weaker claim than "is running the same code," and the task's own wording ("today's real SAI example... should look exactly as clean as it does now") was checking for a regression, not just a resemblance.

**Chose a composite-string key (`${orderId}:${productId}`) for the new per-order article-accordion state over a nested `{ [orderId]: Set }` structure.** Every other multi-open accordion state in this app (Live Stock's `expandedFactories`/`expandedArticles`, Bill/Pack Order's `expandedArticles`) is a single flat `Set` of ids. A nested structure would have been a second shape for the same idea, and would need its own update logic (immutably updating a Set nested inside an object, rather than just the object itself) for no real benefit — a composite string key preserves the existing single-`Set` convention exactly, since two different orders sharing a productId is precisely what the composite key exists to distinguish.

**Confirmed the "no arithmetic drift" requirement wasn't just assumed to hold because nothing LOOKED different — checked it by reading two independently-summed numbers back out of the live DOM and comparing them.** The task called this out specifically (verify the footer explicitly, don't assume grouping rows doesn't affect the sum). Since the footer for the throwaway multi-article order was empty (a PLACED order has no `actualPayable` snapshot yet — the footer only ever renders for a billed order), the equivalent live check was: sum every per-article header's `formatCurrency(group.total)` and sum every individual line's own displayed value, and confirm they're the same number (₹8,650 both ways). Both totals still ultimately come from the same untouched `lineValue()` calls the pre-existing code always made — this only proves grouping didn't introduce a second, diverging sum, not that `lineValue()` itself is correct (which was never in question here). For SAI's already-billed order, the real footer (`Pre-tax total ₹61,200 · GST +₹3,060 · Amount billed ₹64,260`) was compared directly against its pre-existing values instead, since that order's footer reads from server-stored fields untouched by this change.

**Found a real, pre-existing, out-of-scope bug while picking a multi-article test order, and worked around it instead of fixing it.** A genuine 3-article, 13-line, cancelled order (SAI, ~Aug 17) never appeared on the Orders page at all — not a symptom of this change. `Order` has no `cancelledAt` field in `schema.prisma`, yet `bucketDateOf()` reads `order.cancelledAt` for any cancelled order, which is always `undefined`; that feeds `monthKeyOf(undefined)` and the resulting bucket matches no selectable month, so the order is silently unreachable via the month picker forever. Confirmed by direct Prisma query (Prisma itself refused to `select` a field that doesn't exist, which is what surfaced this) rather than guessing why the order wasn't showing up. This is real and worth fixing on its own, but it is unrelated to line-item grouping — noted here rather than fixed, and a throwaway order created through the real API stood in for testing instead.

**Restructured the Owner Dashboard's Article Pricing table into Factory→Article accordion groups (2026-08-26) — and the investigation showed mobile needed the opposite of "make it match."** The task's own step 1 asked whether full parity was actually right or whether mobile needed a simpler treatment. Reading `pages/ArticlePricing.jsx` showed it's already factory-SCOPED, not factory-flat: a `<select>` picks one Factory and the fetch itself (`listProducts({ factoryId })`) is filtered to just that factory, so at any moment there is exactly one factory's worth of articles on screen — there is no cross-factory list to group in the first place. Adding an accordion on top of an already-single-factory list would be a wrapper around one item, which is precisely the case this app's own established convention (Transfer's single-colour articles, Live Stock's single-location articles) says gets no wrapper at all. So the honest finding wasn't "mobile needs a simplified version of the grouping" — it was "mobile already achieves the goal the grouping exists for, via a different, already-established, equally-valid mechanism," and needed zero changes. Confirmed by testing it directly afterward rather than just reasoning about it: mobile's RDX view still shows a flat table with no accordion, and its own existing pending-first/ascending-articleNo sort was already correct and untouched.

**Removed the dashboard's arbitrary per-column sort entirely, rather than layering grouping on top of it, and flagged the trade-off rather than silently dropping a feature.** The pre-existing dashboard table let an owner click any column header (Article No/Name/Factory/Cost/Selling/Margin, either direction) to resort the whole flat list — a deliberate design choice its own header comment defended explicitly ("a desktop owner wants... one sortable table, filtering by eye rather than by a forced picker"). The task's spec was a complete, fixed ordering on its own (Factory alphabetical, then pending-first/ascending-articleNo within each group) and explicitly said to reuse the shared accordion pattern "rather than inventing a new grouping mechanism" — no sibling screen (Low Stock, Live Stock, Transfer) combines a fixed hierarchical grouping with free per-column sort, and building a hybrid here would itself have been the "new mechanism" the instructions ruled out. Rather than deciding this silently, it was named directly in the pre-build report (sortability being traded for structural consistency) so it's a visible, reversible decision rather than a quietly lost capability discovered later.

**Decided pending articles sort to the top of their OWN factory group, not the top of the whole table — matching the task's own reasoning rather than the original global-pending-first design.** Before restructuring, the ONE thing the flat table did that resembled grouping was a global pending-first sort, surfacing every stuck-pending article at the very top regardless of factory (rule 8/71's whole reason for existing). Keeping that exact behavior post-restructuring would have scattered pending articles above the fold while their own factory's OTHER articles sat buried inside a collapsed section below — actively fighting the reason grouping was requested in the first place (a coherent, scannable per-factory article list). Scoping pending-first to within each group keeps every factory's articles together as one coherent unit while still surfacing pending status immediately via a collapsed-header badge (`"2 pending"`), the same "useful summary without expanding" convention Live Stock's own low-stock badge already establishes for its factory layer — so the discoverability rule 8/71 depends on survives collapsing-by-default, it just moved from "always at the literal top of the page" to "visible on every factory's own header."

**Verified "no calculation logic changes" by comparing rendered figures against the raw API response, not by eyeballing that the numbers looked plausible.** Every Cost/Selling/Margin cell for a real factory's articles was captured from the live DOM and checked against `GET /api/products`' own `costPrice`/`sellingPrice` values (formatted through the exact same `formatCurrency`), for both the pending and non-pending branches. This is a stronger claim than "the screenshot looks right" — it confirms the restructuring touched only which DOM node a given `<td>` lives inside, never the arithmetic feeding it, addressing the task's own explicit "verify this explicitly, not just assume grouping doesn't affect the sum" instruction.

**Converted Transfer's colour selection to New Order's chip+stepper+"Add selected" pattern (2026-08-26) — but deliberately did NOT extend it to the search-results list, and named that scope boundary explicitly rather than silently leaving one mechanism half-converted.** The task's own wording scopes the new mechanism to "within an expanded Article" — the search-results list is a flat list spanning any number of DIFFERENT Articles at once (deliberately, so a search stays a faster path to one specific known item than picking a Factory first would be), so there's no single Article to hang one shared "Add selected" button off. New Order's own precedent agrees: its chip mechanism is scoped to one resolved product, and New Order has no cross-article flat-search mode to compare against at all. Rather than forcing artificial per-article grouping onto a flat cross-article list just to make "everything uses chips" literally true, search keeps the pre-existing one-at-a-time `selectedRow`/`handleSelectRow`/"+ Add to transfer" mechanism completely unchanged, and the new `checkedColors`/chip mechanism applies only to the Factory→Article browsing hierarchy. Two mechanisms now coexist on one screen, on purpose — each maps onto a genuinely different real workflow (find one specific known item fast, vs. browse one Article and move several of its colours at once), not an accidental inconsistency.

**Copied New Order's chip+stepper shape but NOT its uncapped stepper — investigated the absence directly rather than assuming it was safe to reuse wholesale.** Read `NewOrder.jsx`'s `setColorSets` directly: it clamps only at 0, and its `+` button carries no `disabled` bound at all — a deliberate design there, since rule 64 explicitly allows ordering more than what's currently in stock (packing later absorbs any shortfall). Transfer moves REAL physical stock between two real locations right now, not a future order commitment, so copying that absence would have let a batch-staged line request more sets than a location actually holds. The new `setColorChipSets(bundleId, sets, max)` clamps on both ends using `remainingForRow(row)` — the exact same cap function Transfer's own pre-existing single-select stepper already used, reused rather than reinvented. The real enforcement stays where it already was and is completely untouched: `createTransfer`'s atomic guarded `updateMany({ where: { qtySets: { gte: qtySets } } } })` in `transferController.js`, which this task never touched — the frontend cap is confirmed to be a UX improvement on the happy path, not a replacement for that check.

**`checkedColors` is keyed by `bundleId`, not `colorId` — a small deviation from New Order's own naming that follows directly from this screen's own established selection unit.** This file's own top-of-file note already explains why a Transfer's unit of selection is a whole Stock row (Product + Colour + source Location + qtySets > 0 all at once), not an article-then-colour cascade the way Receive Stock or New Order work. `bundleId` is what that unit already keys on everywhere else on this screen (`selectedRow.bundleId`, `remainingForRow(row)`, every staged line) — reusing `colorId` instead would have meant either introducing a second identifier for the exact same row or silently allowing the same colour to collide across what should be independently-selectable Stock rows. Naming the new state after this screen's own existing vocabulary, not New Order's, was the correct call even while directly copying New Order's interaction shape.

**Reset `checkedColors` on both a Factory switch and a From-Location switch, using the exact same "in-progress selection, not staging" reasoning already written for `selectedRow`/`qtySets`.** Both existing handlers already carried a comment explaining why a Factory or Location switch invalidates whatever was being actively configured but must never touch `stagedLines` (a staged line is tied to the Location it was picked from, not to what's currently being browsed). Extending that exact reasoning to `checkedColors` — rather than writing a new justification from scratch — is what makes it verifiable that the new state got the same treatment for the same reason, not a different, possibly inconsistent one arrived at independently.

**Restructured Manage Parties to list-first (2026-08-26) — investigation found the real work had already been done in an earlier session, leaving only a naming decision.** Reading `pages/Parties.jsx` before touching anything showed the create form is already collapsed behind an accordion, closed by default (`createOpen` starts `false`), with the party list rendering immediately below it — exactly the "list-first, form-on-demand" shape the task asked for, built in two earlier commits (`ddb1a05`, `16d423b`). Archive/Reactivate already existed per row, wired to the confirmed `PATCH /:id/deactivate`/`/:id/reactivate` endpoints, and "Show archived" already provided the reactivation path (same mechanism as Category/ReceiveStock and Article Pricing's own archive view). So the only genuine deltas were: renaming the accordion header from "Create party" to "Add new party" to match the task's own wording, and deciding how to handle "Delete."

**Chose ONE relabeled button ("Delete") over two buttons doing the identical thing, using the judgment the task explicitly granted.** Aadi's own clarification confirmed "Delete" is the exact same deactivate action as "Archive," not a new hard-delete (Party rows are permanently referenced by Order/PartyStockReturn, same audit-trail principle as `User.isActive`). Showing "Archive" and "Delete" side by side as two separate buttons would have given a real person no way to tell them apart or reason about which to press — pure UI noise for zero behavioral difference. Relabeling the single existing button to "Delete" (same handler, same `action: 'archive'` internal value, same ConfirmModal path) gets the requested vocabulary without inventing a fake distinction. "Show archived" and the "Archived" badge were deliberately left unrenamed — they describe the party's *state*, "Delete" describes the *action* that produces it, the same delete→trash→restore split most apps already use (e.g. Gmail), so the mismatch in wording is not actually inconsistent.

**Added colour-coded avatars to History (2026-08-26) — investigation found the task's own premise about the existing mechanism was wrong, and said so rather than silently building something different from what was asked.** The task described the Parties page avatar as deriving "a colour per name" (e.g. "blue 'A' for Ashiyana") and asked to reuse that exact derivation. Reading `dashboard/Parties.jsx`'s `.dash-party-avatar` and `DashboardLayout.jsx`'s `.dash-rail-logo` (the only two avatar-shaped things anywhere in this codebase — confirmed by grepping the whole frontend for `avatar`/`initials`/`hash`/`palette`) showed both use one single fixed pair of colours, `var(--accent-bg)`/`var(--accent-text)` (a blue, `#E8F0FC`/`#1F5AA6`), for every name alike — differentiated only by which initials render inside, never by which colour. There is no hash-into-a-palette mechanism anywhere to copy. This actually made "blue 'A' for Ashiyana" a completely accurate description of the ONE real pattern (a blue tile, showing whichever initial), not evidence of per-name colour variance — so reusing that exact mechanism verbatim (same tokens, same shape, same initials function) satisfies the letter of the task even though "colour-coded" reads as more varied than what genuinely exists. Extracted the previously-twice-duplicated `initialsOf` helper into `utils/avatar.js` for this third and fourth caller (History mobile + dashboard) rather than copying it a third time, with the file's own header comment stating plainly that no colour-hash exists and none was invented, so a future reader doesn't assume one was silently added.

**Gave each History actor a genuinely distinct colour (2026-08-26, the direct follow-up) — found a 9-entry design-token palette already existed, one this app had already precedent for reusing decoratively rather than semantically.** Grepping `index.css`'s `:root` block for colour tokens (not just the 5 badge roles the previous task already knew about) turned up 4 more: `--teal-*`/`--rust-*`/`--lavender-*`/`--indigo-*`, added on 2026-08-22 specifically for the Home tile grid once the original 5-role palette ran out of tones. That same block's own comments record the exact question this task needed answered: whether reusing a status-named token (Danger, Warning, Success) for a non-status purpose reads as confusing — and the app's own prior answer was no, Bill Orders' Home tile already uses Danger purely as a distinguishing hue, not an error signal. That precedent is why all 9 tokens went into `AVATAR_PALETTE` rather than a hand-picked subset avoiding the "loaded" ones: the app had already established that these tokens are safe to reuse decoratively once their bg/text pair is applied somewhere that isn't itself a status indicator, and a History avatar (next to a name) is exactly that, distinct from the type badge (Placed/Packed/Shipped/etc.) that sits above it on the same row. 9 tokens also clears the task's 6-8 minimum with margin, at zero new CSS colours.

**Added `actorId` to every History entry after confirming it wouldn't touch rule 104's filtering — a small, mechanical, purely-additive backend change, exactly as the task allowed.** The task said to add an id only if doing so wouldn't require touching rule 104's actor-role filtering logic, and to stop and report otherwise. Reading `historyController.js` showed the seven source queries already select `{ name: true, role: true }` on every actor relation and build `actorName`/`actorRole` from it — `id` was never selected, but every actor relation already resolves to a real `User` row (users are never hard-deleted, per that file's own header comment), so adding `id: true` alongside the two already-selected fields, and one `actorId: x.relation.id` line alongside each existing `actorName`/`actorRole` pair, touches neither `actorScope()` (the function that builds each query's `where` clause) nor the rule-104 backstop filter (`entries.filter((e) => e.actorRole === 'STAFF')`) — both are demonstrably unchanged in the diff. This is the same "select one more field, change zero logic" shape as `costPrice` never being selected for STAFF elsewhere in this app: adding data to what's already fetched is a different, much smaller kind of change than adding or altering a `where` condition.

**Hashed on `actorId`, not `actorName`, per the task's own stated reasoning — verified rather than assumed to matter.** The task flagged that two different real people could theoretically share an identical name (this app's `User.name` has no uniqueness constraint, only `username` does) and asked to prefer a real id when available. Since `actorId` turned out to be cheap to add (previous paragraph), the hash function takes `actorId` as its primary key with `actorName` as a defensive-only fallback for an entry that somehow lacks one — never the reverse — so two employees who happen to share a name are guaranteed different colours rather than colliding by construction.

**Replaced the 9-slot avatar palette with a continuous hue (2026-08-26, same day, reported by Aadi as "Aaditya and Ram colours are clashing") — measured the real collision on real data before changing anything, rather than guessing at a fix.** Computed the existing hash for every real active `User.id` directly (a throwaway script, not assumed from the code) and confirmed "Ram" and "Aaditya Arora" landed in the identical one of 9 buckets — with "Aaditya"/"Harsh Arora" also colliding in a different bucket. This is the birthday paradox, not a flaw in the hash itself: cramming much more than 3-4 people into only 9 buckets makes at least one collision the LIKELY outcome, so a bigger fixed palette (say 12 or 15 slots, still built from named tokens) would only have bought a little headroom before hitting the same wall again as this business's headcount grows. The fix changes what the hash produces — a hue angle (0-359°) instead of an index into a short list — which gives roughly 40x the effective distinguishable space of the 9-token version while keeping the same visual register (light background, dark text of the same hue, matching e.g. `--accent-bg`/`--accent-text`'s own relationship) via fixed HSL saturation/lightness constants. Verified the fix against the same real ids that had just collided (Aaditya Arora -> 349°, Ram -> 304°, a full 45° apart) before considering it done, not just re-running the same test and trusting a different-looking output.

**Abandoned hashing entirely for a hand-curated name -> colour map, on Aadi's own explicit instruction, after the hue-based fix (previous paragraph) was itself judged insufficient.** Two independent hash designs — a 9-slot discrete palette, then a 360-value continuous hue — were each built to solve "make colours consistent per actor," and each one measurably failed at the actual goal underneath that ("make colours a human reads as clearly different"), because neither could know in advance that two hash outputs would land close enough to look alike to an eye rather than to a modulus. Aadi's instruction named this directly: don't hash or derive this at all, assign it by hand. The resulting map (`ACTOR_BADGE_COLORS` in `utils/avatar.js`) trades the two prior versions' "works for any actor, including ones nobody's thought about yet" for "correct for exactly the four people someone actually looked at and chose colours for" — a real reduction in generality, made deliberately, with a fail-closed neutral badge (reusing `--section-fill`/`--text-secondary`, not one of the four assigned tones) for anyone not on the list, so an unmapped person renders as visibly "not yet assigned" rather than silently borrowing someone else's colour.

**Verified "Harsh Arora" renders indigo without being able to see it in a real History row — that account has zero real activity yet.** Testing all four confirmed names, three appeared naturally in the live feed (Aaditya Arora, Ram, Mukesh Kumar Yadav) but Harsh Arora did not; querying the database directly confirmed why — 0 `Transaction` rows and 0 `Order` rows for that real, active `harsh123` account, not a rendering bug. Rather than fabricating a fake business event against a real production account just to get a screenshot (or guessing at their password to log in and create one legitimately), verified the colour-assignment function directly and non-invasively: Vite's dev server serves every source file as a real importable ES module, so `page.evaluate(() => import('/src/utils/avatar.js'))` in a Playwright browser context calls the exact shipped function with zero app state required, confirming `actorBadgeColorFor('Harsh Arora')` returns the indigo token pair. This is the same guarantee a live screenshot would have given for a pure, stateless function — the only thing a live entry would add is proof the CSS renders it correctly in situ, which the other three names' real screenshots already established, since all four go through the identical badge markup.

**Changed Ship Order's tile from Teal to a new Olive token (2026-08-27) — measured the suggested starting hex against Warning before using it, rather than eyeballing "looks different enough."** The task's own suggested values (border `#C8C278`/text `#6B6220`) were a starting point with an explicit instruction to verify and adjust. Running both that suggestion and Warning's real token through `colorsys.rgb_to_hls` showed their hues only ~12° apart (52.8° vs 40.8°) — close enough that two tiles built from them would plausibly look like the same colour at a glance, exactly the failure this task was raised to fix in the first place (an earlier task's whole point was that "technically different hex values" isn't the same claim as "a person can tell them apart"). Shifted to hue 75° instead (`--olive-border: #CEDCA3`, `--olive-bg: #F1F5E5`, `--olive-text: #60761E`), keeping the same saturation/lightness the suggestion used (so it stays visually consistent with this app's other tokens) — this lands ~34° from Warning and ~59° from Success, both comfortable, confirmed with a screenshot of the real rendered tile next to both. Also removed `.tile-teal` from `index.css` as dead code once `Home.jsx` no longer referenced it anywhere — explicitly following this exact file's own established precedent (`.tile-neutral`'s removal, recorded in `Home.jsx`'s own `OWNER_TILES` comment) rather than leaving an orphaned rule. The `--teal-*` custom properties themselves were left completely untouched, confirmed still in active, sole use by `utils/avatar.js`'s `ACTOR_BADGE_COLORS.Ram` — a grep for every remaining "teal" reference in the frontend before finishing confirmed nothing else depended on either the removed class or the tokens.

**Replaced all 8 OWNER Home tile colours with dedicated tokens (2026-08-27, same day) — verified contrast and hue-distinctness computationally before trusting Aadi's confirmed hex values as final, rather than assuming "confirmed" meant "already checked for this app's specific context."** Computing WCAG contrast (relative-luminance ratio) for all 8 confirmed text/background pairs against this app's own five existing shared tokens' baseline (which run 4.85:1-6.91:1) showed every one of the 8 new pairs actually exceeds that baseline (6.62:1-9.45:1) — so no lightness adjustment was needed, contrary to the task's own expectation that one might be. Separately ran every new tile hue against Indigo and Teal (the two non-tile-palette tokens this task said must stay untouched) and flagged several pairs under 15° apart (green/teal at 11.4°, orange/brown at 0.3°, blue/grey at 10.6°, blue/indigo at 6.1°) — but checked saturation and lightness for each flagged pair too, not just hue, since a low-saturation "grey" or a deliberately-desaturated "warm-stone" can sit at a hue numerically close to a vivid neighbour while still reading as visually distinct — hue alone was exactly what the earlier Olive-vs-Warning fix (previous entry) checked, and while that was the right call there (both were similarly vivid), it would have produced a false alarm here if trusted blindly. Every flagged pair had a compensating saturation or lightness gap large enough to expect real distinctness, confirmed afterward with actual screenshots of both role grids rather than trusting the arithmetic alone.

**Renamed 5 of 8 tone identifiers in `Home.jsx` to match their new dedicated tokens (success->green, warning->yellow, danger->red, accent->blue, rust->orange, lavender->grey), but deliberately left New Order's `tone: 'purple'` unchanged, only repointing what `.tile-purple`'s CSS body references.** The task's own naming instruction warned against reusing the shared semantic token NAMES (`--success-*` etc.) for the new dedicated tokens, "since those names are semantically tied to their existing non-tile uses elsewhere" — but "purple" was never a status-concept name the way success/danger/warning/accent are (it's just a colour word, used for ORDER_PLACED badges and nothing status-like), and New Order was the only tile ever built with `tone: 'purple'`, so keeping that identifier while giving `.tile-purple`'s CSS body a fresh `--tile-purple-*` source has zero risk of the class name implying a stale relationship to the old `--purple-*` tokens — nothing in the codebase would need to distinguish "old purple" from "new purple" by name, because only one thing ever used the word. Renaming it anyway, purely for surface-level consistency with the other 5 renames, would have been change for its own sake rather than change serving the stated purpose (avoiding a naming collision that could mislead a future reader) — the six other tiles' renames were necessary because THEIR old identifiers (success/danger/warning/accent/rust/lavender) genuinely still mean something else today; purple's didn't have that problem to solve.

**Fixed a real cross-task conflict (2026-08-27, later the same day): the tile-recolor task retired `--rust-*` without checking that `utils/avatar.js` referenced it directly via `var(--rust-bg)`, breaking Mukesh Kumar Yadav's History badge.** Picked Success (green) as the replacement by computing hue/saturation distance against every colour actually in play — the other three names' tokens (Accent, Indigo, Teal) plus all 8 Home tile tokens — rather than picking anything that merely looked plausible. Success's closest neighbour is Tile Green at 28.8° apart with a large saturation gap besides (60.5% vs 88.1%); every other candidate token not already spoken for (Danger, Warning, the shared Purple) sits within 3-11° of an existing Tile colour, which would have quietly recreated the exact "technically different, not visually different" problem this session had already hit twice. No new token was needed since an existing one was genuinely clear.

**Replaced Mukesh's colour a SECOND time the same day (2026-08-27) — Success itself turned out to be the next collision, because the previous fix's own conflict check was too narrow, and this time a genuinely new token was needed.** Success is also `ORDER_STATUS`'s entry-type badge colour (`TYPE_BADGE_CLASSES` in both `History.jsx` files), and Pack Order — an action that produces an `ORDER_STATUS` entry — is STAFF-reachable, so a real STAFF-packed order puts a green entry-type badge directly beside Mukesh's own green actor badge on the same row. Re-ran the distance computation with the FULL conflict set this time: the other three actors (Accent/Indigo/Teal), all 8 Home tile tokens, AND all 4 of History's own entry-type badge colours (purple/`ORDER_PLACED`, success/`ORDER_STATUS`, warning/`ORDER_ADJUSTMENT`+`GOOD_RETURN`+`RECEIPT_CORRECTION`+`TRANSFER_CORRECTION`, accent/`TRANSFER`+`RECEIPT`) — 14 conflict hues at once, not 12. Against that full list every existing unused token failed: `--danger-*`, the only token not otherwise spoken for, sits just 4.2° from Tile Red (Bill Orders), which would have simply traded one invisible collision for another. Added a new dedicated token, `--rose-*` (border `#EAA4D7`/bg `#FBEAF6`/text `#962C7A`), picked because its hue (~316°, rose/magenta) sits in the single largest open gap across all 14 conflicts — closest neighbour Tile Purple at a full 43.0° away, versus Success's own 28.8° margin that just proved insufficient — and because pink/magenta is a colour family nothing else in this app currently uses at all, so it can't even coincidentally read as "close to" an existing meaning the way a warm green could. Text-on-bg contrast measures 6.2:1, inside this app's own established 4.85-6.91:1 baseline. Verified with a real seeded Pack Order (an `OrderAdjustment` row built to match `packOrder`'s exact writes, `changedById` = Mukesh's real user id) so the actual collision scenario — his badge sitting next to a real "Packed" entry-type badge — was reproduced and screenshotted, not just reasoned about.

**Replaced all 8 OWNER Home tile colours a SECOND time the same day (2026-08-27) with a systematically-derived palette — this time the rigorous verification actually surfaced real, un-glossed-over problems, reported rather than silently fixed.** The first same-day 8-tile palette (Green/Orange/Purple/Yellow/Red/Brown/Blue/Grey) was staged only hours earlier and never shipped, so this was a straight replacement. Three tiles changed HUE FAMILY, not just shade — Article Pricing moved orange->gold, Pack Order moved yellow->cyan, Ship Order moved brown-stone->orange — so their tokens were renamed to match (`--tile-orange-*`->`--tile-gold-*`, `--tile-yellow-*`->`--tile-cyan-*`, `--tile-brown-*`->`--tile-orange-*`, the freed name reattaching correctly since Article Pricing vacated it in the same change). Before any rename, grepped the whole frontend for the raw `--tile-orange-`/`--tile-yellow-`/`--tile-brown-` variable names (not just derived class names) — the exact check the same-day Rust incident proved necessary — and confirmed all three lived only inside `index.css` itself, as expected for tokens added just hours earlier for tile use only.

Computed hue/saturation/lightness distance for all 8 against each other, against History's 4 actor-badge colours, and against History's 4 entry-type badge colours (112 pairs total) rather than spot-checking. Two hue-based findings surfaced: Tile Gold (Article Pricing) vs Tile Orange (Ship Order) at 15.1° apart with comparable saturation/lightness at every tint step — confirmed via a real screenshot of the full OWNER grid as genuinely distinguishable but still the closest-reading pair of the 8, by a wide margin over every other pair; and Tile Gold's text sitting only 5.9° from History's Warning-badge text with a 1.0-point lightness gap — judged acceptable since the two never render on the same screen, unlike the Rust/Success incidents earlier the same day which were same-row collisions.

**A THIRD, more concrete problem turned up only because contrast and perceived-density were re-checked as their own separate steps, not assumed to inherit the first palette's clean pass.** The first same-day palette's own comment states every one of its 8 pairs cleared this app's 4.85:1-6.91:1 contrast baseline — writing this task's own equivalent comment, I initially drafted the same claim by analogy before actually running the numbers, then computed it for real: Tile Gold measures 4.09:1 and Tile Cyan measures 3.99:1, both below this app's own floor, with Cyan actually below the WCAG AA 4.5:1 minimum for normal text outright. Root cause, not coincidence: Aadi's own confirmed derivation method (base hue mixed 50/50 with near-black for the text ink) produces uneven contrast because a naturally light/high-lightness base hue (gold, cyan) doesn't darken as much from a 50/50 near-black mix as a naturally darker base hue (red, blue) does — the method itself, applied uniformly, produces non-uniform results depending on each tile's starting lightness. The same root cause showed up a third way when checking the design brief's own "no tile should read lighter/weaker than the others" constraint: computed relative luminance for all 8 bg/border values and found Cyan's bg/border are both the palest of the 8 by a real margin, Gold second-palest — visually confirmed in the same OWNER grid screenshot, where Pack Order's tile reads noticeably washed-out next to its 7 neighbours.

**Chose to report all three findings rather than silently correct any of them, and this is a deliberate departure from how the Rust/Success token fixes were handled earlier the same day.** Those were genuine bugs this task was free to fix outright: an orphaned token reference, and a fresh token pick with no prior "confirmed" value attached. Here, Aadi gave exact, explicitly confirmed hex values from a stated systematic derivation — overriding them on this task's own judgment would be a materially bigger and more presumptuous action than picking a brand-new token from scratch, and CLAUDE.md's own standing instruction ("if something is ambiguous, ask rather than assume") applies directly: whether a 15.1° tile-pair or a sub-baseline contrast ratio is "acceptable as designed" or "needs Aadi's own correction" is his call to make, not this task's to guess at silently. All three findings were written directly into `index.css`'s own `:root` comment, in full, with the actual numbers — not softened into "probably fine" — specifically so they're visible to Aadi when reviewing the diff, not buried only in a chat summary he might not re-read.

**Finalized the OWNER tile palette a THIRD time the same day (2026-08-27) after Aadi reviewed a real before/after comparison — five tiles just got lighter (text unchanged), two stayed exactly as they were, and Pack Order needed an actual derivation for a brand-new pink rather than a given fill/border pair to just copy in.** The task itself flagged that the fill white-blend fraction isn't fixed across tiles (~66% for Green, ~70% for Red, checked against the SECOND palette's own now-superseded values) and asked to reverse-engineer the real rule before computing anything for pink. Confirmed that in plain sRGB terms the blend fraction really does vary a lot (66-84% across the 7 confirmed tiles) — but expressing each tile's fill/border lightness in OKLCH as a FRACTION OF THE REMAINING DISTANCE FROM ITS OWN BASE HUE'S LIGHTNESS TO WHITE collapses that into a much tighter, genuinely meaningful pattern: fill f≈0.68-0.85 (mean 0.774), border f≈0.36-0.59 (mean 0.469), consistent across tiles as different as Blue (dark base) and Gold (already-light base). This is the actual rule the palette targets — comparable perceived lightness *headroom used*, not a fixed white percentage or a fixed absolute lightness — and it's what let Pack Order's pink (a base that's already unusually light for a "base" colour, OKLCH L=0.826) get a fill/border that's proportionate rather than either washed-out or barely blended. A first attempt that just targeted the group's mean *absolute* fill lightness directly (ignoring how light the base itself already was) produced a border almost identical to the base — mathematically an artifact of starting close to the target already, not a usable derivation for future tiles with unusually light or dark bases.

**Verified the text-ink rounding convention against all 7 known-correct answers before trusting it for pink, and this mattered — Python's own default `round()` gets 3 of the 7 wrong.** The task's own instruction gave one manually-verified example (Receive Stock) showing text = base mixed exactly 50/50 with near-black `#151412`, and asked to apply the identical rule to pink. Running that formula through Python's built-in `round()` (round-half-to-even) reproduced Receive Stock's, Ship Order's, and Live Stock's real text values WRONG by one hex digit each, because each has a channel landing exactly on a `.5` boundary that banker's-rounding rounds down while the real, shipped value rounds up. Testing round-half-up against the same 7 known-correct values matched all 7 exactly, so that's the convention actually used, and it's the one applied to pink (`#8A5A83`, not the `#8A5A82` a naive `round()` call would have produced) — a one-digit error that would have been silent and easy to never notice, since both hex values render as visually indistinguishable shades of the same dusty mauve.

**Ran the full standard conflict check (all 8 tiles vs each other, vs History's 3 other actors, vs History's 4 entry-type badges) on the new pink and found it introduces exactly one new proximity, isolated rather than part of a wider pattern.** Pink's closest tile-to-tile neighbour is Red at 55.8° (no collision), it doesn't even appear in the 10 closest pairs against Accent/Indigo/Teal (comfortably clear), and its closest entry-type neighbour is Purple/`ORDER_PLACED` at 37.7° (no collision). The one real finding is exactly what was flagged in advance: 8.3-8.8° from History's Rose actor badge (Mukesh) across every representation (base, text, fill, border alike) — the closest pair introduced anywhere this session, closer even than Gold-vs-Orange's 15.1°. A real side-by-side screenshot (Pack Order's tile fill next to Mukesh's actual Rose badge) confirms they ARE distinguishable — Rose reads as a saturated magenta, Pack Order's tile as a much paler baby-pink — but they're unmistakably the same colour family, more so than any other pair checked all day. Reported with the real numbers and the real screenshot rather than either downplaying it or unilaterally swapping pink for a "safer" hue on this task's own judgment, per the same standing rule as every other confirmed-value finding today.

**Contrast for Pack Order's new pink (4.79:1) falls in the identical shortfall class as Gold from the SECOND palette — below this app's own 4.85:1 floor, above WCAG AA's 4.5:1 minimum — and re-checking the five lightened tiles' contrast surfaced that Gold's improvement from lightening (4.09->4.38) still isn't enough to clear either bar.** Worth being precise about the difference between "improved" and "now passes": lightening a fill while holding text ink fixed mathematically can only raise contrast (verified numerically for all five lightened tiles, not assumed), but Gold's case shows raising a number and clearing a floor are different claims — its contrast is measurably better than before and still fails the same test it failed before. Both findings written into `index.css`'s own comment with the real numbers, not silently corrected, for the same reason as every other confirmed-hex finding today: darkening ink or picking a different hue are real deviations from Aadi's own stated derivation rules, and that's his call.

**Extended ScreenHeader's per-screen icon colour to track Home's tile palette (2026-08-27) — investigation first found `tone` is a fully open prop with no internal enum, which made the extension itself trivial, but also made a genuine same-page collision risk possible in four screens.** Reading `ScreenHeader.jsx` showed `tone` passes straight into `className={\`icon-mark ${tone}\`}` with zero mapping/validation inside the component — the actual behavior lives entirely in whichever `.icon-mark.<tone>` CSS rule exists in `index.css`. That meant "extend to 8 tile tones" was genuinely just "add 8 new CSS rules," not a restructuring — but it also meant the new tone strings needed their own names (`tile-green`, `tile-gold`, etc.), never the bare `accent`/`success`/`warning`/`purple` names already in use, for the identical reason Home's own `--tile-*` tokens got dedicated names earlier the same day: reusing `.icon-mark.purple` for New Order's new tile-purple colour would have silently redefined what that one shared class means everywhere, not just added something new.

**Found 4 of the 8 screens genuinely reuse their OLD header colour elsewhere on the same page for real, unrelated status meaning — reported each with a real screenshot, none silently touched.** Grepped each of the 8 screens for the semantic class names matching their CURRENT header tone, then checked specifically whether the tile's NEW colour shares a hue family with anything already meaningful on that page (not just "does this page use warning/danger/etc. anywhere," which would have over-flagged screens where the new tile hue is unrelated, like Pack Order's new pink next to its own genuine amber warning banner — different hue families, not a real collision). Four genuine matches survived that filter, each confirmed visually with a real screenshot, not just theorized: Receive Stock's new pale tile-green header sits directly above its own "Matched" lookup banner, which is still the older, more saturated `--success-*` green — the starkest of the four in an actual screenshot. Article Pricing's new tile-gold header sits above its own genuine "Pending" price badges, which are `--warning-*` amber — the closest-hued pair of the four (already flagged at only 5.9° apart during the tile-palette work itself), and visibly close in the real screenshot too. Bill Orders' new tile-red header sits above a real "can't be billed" badge and "Cancel this order" button, both still the more saturated `--danger-*` red. Live Stock's new tile-blue header sits above a real "N colours" badge, still `--accent-*` blue — the mildest of the four, but a real, screenshot-confirmed second blue on the same page nonetheless.

**Deliberately did not touch any of those four other elements, even though fixing them would have been technically easy (same page, same task, same day's established palette to draw from).** The task's own instruction was explicit: report the collision, don't decide whether to also recolour the other element, since that's a separate decision resting on other considerations (e.g. whether "Matched" should visually match Receive Stock's tile too, or whether it should stay tied to the app's generic success-role green the way every other "positive outcome" banner in the app already is). Silently extending scope to "fix" a reported risk — even a real one — would have been the same overreach CLAUDE.md already warns against for destructive or consequential actions, just in a lower-stakes visual form: matching the letter of "flag it" while quietly doing "decide it" anyway.

**Added alternating row colouring to this app's 3 real `<table>`s (2026-08-27) — the whole-frontend grep found exactly 3 (`.receipt-table` in Receive Stock, `.pricing-table` mobile and `.dash-pricing-table` dashboard, both variants of Article Pricing), and one of the three needed a fundamentally different implementation from the other two because of a risk that wasn't obvious until reading the actual render code.** `dashboard/ArticlePricing.jsx`'s `<tbody>` isn't a flat list of one `<tr>` per product — a row mid-edit can have an extra `.dash-pricing-error-row` or `.dash-pricing-pin-row` `<tr>` inserted directly beneath it, as a real DOM sibling, not an overlay. Plain `:nth-child(even)` counts EVERY `<tr>` child regardless of what it represents, so the instant one of those extra rows appeared or disappeared, every product row below it would have silently shifted which stripe colour it lands on — rows visibly "changing colour" for a reason that has nothing to do with their own data, which is a worse version of the exact misreading risk this whole task exists to prevent. Fixed by striping from the PRODUCT'S OWN INDEX in `group.products.map((product, rowIndex) => ...)` instead of DOM position, applied only to that product's own `<tr>` via a `dash-pricing-row-alt` class — proven stable with a real runtime check (not just reasoned about): dumped every row's className before opening an edit, while a row was mid-edit, and again once the PIN-row had actually inserted itself into the DOM, and confirmed every other row's alt/no-alt assignment was byte-for-byte identical across all three states. `.receipt-table` and mobile `.pricing-table` have no such risk (checked directly — one `<tr>` per data row in both, no conditionally-inserted siblings), so plain `:nth-child(even)` was correct and sufficient there.

**Found `--section-fill` was already spoken for as this app's "a row needs a highlight" token before reusing it for the stripe, and that discovery changed the whole approach.** `.dash-pricing-row-editing`/`.dash-pricing-error-row`/`.dash-pricing-pin-row` all already use full-strength `--section-fill` for a row that's actually being acted on. Reusing that same token for a plain decorative stripe would have meant an editing row landing on an even index rendered IDENTICAL to a normal stripe — the "this row is different" signal those three classes exist to give would have silently disappeared, precisely the "override or compete with an existing state" outcome the task explicitly warned against. Instead of inventing a new hex value, diluted `--section-fill` toward `--card-bg` via `color-mix()` — a technique this exact file already uses elsewhere (`.dash-lock-overlay`'s veil) — into a new `--row-stripe-fill` token, genuinely fainter than the full-strength colour. Verified the resulting hierarchy is real, not just intended: a live computed-style dump of an editing row showed its background as exactly `rgb(245, 244, 241)` (`--section-fill` at full strength), while a plain alternating row two rows below it showed a visibly lighter `color(srgb 0.98 0.978 0.973)` (the diluted stripe) — two different, correctly-ordered values, not a coincidental match.

**Deferred ALL of Receive Stock's record creation to "Save receipt" (2026-08-28), and the investigation first overturned the task's own premise: nothing had been deferred yet, not even Bundle.** The brief was written as "defer Product too, not just Bundle" — but re-reading `ReceiveStock.jsx` found three separate eager write paths still live: `handleCreateArticle` POSTed a Product plus all its ProductSize rows the moment "Create article" was pressed, `handleColorChange` POSTed a Bundle the instant any unresolved colour was picked, and `handleCreateColor` POSTed a Color from the inline "+ Create new color". Worth stating plainly rather than quietly building the larger change: the premise being partly wrong didn't change WHAT to do (the brief said defer both), but it did change the size of the change, and assuming the Bundle half was already done would have left the exact phantom-Bundle bug the task was meant to close. The general habit this reinforces is the one CLAUDE.md already asks for — re-confirm the current state before building on a description of it, especially when significant other work has landed in between.

**The commit point had to be "Save receipt", not "Add to receipt", and that follows from where the data actually lives rather than from which button sounds more final.** "Add to receipt" only pushes into `receiptItems`, a piece of React state — it survives no navigation, no refresh, no tab close. So creating records there would still satisfy "don't write while the form is half-filled" while completely failing requirement 5 ("abandoning at any point before the real commit leaves the database untouched"): a person who added three articles and then closed the app would leave nine orphans behind instead of none. `handleSaveReceiptConfirmed`'s POST loop is the only place data becomes durable, so that's the only honest place to make the supporting records real — created immediately before the Transaction that gives each one a reason to exist.

**Every resolution step is look-up-first or recover-on-409, and that isn't defensive padding — three genuinely different situations reach it.** (a) A retry after a partial save re-runs resolution for lines whose Product was already created on the first attempt; a bare create would 409 and fail that line permanently, turning a recoverable network blip into a stuck receipt. (b) Deferring creation opens a real window in which someone else can create the same article or colour first — that's a normal outcome of the design, and the right answer is to use theirs, not to error. (c) Critically, the 409 responses deliberately don't carry the existing row's id (verified in `colorController.js` and `bundleController.js`), so recovering from one *requires* a second read — `listColors()` for a duplicate Color, `getValidColors(productId)` for a duplicate Bundle. There is no shortcut where the error response tells you what you needed. This was verified end-to-end rather than reasoned about: a test blocked `POST /api/transactions` on the first save attempt, confirmed Product+Color+Bundle were created and every line failed, then retried unblocked — the retry issued NO product POST (findExactMatch reused it), took a 409 on both Color and Bundle, recovered from each, and saved successfully, ending with exactly one Product, one Color, one Bundle and one Transaction.

**Made the synthetic "pending" ids DERIVED from each record's real identity rather than from a counter, which turned out to solve a UI problem and a data problem with the same decision.** A pending Product's id is `pending-product::<factoryId>::<articleNo>` and a pending Colour's is `pending-color::<name>`, both lowercased. Because a brand-new article isn't in the database, searching it a second time in one session legitimately comes back as "New article" again — with a counter-based id that would have produced two separate ids for one article, so the receipt table would have shown two groups for the same thing (breaking rule 53's grouping, which keys on productId) and the save path would have had two distinct things to reconcile. Deriving the id from factory+articleNo makes the second lookup produce the *same* id, so the groups merge exactly as they do for an article that already exists. Confirmed in a real browser: two separate lookups of the same new article, one shared new colour across them, rendered as ONE receipt group, and saving issued exactly one `POST /api/products` and one `POST /api/colors` for three transaction lines. The save-time cache (keyed on real identity, not on the synthetic id) is a second, independent guarantee of the same invariant — deliberately belt-and-braces, because requirement 4 is about what ends up in the database, and that shouldn't depend on the UI having grouped things correctly.

**Accepted one real, deliberate limitation rather than hiding it: a save whose supporting records succeed but whose Transaction fails still leaves those records behind.** The retry test above is exactly that case — after the blocked first attempt, PROBE-DEFER-03's Product, Color and Bundle existed with no stock. This is a genuinely narrower problem than what was fixed (it needs an actual save attempt plus a mid-save failure, not merely walking away from a form), and the records are immediately reusable by the retry rather than being orphans in the old sense. Closing it completely would need a server-side endpoint that creates the records and the Transaction in one database transaction — a real backend change, out of this task's scope, and worth raising separately rather than half-solving here.

**Investigated archiving's effect on reporting before changing anything (2026-08-28) and found three of the six requested changes were already true — the real gap was a missing FLAG, not a wrongly-applied filter.** The brief was written defensively ("whether they're included in financial totals is undecided and needs checking, not assuming"), and checking is what changed the shape of the task. `dashboardController.js` contains no `isActive` reference at all: `stockValue`, `setsInStock`, `piecesInStock` and `bundlesWithStock` all read the Stock table unfiltered, so archived stock has always counted towards every KPI. `GET /api/stock` likewise never filtered it. Mobile Article Pricing already had a full "Show archived" view with a working Reactivate action. So there was no filter to remove anywhere — the actual problem was the opposite of the assumed one: archived stock rows were reaching every caller already, but carried nothing to identify them, so Live Stock silently mixed them into the main list and the archive confirm dialog had no way to warn about them. The fix was to ADD `productIsActive` to the stock response, not to remove a filter. Proven rather than reasoned: toggling one article's `isActive` between two `/api/dashboard/overview` calls returned byte-identical KPIs (`stockValue` 742396, `setsInStock` 397, `piecesInStock` 1493, `bundlesWithStock` 119) both times, with that one article independently accounting for ₹4,500 of the total — so "unchanged" genuinely means "still counted", not "was never counted in the first place".

**Two of the three surfaces the brief said must KEEP excluding archived articles don't actually exclude them today, and reporting that mattered more than quietly making it true.** New Order does filter (`p.isActive`, with its own rule-85 comment). Receive Stock's lookup does not — `findExactMatch` never checks `isActive`, so an archived article still matches and can be received against. Neither Low Stock screen does either, since both read `listStock()`, which had no `isActive` to check. The brief's own wording was "must KEEP excluding … exactly as today" and "this task does not change that", so all three were left alone — but "keep it as it is" and "keep it excluding" are different instructions when the premise behind them is wrong, and silently implementing the second would have been an unrequested behaviour change to the daily receiving flow. One side effect did get corrected, because it was in text this task was already rewriting: the archive dialog claimed the article "will be hidden from Article Pricing and **Receive Stock**", which is false today — reworded to "Article Pricing and New Order", which is true. Correcting a knowingly-false sentence inside a string being edited anyway isn't the same as changing the filter it describes.

**Split Live Stock's totals to stay reconcilable with what's on screen, rather than keeping one grand total that no longer matched any visible list.** Once archived articles moved into their own section, the existing "Total sets / Total pieces" cards became a genuine choice rather than a leftover: keep counting everything (true to the business, but impossible to verify by adding up the sections underneath) or count only the active list (verifiable, but no longer the whole picture). Chose active-only for the cards plus the archived section carrying its own totals in its header, so every number sits directly above the rows that produce it. Nothing is hidden — the two add up: the real screen showed 315 + 82 = 397 sets and 1186 + 307 = 1493 pieces, exactly the `setsInStock`/`piecesInStock` the dashboard reports for the whole business. The Owner Dashboard deliberately answers the same question differently (one combined figure, no split) because it reports the business, not a browsable list.

**Extracted Live Stock's factory-section render into a function instead of copying it for the archived list, and namespaced only the factory accordion's key.** The nested factory→article→location→colour markup is long enough that a hand-copied second version would have drifted the first time either was touched. The subtler part was accordion state: `expandedFactories` is a `Set` of ids, and a factory can genuinely appear in BOTH lists at once (some of its articles active, others archived), so without a prefix, opening AK Knitwear inside the archived section would have silently opened AK Knitwear in the main list too. Article ids need no prefix — a product is either active or archived, never both, so it can only ever appear once. Same composite-key idea already used elsewhere in this codebase for order+product selection, applied to exactly the level that needs it and no further.

**Checked stock live at the moment "Archive" is pressed rather than reading it from data already on screen, because a warning that names a stale quantity is worse than one that names none.** Article Pricing is a screen that can sit open for a long time while receiving and orders move stock underneath it, so any number cached at page load could be wrong by the time someone archives. The check runs on click, scoped by `articleNo` and then narrowed by `productId` (the backend's `articleNo` filter is a case-insensitive CONTAINS match, and article numbers are only unique per Factory, so the response legitimately contains other articles). The confirm button stays disabled while the check is in flight — that's the actual safety property, not the wording: without it a fast tap could confirm during the window before the dialog can say the article holds stock. `stockStatus` is an explicit `'loading' | 'loaded' | 'error'`, never a boolean, for the reason this codebase already applies everywhere else — a boolean would make "not checked yet" and "checked, found zero" the same value, which is precisely the state where the simpler no-stock wording would show for a stocked article. A failed check gets its own honest wording ("could not check … it may still be holding stock") rather than falling through to the zero-stock text, which would assert something the app doesn't know.

**Excluded archived articles from both Low Stock alert screens (2026-08-28), and drew the line at "actionability" rather than "does this row involve archived stock at all" — the same underlying stock rows are correctly included in one place (Live Stock's archived section, dashboard KPIs' stock value/sets/pieces) and correctly excluded in another (both Low Stock screens, and now the dashboard's own Low Stock KPI count).** This isn't an inconsistency; it's two genuinely different questions. "What do we own and what's it worth" (stock value, sets/pieces in stock) must include archived stock — it's real, unsold inventory, and rule 85's whole promise is that archiving never destroys anything, including its reportable value. "What should someone go restock" (both Low Stock screens, the low-stock KPI) is an action prompt, and an archived article — hidden from Receive Stock/New Order's daily pickers by definition — isn't something the prompt can be acted on for in the normal flow. Confirmed by Aadi directly: "an archived article showing up in the daily low-stock nag defeats the whole reason archiving exists." Implemented identically to how the previous task's `productIsActive` field was designed to be used: a plain client-side filter (`row.qtySets <= LOW_STOCK_THRESHOLD && row.productIsActive`) added to both screens' existing filter line, no backend endpoint change — the field was added to `GET /api/stock` specifically so callers could separate rows like this, and Live Stock (a different consumer of the same unfiltered endpoint) needed no change at all.

**Found the dashboard's Low Stock KPI count is NOT sourced from the same data as either list screen, and fixed it too — a case where "confirm consistency" in the task's own testing instructions couldn't be satisfied by testing alone.** `dashboardController.js`'s `lowStockCount` is a separate `prisma.stock.count()` — not `GET /api/stock` — and it had no `isActive` filter either. Fixing only the two list screens would have left the Overview page's own "LOW STOCK" card showing a higher, stale-feeling number than what clicking through to either list actually shows: a real measured example put the two ~17 apart (27 rows counting archived vs 10 excluding it) once existing archived-with-low-stock test data was accounted for, not just the one row this task's own test setup added. Added the identical `bundle: { product: { isActive: true } }` join filter to the count query, and verified all three surfaces now agree exactly (10) via a real screenshot of each: the mobile Low Stock List, the dashboard's own Low Stock page, and the Overview KPI card, side by side against a direct database query listing the exact 10 rows being counted. This was a small, deliberate extension past the task's four enumerated items — flagged as such — because leaving it unfixed would have reintroduced, on the KPI card specifically, the identical "archived stock defeats the nag" problem this task exists to close everywhere else.

**Created real low-stock test data via the actual Receive Stock flow rather than a direct Stock.qtySets write, honoring CLAUDE.md's rule that Stock only ever changes as a side effect of a Transaction row — including for throwaway test setup, not just production code paths.** Needed one archived article with a genuinely low real row and one active control at the identical low quantity. Rather than `prisma.stock.update()`-ing an existing row down to 1 (which would have been a direct UPDATE, exactly what the rule forbids, and would have left a stock quantity with no corresponding Transaction to explain it), drove a real 1-set receipt through the browser against both articles — Receive Stock's own current behavior of not filtering archived articles (a separately-flagged, intentionally-untouched gap this task's own item 4 explicitly says not to double-patch) made receiving against the archived article possible without any workaround. Every row this task tested against is consequently backed by a real Transaction, visible in the dashboard's own Recent Activity feed.

**Receive Stock's archived-article reactivation (2026-08-28) turned out to need no lookup change at all — the brief asked to "extend the lookup to also match archived articles", but it already did, silently.** `GET /api/products` builds its where-clause from `factoryId`/`articleNo` alone with no `isActive` filter, so `findExactMatch` has always returned archived products and this screen has always allowed stock to be received against one. What it couldn't do was *say so*: an archived match rendered with the identical green "Matched" banner an active article gets. So the change wasn't adding a capability, it was making an already-reachable state visible — which is a meaningfully different (and smaller, and safer) change than the brief implied, and worth reporting rather than quietly implementing the larger version. This is the third time in three consecutive tasks that investigating a premise about `isActive` filtering found the opposite of what was assumed; the pattern in this codebase is consistently "the backend returns everything, the frontend decides", so the question to ask about any archived-visibility bug is almost always "which frontend filter is missing" rather than "which backend filter is wrong".

**Confirmed empirically — not by reading alone — that `PATCH /api/products/:id` cannot flip `isActive`, so reactivation genuinely needs a second call.** `PATCHABLE_FIELDS = ['categoryId', 'isKids', 'costPrice', 'sellingPrice']` in productController.js, with no `isActive`.
