The brief explicitly asked to report which of the two shapes was true rather than guess, so this was verified twice over: by reading the constant, and then by a real API call as OWNER that successfully updated both prices and came back with `"isActive": false` still in the response body. The price path therefore issues `PATCH /api/products/:id` (prices + pin) followed by `PATCH /api/products/:id/reactivate`. Ordered price-first deliberately: if the second call fails, the article is left archived but correctly re-priced AND the line still carries its deferred reactivation intent, so "Save receipt" finishes the job — the failure degrades into the no-price path. The reverse order would leave an article reactivated at a stale price, which is the worse of the two failure states.

**The price path is OWNER-only, not merely PIN-gated, and that's a stronger gate than the brief's own test case assumed.** The brief asked to "confirm a STAFF user without a PIN set can't reach this path at all (same gate as every other PIN-required action)" — but `router.patch('/:id', requireAuth, requireRole('OWNER'), requirePinForPriceEdits, ...)` makes OWNER unconditional, so a STAFF request is rejected for its ROLE before the PIN is ever considered. Proved with a direct API call: STAFF sending a perfectly valid PIN gets `FORBIDDEN_ROLE`, not a PIN error. This is CLAUDE.md's own non-negotiable rule working as designed (price edits need OWNER *and* PIN, never either alone), and it's reinforced by `priceEditPinHash` being "only ever set for the OWNER account" — a STAFF user has no PIN to be missing. Consequently the price affordance isn't rendered for STAFF at all rather than rendered-and-rejected: `productSelect()` never selects `costPrice` for a STAFF request either, so there would be no current price to show them even if the form opened. STAFF still reactivate archived articles perfectly well — they take the deferred no-price path, which is the default and needs no extra interaction.

**Kept the archived flag alongside `status: 'matched'` rather than inventing a third lookup status, because an archived article needs every downstream behaviour of a matched one.** Both colour-fetch effects, `handleColorChange`, and `handleAddToReceipt` all branch on `status === 'matched'`, and an archived article is a real Product with real Bundles, real sizes and real stock — every one of those paths must run unchanged. A new status would have meant touching all four call sites to treat it as matched-except-visually, which is strictly more code to get the same result. Only the banner and the reactivation affordance read `lookup.archived`. The banner reuses the existing amber `.lookup-banner-warning` rather than a new colour: amber already means "something other than the straightforward path is about to happen" on this screen (it's the New-article state's colour), and the two amber states can never co-occur — an article is either found or not — so there's no ambiguity to design around.

**The reactivation cache is a `Set`, not a `Map`, and needs no 409-recovery unlike every other record type resolved at save time.** `reactivateProduct` is idempotent by construction (productController.js sets `isActive: true` unconditionally and returns 200 whether or not it was already true), so a retry after a partial save is safe with no duplicate-detection at all — a genuine asymmetry with Color and Bundle, whose creates 409 and require a second read to recover. The cache still earns its place for a different reason than the others: it isn't resolving an identity to an id, it's just preventing a receipt with several colour lines against one archived article from firing the same reactivate call once per line. Verified in a real browser: three colour lines against one archived article produced exactly one `PATCH /reactivate`, followed by three bundle/transaction pairs.

**Flagging price history as a genuine follow-up candidate, but a smaller one than it first appears, because the financially load-bearing history already exists.** `Product` keeps a single current `costPrice`/`sellingPrice`, so an OWNER changing the price during reactivation overwrites the old value with no record of what it was. That sounds worse than it is: `Transaction.costPriceSnapshot` already captures the cost price at the exact moment stock was received (its own schema comment says this is precisely so "a later price change never retroactively alters what was actually owed to the factory for this receipt"), and `OrderLineItem.priceAtOrder` does the same on the selling side. So every figure that depends on a historical price already has its own snapshot, and what a price-history table would add is reporting ("when did we change our list price, and to what"), not correctness. Worth building if that reporting question comes up for real; not worth pre-emptively building to fix a data-loss problem that the snapshot columns already prevent.

**Added History's "Group by" toggle (2026-08-28) and the investigation found the real constraint before any code was written: only TRANSFER and RECEIPT entries carry a structured articleNo/location field at all.** Reading every `entries.push(...)` in historyController.js directly (not assumed from the description text) showed ORDER_PLACED/ORDER_STATUS/ORDER_ADJUSTMENT, GOOD_RETURN, RECEIPT_CORRECTION and TRANSFER_CORRECTION all mention an article or location only INSIDE their pre-built `description` string, never as a field on the entry object. This ruled out one otherwise-tempting approach immediately: parsing the description to recover a location/article for grouping. That would have been exactly the "component builds its own event copy" pattern this screen's own header comment says it deliberately avoids — and brittle besides, since a future wording tweak to a description would silently break grouping with no error anywhere. Chose a named fallback bucket instead ("Order events" for the three order-level types, "Other" for everything else lacking the field) and reported this constraint rather than quietly extending the backend to add more fields, since the task was explicitly scoped to re-presenting already-fetched data, not fetching more of it. Flagged exposing those fields server-side as a worthwhile follow-up if finer-grained grouping for those types matters later.

**Picked Transfer's DESTINATION over its origin for Location grouping, specifically to make the rule consistent with an existing convention rather than picking arbitrarily.** A Transfer is the one entry type with two genuine locations (from and to), and duplicating the entry into both buckets was ruled out by the same logic the task itself applied to Order-level entries spanning several articles — an event should appear once, not once per dimension it touches. Reasoned it as: a Transfer's arrival leg is the same KIND of event a Receipt already represents for grouping purposes (stock becoming newly present at a location), so bucketing by destination makes "Group by Location" answer one consistent question across both stock-movement types — "what did this location receive" — rather than answering a different question for each type. Explicitly flagged this as a judgment call in the report rather than presenting it as the only defensible answer, since an equally reasonable case exists for the origin (where the deficit occurred). Verified against real data in both directions after building: a transfer described as "Gurgaon → Delhi" landed in the Delhi bucket, and one described as "Delhi → Gurgaon" landed in Gurgaon — the rule behaves exactly as documented, not just as intended.

**Extracted the entry-row markup (badge + description + actor pill) into `HistoryEntryRow`, and the section wrapper (heading + card) into `HistorySection`, so four rendering paths (day-grouped default, Person, Article, Location) share exactly one implementation each rather than four hand-copied near-duplicates.** This is the same "extract before a second copy can drift" reasoning applied repeatedly earlier this session (Live Stock's `renderFactorySection`) — grouping mode was about to need the identical row markup rendered from four different data shapes (a day's entries, a person's entries, an article's entries, a location's entries), and copy-pasting it four times would have meant any future change to how a row renders (a new entry type, a new badge treatment) needing to be made in four places to stay correct.

**Reused `.history-day`/`.history-day-heading`/`.history-day-card` verbatim for every grouping mode's sections, rather than introducing neutrally-named classes, because `dashboard/History.jsx` already depends on those exact names for its own separate day-grouping copy.** That file's own header comment says it "borrows this screen's grouping logic and CSS classes... so the two surfaces can't quietly drift apart" — meaning renaming them here to something more semantically accurate for a Person/Article/Location section (which isn't a "day" at all) would have risked a file this task was never scoped to touch. The rule itself was already generic in behaviour ("an eyebrow label above a padded card of rows," nothing calendar-specific), which is what made reusing a day-named class for a non-day section correct rather than a misuse — the CSS doesn't know or care what the heading text says.

**Verified the one code branch real dev data couldn't exercise (a non-order-level type with no structured article/location field) with a pure-function unit test instead of forcing a flaky UI flow to produce one.** No `GOOD_RETURN`/`RECEIPT_CORRECTION`/`TRANSFER_CORRECTION` entries existed in the database at the start of this task, so real-browser testing alone would have left the "not order-level, falls to Other" branch of `articleGroupFor` completely unexercised — a genuinely different code path than the "order-level, falls to Order events" branch that DID have real data. Rather than fighting a multi-step Good Returns form (which failed twice for unrelated reasons — an archived article rejected by the picker, then an article with no selling price rejected by the return-valuation check — before a third attempt with a fully-priced active article actually succeeded), ran the exact grouping functions directly in Node against synthetic entries shaped like every real type historyController.js produces. This is a case where testing the pure logic function directly is a MORE precise test than a browser proxy for it would have been, not a lesser substitute — the browser can only prove one specific data shape happened to render correctly, while the unit test proves the general rule holds for every type by construction. Once the third Good Return attempt did succeed, its real resulting entry was confirmed on screen too, landing in "Other" for both Article and Location grouping exactly as the unit test predicted — so the finding was checked both ways rather than resting on either alone.

**Extracted History's "Group by" bucketing rules into `utils/historyGrouping.js`, shared by both `History.jsx` and `dashboard/History.jsx`, but deliberately left rendering un-shared between them (2026-08-28).** Bringing the toggle to the dashboard screen was framed from the start as "the RULES are already decided, this is about applying them to a structurally different file" — reading `dashboard/History.jsx` in full confirmed exactly how different: its rows carry an entire Correct/PIN-correction UI (Transaction Corrections and Transfer Corrections, each with their own multi-step form) wired to a large block of the component's own top-level state (`correctingId`, `draft`, `touchPrice`, `transferDraft`, ...), none of which mobile's read-only rows have any equivalent of. Sharing the *rendering* (a `HistoryEntryRow`-equivalent component) would have meant either passing a dozen pieces of state and handlers through as props for a component only one of the two screens actually uses, or duplicating the correction JSX outside the shared component anyway — neither is a real simplification. Sharing the *bucketing logic* (`articleGroupFor`, `locationGroupFor`, `buildGroups`, `buildPersonGroups`, `buildDayGroups`, all pure functions of `entries` alone) had no such conflict: both screens must agree on which entry lands in which bucket under which mode, with zero legitimate reason to differ, so a silent drift there would be a real bug rather than an intentional difference. Landed on: one shared utility module for the rules (importable, independently testable — exactly the pure-function design the previous entry above already validated), and per-file rendering — `HistoryEntryRow`/`HistorySection` as extracted components on the mobile side (nothing to close over), `renderEntry`/`renderSection` as plain closures over local state on the dashboard side (closing over `correctingId` etc. is the entire reason they can't be pulled out as standalone components without a large prop-drilling exercise that would buy nothing).

**The dashboard's Correct action needed to keep working identically regardless of grouping mode, and testing it required understanding the dashboard's separate PIN-lock overlay isn't the same as the correction's own inner PIN branch — two independent gates the task's phrasing could easily be conflated.** `dashboard/History.jsx` has TWO points where a PIN can matter: (a) `DashboardLayout`'s own screen-privacy lock (unrelated to this feature, applies to the *entire* dashboard, starts locked on every mount, gates on `user.hasPinSet`) and (b) the correction form's own `touchPrice` branch (only appears if the correction changes cost price, per the pre-existing Transaction Correction feature). Confirming "a real Correct action still works from within a non-chronological grouping mode" only exercises (b) — this task never asked for or needed a price-touching correction, so a plain quantity-reason correction (no PIN at all) was the right and sufficient test. (a) had to be unlocked first just to click anything at all, using a PIN set directly via Prisma on the probe account for the duration of testing — a completely separate concern from the feature under test, logged as its own Mistakes & Fixes entry below since it wasn't anticipated going in.

**Renamed "Ship"/"Shipped" to "Dispatch"/"Dispatched" as a display-only sweep (2026-08-28), and the investigation found one place where display text had NEVER been a separate string at all — it was derived live from the enum spelling, which is exactly what made this one rename structurally different from a find-and-replace.** `historyController.js`'s `statusWord()`/`statusLabel()` build the History tag text and description directly from `String(status).toLowerCase()` (`'SHIPPED' -> 'shipped' -> 'Shipped'`), by deliberate design — their own comment explains this exists specifically so a separate `{ SHIPPED: 'Shipped' }` lookup table could never silently disagree with the description built from the same value. That design assumed display word and enum spelling would always match; this task's entire premise is the one case where they must now permanently diverge. Rather than replace the derivation with a full lookup table (re-introducing the exact "two things that could drift" risk the comment warns against, for the sake of one entry), added a single named override — `STATUS_WORD_OVERRIDES = { SHIPPED: 'dispatched' }`, consulted before falling back to the lowercase derivation — so PLACED/PACKED/BILLED keep the zero-maintenance "derive from one input" guarantee, and SHIPPED gets its one necessary, explicitly-documented exception. Confirmed correct by watching a live dispatch action generate a brand-new History entry reading "SAI: order dispatched" with a "Dispatched" tag, then separately confirming via Prisma that the underlying `Order.status` and the `OrderAdjustment.newValue` audit record both still say `SHIPPED` verbatim — the override only touches what renders, never what's stored.

**Found and fixed the frontend's OWN equivalent of the same derive-vs-diverge problem, just simpler: `ORDER_STATUS_LABEL` in `utils/orderStatus.js` was already a plain lookup table (`{ SHIPPED: 'Shipped' }`), not a derivation — one key's value changed to `'Dispatched'` and every consumer (dashboard Orders' and Parties' status pills, confirmed by grepping for every import of the constant) picked it up with no further changes, which is the whole reason that table was centralised in the first place rather than each screen hand-rolling its own status-to-text map.**

**Extended Transfer's chip-based multi-select to the search-results list (2026-08-28) by grouping the flat search results by Article and reusing `renderArticleChips` verbatim, rather than teaching that component a second shape or building a parallel selection UI for search.** The real complication, investigated before writing any code: search results are a flat list that can span several different Articles — and, unlike the Factory→Article browsing view, several different FACTORIES too, since search deliberately bypasses Factory scoping (requirement 3, unchanged). Grouping by `productId` (not the raw `articleNo` string) is what makes this safe: article numbers are only unique PER Factory (rule 54), so two different Factories can genuinely share a number, and a real dev-data example of exactly that collision existed already (`"6099"` under both Comeco and RDX, and separately `"6002"` under both RDX and Jyoti Creations, both at Gurgaon) — grouping by the string would have silently merged two unrelated Factories' stock into one chip block. Extracted the browsing view's previously-inline grouping logic into a shared `buildArticleGroups(rows)` function, called once Factory-filtered (browsing) and once unfiltered (search) — one function that can't drift into disagreeing with itself, per the same reasoning this session has applied repeatedly to shared bucketing/grouping logic elsewhere (e.g. `[[pure functions and testability]]`, History's own `buildGroups`).

**Search's grouped rendering needed one thing the browsing view's own `renderArticleChips` calls don't: a visible header naming the Article number AND its Factory, since search has no other context to disambiguate two Factories' identically-numbered articles.** The browsing view already knows which Factory it's scoped to (the dropdown), so its own multi-colour accordion header only needs the article number, and a single-colour article skips the header entirely (nothing to disambiguate within an already-Factory-scoped list). Neither shortcut is safe for search. Built `renderSearchGroupHeader` as a small, deliberately NON-collapsible label (reusing `accordion-header-text`/`accordion-title-sm`/`accordion-subtitle` for visual consistency with the browsing view's real accordion, but with no button or chevron) shown above every search group unconditionally, including single-colour ones. Chose non-collapsible over an accordion specifically because a search result is already a narrowed, deliberately-sought list — hiding what someone just searched for behind an extra click they'd have to already know to make would undercut the reason they searched, not aid it. Confirmed the disambiguation works against the real "6099" collision: searching it surfaced two separate, correctly-labelled groups ("6099 — RDX" and "6099 — Comeco"), each independently checkable.

**Confirmed, empirically and not just by code inspection, that `handleAddSelectedForArticle`'s existing per-article clearing already generalizes correctly to search-built groups, with zero changes needed to the function itself (requirement 4).** The function only ever reads `article.rows` and keys everything off each row's own `bundleId` — a value with no per-Factory scoping to get confused by, unlike `articleNo`. Real-browser test: checked colours under two DIFFERENT article groups surfaced by the same search (`"6002"` and `"6040"`), set different quantities on each, clicked "Add selected" on only the `"6002"` group, and confirmed the `"6040"` group's checked chip and its in-progress stepper value survived untouched — proving the clearing logic really is scoped to the invoking article's own bundleIds, not a broader wipe that happened to look right in the browsing view only by coincidence of usually having just one article open at a time.

**Extending the chip mechanism to search made the entire older one-at-a-time mechanism (`selectedRow`/`qtySets`/`note`, staged via an explicit "+ Add to transfer" button) fully dead, not just unused-by-search — surfaced to Aadi before building, since it silently drops a working feature.** That mechanism's own comment already said nothing else on the screen used it; once search stopped being the one remaining caller, `finalizeActiveLine`, `handleSelectRow`, `handleStageAnother`, `renderStockRow`, the "Sets to move" stepper block, and the whole optional per-line note field had no path left to them. The task's own instruction ruled out the alternative (keeping a second selection UI just for search) explicitly, so the only real open question was whether the note capability should survive in some other form. Asked directly rather than assumed silently: confirmed the answer was to drop it entirely, matching the chip/"Add selected" mechanism exactly (which, like New Order's, has no note concept at all) — the cleaner and explicitly-confirmed choice over inventing a new "note the whole batch" affordance nothing had asked for. The dead code's removal was a net simplification, not just a wash — the production JS bundle shrank slightly (453.89 kB → 452.13 kB) from deleting it.

**Article rename (2026-08-28) — Phase 1's investigation changed the shape of the whole task, and its most useful finding was which places did NOT need fixing.** The brief assumed the likely case was "every historical surface joins Product.name live." Reading each one directly rather than assuming found a more mixed picture, and the negatives mattered as much as the positives:
- **Order line items — LIVE join, real gap.** `lineItemToResponse` read `li.bundle.product.name`, with no snapshot despite `priceAtOrder` sitting on the very same row already doing exactly this for price. This is the one user-visible historical surface: the Owner Dashboard's Orders page renders months-old completed and cancelled orders, and Pack/Bill/Ship detail all share the same response builder.
- **Transfers and Returns — LIVE joins, real but currently unreachable.** Both `transferController.toResponse` and `returnController.toResponse` expose `productName` off a live join, but `listTransfers`/`listReturns` are defined in the frontend API layer and imported by **nothing** — no screen renders either list today. Snapshotted them anyway: both are genuinely immutable historical event records (a return's own schema header literally calls it "a simple event log"), both already snapshot a price, and leaving them live would silently re-open the identical gap the moment someone builds a transfers screen.
- **History — no gap at all.** History never selects `product.name`; every entry is built from `articleNo` + colour (`articleLabel`, and every `entries.push` verified individually). A rename cannot touch it.
- **Dashboard KPIs / revenue / locationRevenue — no gap.** None of them read a product name; revenue math is entirely price-and-quantity.
- **Live Stock / Low Stock — live join, and correctly so.** These are current-state views, not historical records. They *should* follow a rename immediately, and do. Snapshotting there would have been the actual bug.

**Chose `productNameSnapshot` nullable with a `?? live name` read fallback, rather than backfilling — and the fallback is a disclosed limitation, not a design preference.** The brief explicitly ruled out inventing "correct" historical names for pre-fix rows, which is right: the original name was never captured, so any backfill would be fabricating data that looks authoritative. Nullable + `productNameSnapshot ?? bundle.product.name` gives the honest split — rows created from 2026-08-28 onward are fully immune to a rename; older rows keep following the current name, which is the only value that ever existed for them. Recorded in `03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md`'s hard-rules section and both API/schema docs so the limitation stays visible rather than becoming folklore. Also noted there: `articleNo` needs no equivalent snapshot, because it's immutable after creation (the endpoint rejects patching it), so it can never drift from what a record captured.

**Role-gating for rename was determined by reading the route chain, not by assuming either extreme.** The brief warned against assuming OWNER-only *or* any-role. `routes/products.js` shows `router.patch('/:id', requireAuth, requireRole('OWNER'), requirePinForPriceEdits, updateProduct)` — so the established convention for *non-price* Product attribute edits (`categoryId`, `isKids`) is **OWNER role, unconditionally, no PIN**; the PIN middleware inspects the body and only engages for price fields. `name` therefore joins `PATCHABLE_FIELDS` as an ordinary attribute: OWNER-only, no PIN. Worth noting the contrast that could have misled a guess — `deactivate`/`reactivate` on the same resource *are* any-role, but those are archive lifecycle actions, not attribute edits, so they're the wrong analogue. Verified live: STAFF renaming gets `403 FORBIDDEN_ROLE`; OWNER renaming with **no `pin` in the body at all** returns `200`.

**Rename-applies-to-the-whole-article was confirmed structurally, not assumed.** `name` is a column on `Product`; `Bundle` carries only `productId` + `colorId` and has no name of its own. There is therefore no representable way for two colours of one article to disagree on a name, and nothing per-colour was built. The test article deliberately had two colours to make this visible in practice as well as on paper.

**For Receive Stock, "immediate" was re-derived from first principles rather than copied from the price case — and it turned out to be right for a completely different reason.** The price path commits immediately because it exercises a **PIN**: a point-in-time grant of authority that must not be held in browser state until some later, unrelated button (the reasoning already logged as [[defer vs commit: intent vs authority]]). A name edit involves no PIN, so that argument doesn't transfer at all. The deferred things on that screen (pending Product, pending Colour, staged reactivation) share a different property: they describe intent *about this receipt* and are meaningless if it's abandoned — reactivating an archived article only makes sense if stock actually arrives. A wrong name is in **neither** category: it's a correction to already-real, already-shared data that is wrong independently of whether the receipt is ever saved. Deferring it would mean noticing a typo, fixing it, then abandoning the receipt for an unrelated reason (wrong location) and silently losing a correction that was valid on its own terms. So: immediate — but because the correction's validity is independent of the receipt, not because of any authority-holding concern. Proved in the browser by correcting the name, then navigating away without saving the receipt, and confirming the new name had persisted.

**Put rename on Article Pricing as its own form rather than as an extra field on the existing price form.** Both mobile and dashboard Article Pricing are already the OWNER-only article-management screens with edit infrastructure, so it's the natural home — no new screen needed. But merging it into the price form would have meant either dragging a PIN requirement onto an edit that doesn't need one, or making the price form's PIN *conditional on which fields happen to be dirty* — a subtler, more breakable rule than two clearly separate forms. The rename form's complete absence of a PIN field is itself the clearest statement of what kind of edit it is. Both rename forms (Article Pricing and Receive Stock) state the snapshot guarantee in plain language under the input, because "will this rewrite my old orders?" is the first thing anyone renaming an article will wonder, and the honest answer is reassuring.

## Mistakes & Fixes

Every entry here follows the same five-part structure, backend or frontend, no exceptions: **(1) Original approach** — what was tried first and why it seemed right at the time. **(2) What went wrong** — the actual symptom, and how it was noticed. **(3) Diagnosis** — how the real cause was tracked down, not just guessed at. **(4) The fix** — what was actually changed. **(5) Why this fix is correct** — the reasoning for why it addresses the real cause, not just a workaround that happened to make the symptom disappear.

### Prisma CLI install failing outright on this machine
**(1)** Ran a plain `npm install -D prisma nodemon` in the backend, expecting npm to grab the latest stable release like any other package. **(2)** The install failed outright: `npm ERR!` from `prisma`'s own preinstall script, refusing to proceed. **(3)** The error message named the actual cause directly — Prisma 7.x requires Node `^20.19 || ^22.12 || >=24.0`, and this machine runs Node `20.12.2`, just short of the 20.19 floor. **(4)** Pinned both `prisma` and `@prisma/client` to `6.19.3` (confirmed via `npm view prisma@6 version`/`engines` to be the latest 6.x release, requiring only Node `>=18.18`) instead of the default-installed 7.9.1. **(5)** This is the correct fix, not a workaround, because it resolves the actual incompatibility (wrong engine requirement for this Node version) without touching Node itself — upgrading Node was out of scope for "initialize the backend," and both packages are still on a genuinely current, supported release line.

### Prisma Studio: "Response from the Engine was empty" on every query
**(1)** The reporting hypothesis was version drift between `prisma` and `@prisma/client` (reasonable, given the 7.x/6.19.3 pinning incident above), or a corrupted/wrong-architecture query engine binary. **(2)** Studio's browser UI threw "Response from the Engine was empty" the moment any model (confirmed on `Factory`) was queried. **(3)** Diagnosed layer by layer instead of guessing: `npx prisma -v` showed `prisma` and `@prisma/client` both at exactly `6.19.3` (no drift); the engine binary at `node_modules/.prisma/client/libquery_engine-darwin-arm64.dylib.node` was the correct size (~19MB, not truncated), a valid Mach-O arm64 binary matching this machine's actual architecture (`uname -m` → arm64), ad-hoc code-signed as expected for Prisma's Mac binaries, with no macOS quarantine flag blocking it, and all its dynamic library dependencies were plain system frameworks (Security, CoreFoundation, libSystem — nothing third-party to fail to resolve). A direct `@prisma/client` query (`prisma.factory.findMany()`) run from a small standalone script succeeded immediately, returning `[]`. To rule out something Studio-specific, traced Studio's own bundled server code (`node_modules/prisma/build/index.js`) to find its internal `/api` JSON-RPC endpoint, then replayed the exact `getDMMF` + `clientRequest` calls a real browser tab sends against a freshly started Studio instance — that succeeded too, returning the empty `Factory` table correctly. A process check also confirmed no stale Studio process was left running from whatever earlier session hit the error. **(4)** No code change was needed — every layer (dependency versions, the engine binary itself, the live database connection, and Studio's own request/response protocol) checked out working correctly against a fresh instance. **(5)** Since the identical schema, database, and engine binary succeed end-to-end when driven fresh, the original error couldn't have come from a persistent defect in this project's dependencies or files — the most consistent explanation is transient/stale state from the earlier session (e.g. a Studio tab left open from before the migration existed, whose cached schema hash no longer matched the database, or a since-closed Studio process in a bad state). If this recurs: fully close old Studio browser tabs/processes and relaunch fresh with `npx prisma studio --schema=src/prisma/schema.prisma`.

### Login endpoint returned 404 "Cannot POST /api/auth/login" despite the route being mounted correctly
**(1)** After adding `app.use('/api/auth', authRoutes)` to `server.js`, started the server in the background and curled `/api/auth/login` to test it end to end. **(2)** Got a 404 HTML error page ("Cannot POST /api/auth/login") — as if the route didn't exist at all, even though `server.js` clearly had it mounted. **(3)** `lsof -i :3001` showed a *different*, older node process already bound to port 3001, left running from an earlier session (probably the backend-init task's manual server test). Since that port was already taken, the freshly started server process silently failed to bind, and every curl request was actually hitting the stale old process — which predates the auth routes entirely. **(4)** Killed the stale process (`kill <pid>`), confirmed the port was free with `lsof`, then restarted the server. **(5)** This is the correct fix, not a workaround, because the code was never actually broken — the bug was in test hygiene (not verifying the port was free before starting a new instance), not in `server.js`. Re-ran the full test suite (login, `/me` with a valid/missing/garbage token, wrong password, unknown username) against the properly-started server and every case passed.

### Test cleanup failed on a foreign-key error when deleting a throwaway test Product
**(1)** After the full Products test suite passed, the test script's own cleanup step tried `prisma.product.deleteMany({ where: { factoryId: factory.id } })` to remove the throwaway test product before deleting the test Factory. **(2)** Postgres rejected it: `update or delete on table "Product" violates RESTRICT setting of foreign key constraint "ProductSize_productId_fkey"` — the delete never ran. **(3)** The cause was in the error message itself: each test `Product` had `ProductSize` rows (created via the nested `sizes: { create: [...] }` in `createProduct`) pointing back at it via a foreign key with the default `RESTRICT` behavior, which blocks deleting a parent row while children still reference it. **(4)** Deleted the `ProductSize` rows for each test product first, then the `Product` rows, then the `Factory`, then the test `User` — respecting the actual dependency order instead of deleting parent-first. **(5)** This is the correct fix, not a workaround: `RESTRICT` (Prisma's schema default when a relation doesn't specify `onDelete`) is doing exactly its job here, and it's the *right* job for this schema — silently cascading a `Product` delete into deleting its `ProductSize` rows would be a real footgun in the live app (accidentally deleting an article's size list by deleting the article), so the fix was to respect the ordering the constraint is enforcing, not to weaken the constraint.

### Downgraded react-router to "fix" a vulnerability, in the wrong direction
**(1)** `npm install` reported 2 high-severity advisories. Ran `npm audit`, which printed one advisory — "RSC Mode CSRF Bypass", vulnerable range `7.12.0 - 8.2.0` — and suggested `react-router-dom@7.11.0`. Since we don't use RSC mode at all, and 7.11 is below the vulnerable range, pinning down to `7.11.0` looked like a clean way to clear the advisory without carrying a "doesn't apply to us" justification. **(2)** After reinstalling at 7.11.0, npm *still* reported 2 high-severity advisories — but now the vulnerable range printed as `7.0.0-pre.0 - 7.11.0`, with the fix being to *upgrade* to 7.18.2. The two suggestions pointed in opposite directions, which is what made it obvious something was being missed rather than genuinely unfixable. **(3)** `npm audit`'s human-readable output only surfaces a summarised subset. Ran `npm audit --json` and parsed every advisory individually: there were **14**, not 1. Most are SSR/RSC-specific (ScrollRestoration SSR, prerendered redirect HTML, single-fetch, `__manifest`, Server Actions, SSR hydration) and genuinely don't apply to a client-side-only SPA. But `GHSA-wrjc-x8rr-h8h6` — "Open redirect via backslash in `<Link>` and `useNavigate`", range `>=6.0.0 <7.18.0` — very much does: `useNavigate` is used on the login screen. **(4)** Went *up* to `^7.18.2` instead of down. Re-checked: exactly one advisory remains at that version (the RSC-mode CSRF one), and since no v8 has been published yet, it has no available fix — but it's categorically N/A to this app, which uses no React Server Components. **(5)** This is the correct fix because it's driven by which advisories actually apply to *this* app's usage, rather than by which single number `npm audit` chose to print first. The original reasoning wasn't wrong about RSC mode being irrelevant — it was wrong to conclude anything from one advisory without checking whether it was the only one. The general lesson: `npm audit`'s summary view is a starting point, not the full picture; when a suggested fix is a *downgrade*, that's a strong signal to read the complete advisory list before acting, because downgrading almost always steps back into older unfixed issues.

### jsdom (installed only for testing) failed to load on this Node version
**(1)** To verify the React app actually mounts and renders — something `vite build` can't prove, since it only checks that modules parse and resolve — installed `jsdom` in the scratchpad directory (deliberately not in the project, to keep its dependencies clean) to run the real built bundle in a DOM. **(2)** The test script crashed immediately on import: `ERR_REQUIRE_ESM` from `html-encoding-sniffer`, a transitive dependency, trying to `require()` an ESM-only module. **(3)** Same root cause as the Prisma 7 and Vite 7 incidents: `require()`-ing an ES module only works on Node 22+, and this machine runs Node 20.12.2. The latest jsdom's dependency tree assumes the newer Node. **(4)** Pinned `jsdom@25`, whose tree predates that change. **(5)** Correct fix for the same reason as the Prisma pin — it resolves the actual Node-version incompatibility without upgrading Node (out of scope) or abandoning the verification. Worth noting this is the *third* time this exact class of problem has appeared in this project (Prisma 7, Vite 7, jsdom); checking `npm view <pkg> engines` before installing has become a standing habit as a result, and it's why Vite was pinned to 6 proactively rather than after a failure.

### Test script reported real failures that weren't real — a fixed sleep was too short for a live network call
**(1)** The Receive Stock test followed the pattern used successfully in every earlier jsdom test: boot the bundle, `await sleep(1200)`, then assert. That fixed delay had been enough in every previous test (login, Home, refresh). **(2)** This time, checks against the Factory/Location dropdowns failed — the options list was still just the "Loading…" placeholder — but a later check in the *same run*, several hundred milliseconds further in, showed the real factory names present in the page text. That inconsistency (failing early, passing later, same data) was the tell that this wasn't a real bug, just a race between the assertion and the app. **(3)** Wrote a throwaway debug script that logged every `fetch` call and its resolution time: `GET /api/auth/me` (the rehydration call every protected page makes) has to resolve *before* `GET /api/factories`/`GET /api/locations` even start, since they're triggered by a separate effect that only matters once the page is confirmed authenticated — and all three round-trip to a real, non-local Neon Postgres instance, not an in-memory mock. On a cold process (no warmed connection), that chain sometimes took longer than 1200ms. **(4)** Replaced every fixed `sleep()` that was waiting on a network-backed state change with a `waitFor(condition, { timeout, interval })` poll — checking every 100ms up to a generous ceiling (6s) instead of guessing a single delay. Left plain `sleep()` only where the wait was for a synchronous React state update (e.g. after clicking "Change"), which doesn't depend on network timing at all. **(5)** This is the correct fix, not just a bigger number: a longer fixed sleep (e.g. 3000ms) would have "worked" today but reintroduced the identical flakiness the moment a slower network day or a colder connection pushed past whatever number was picked — a poll succeeds as soon as the real condition is true and fails loudly (with a labelled timeout error) only if it genuinely never becomes true, which is what a test assertion should actually be checking.

### Guessed the wrong Prisma error field for a P2003 foreign-key violation, in a way that misrouted the error rather than crashing
**(1)** Round 12 made `Product.categoryId` a required foreign key, alongside the already-existing required `factoryId`. Both throw the identical `P2003` code on violation, so `createProduct`'s catch block needed to tell them apart — exactly the same problem `P2002` already had for `DUPLICATE_SIZE` vs. `DUPLICATE_ARTICLE`, solved there by reading `err.meta.target`. By analogy, wrote `err.meta?.field_name?.includes?.('categoryId')`, since "field_name" is the name used in some Prisma documentation and older error shapes for this purpose. **(2)** A dedicated test posting a real `factoryId` with a deliberately bogus `categoryId` got back `404 FACTORY_NOT_FOUND` instead of `404 CATEGORY_NOT_FOUND` — wrong, but not a crash, which is exactly why writing a real test for this (not just eyeballing the code) mattered: a silently-wrong error code is invisible unless something actually asserts on it. **(3)** Wrote a two-line throwaway script that triggered the exact same P2003 directly and printed `JSON.stringify(err.meta)` in full, rather than guessing again. The actual shape on this Prisma version (6.19.3) is `{ modelName: "Product", constraint: "Product_categoryId_fkey" }` — no `field_name` key exists at all, so the `.includes?.()` optional-chain silently evaluated to `undefined` (falsy) on every call, always falling through to the `FACTORY_NOT_FOUND` branch regardless of which FK actually failed. **(4)** Switched to `err.meta?.constraint?.includes?.('categoryId')`, matching the field genuinely present in the error. **(5)** This is the right fix because it's based on an observed error object, not a remembered field name — Prisma's exact `meta` shape for a given error code isn't part of its stable public API and has changed across versions/error codes before (P2002 uses `target`, P2003 uses `constraint`, not the same key). The general lesson, worth applying to any future `err.meta` branch: dump the real error once with a throwaway script before writing the condition, don't pattern-match from a different error code or from memory — and prefer a query that would fail loudly (wrong branch, wrong status code) over one that fails silently, which optional chaining on a guessed field name does by design.

### The regression sweep caught two of its own prior-task tests asserting a now-superseded snapshot
**(1)** After finishing the Round 12 migration and the productController.js compatibility patch, ran the full regression sweep as standard practice. Two failures: `test-schema-migration.mjs`'s enum check, and (after fixing that) the same file's CHECK-constraint fixture crashing outright with "Argument categoryId is missing." **(2)** Both were Round 11 artifacts that Round 12 legitimately superseded, not regressions in this task's own work. The enum check asserted `TransactionType` was *exactly* `[STOCK_IN, STOCK_OUT, DEFECT_RETURN, PARTY_RETURN]` — true when written, no longer true now that `TRANSFER_OUT`/`TRANSFER_IN` correctly exist too. The fixture crash was different in kind: that test creates its `Product` fixture via **raw Prisma**, not through the API, so it never went through `productController.js`'s new "default to Others" fallback at all — that fallback is application logic, not a database default, and a direct `prisma.product.create()` call bypasses application logic entirely by construction. **(3)** Confirmed both diagnoses before touching anything: read the exact assertion text against the current, correct enum list for the first; read the stack trace's exact `Argument categoryId is missing` message (identical wording to the pre-patch `createProduct` crash from earlier in this same task) for the second, then grepped every other test file for direct `.product.create(` calls to confirm no other file had the same latent gap. **(4)** Updated the enum assertion to the current six-value list, and added an explicit `categoryId` (looked up via `findFirst({ where: { name: 'Others' } })`) to the raw fixture creation. **(5)** Same principle as the earlier receipt-table and Save-receipt entries in this log: a later task legitimately changing something an earlier task's test snapshotted isn't a regression in the later task, but the sweep is exactly the mechanism meant to surface it, and it needs a real fix (update the assertion to current truth) rather than being waved off as "expected, ignore it." The raw-Prisma-fixture case is the more general lesson here: a required-field default implemented in a controller protects only requests that go through that controller — any other caller of the same model (another controller, a raw script, a future background job) needs its own value, and grepping for other direct model-creation call sites is the way to find them before they surface as a production crash instead of a test failure.

### "+ Create new color" had been throwing an error into its own UI on every successful use
**(1)** While deferring Colour creation, `handleCreateColor` needed rewriting anyway, so its contract with `Combobox` got read properly for the first time rather than assumed. The old version did its work and returned nothing — it created the Color, appended it to `globalColors`, and called `handleColorChange(color.id, color)` itself to select it. Selecting it directly looked right, and there was even a good documented reason for it (passing `knownColor` avoids reading a state value that hasn't re-rendered yet). **(2)** Reading `Combobox.jsx`'s `triggerCreate` showed it does `const created = await onCreate(trimmed); onChange(created.id);` — so with `onCreate` returning `undefined`, `created.id` throws `Cannot read properties of undefined`, lands in `triggerCreate`'s own `catch`, and renders a `createError` banner. The colour genuinely was created and selected (by `handleCreateColor`'s own call), so the visible result was a working action with a spurious red error underneath it — which is exactly why it had survived: the thing the person wanted did happen. **(3)** Confirmed by reading both sides of the contract rather than inferring from behaviour, then verified the fix empirically — the post-change browser run creating a brand-new colour reported zero console errors and the combobox input correctly showing the new colour's name. **(4)** `handleCreateColor` now returns the colour object and does NOT select it; `Combobox`'s own `onChange(created.id)` drives selection through the one normal path. **(5)** This is the correct fix rather than "also return the object and keep selecting it too", because that version would have run `handleColorChange` **twice** for one create — and its first act is `setStagedColors(prev => finalizeActiveColor(prev))`, which reads `selectedColorId`/`currentSets` from a closure that is still stale on the second call within the same tick. The active colour would have been folded into the staged list twice, silently doubling a real received quantity. Having exactly one path perform selection removes the double-invocation entirely instead of trying to make a doubled invocation safe. The general lesson: when a component awaits your callback and then uses its return value, that return value is part of the contract — returning nothing isn't neutral.

### Two selector bugs in the Receive Stock browser test, one of which would have made the test prove nothing
**(1)** The first test script waited for `.stepper` to confirm a colour had been selected, and clicked the inline create row via `page.locator('li,div,button').filter({ hasText: /Create new color/ })`. Both read as reasonable: `.stepper` is the Sets control that appears once a colour is picked, and filtering by the create row's visible text seemed more robust than depending on a class name. **(2)** The second script timed out waiting for `.stepper` after clicking create. **(3)** Two separate causes. The click failure: `.filter({ hasText })` matches any element *containing* that text, so the first match in DOM order was an ancestor `<div>` wrapping the dropdown, not the `<li>` itself — clicking the wrapper does nothing. The wait was the more serious one: grepping `className="stepper` in `ReceiveStock.jsx` showed `SizeStepperChip` also renders `.stepper` (as `stepper size-stepper`), so on any path where the size chips are still on screen, `.stepper` is *already present before the action being waited for* — the exact failure mode CLAUDE.md warns about, where a `waitFor` predicate that's already true passes instantly and asserts against stale state. **(4)** Clicked `.combobox-create-row` directly, and re-keyed the wait onto `.checkbox-field` ("Damaged on arrival"), which renders only while a colour is actually selected and does not exist before it. **(5)** This is the right fix for the wait specifically because it satisfies the rule's actual requirement — false in the starting state, true only in the target state — rather than just being a different selector that happened to work in the one script that failed. The first script's `.stepper` wait had *passed*, and that's the point: it passed because the run happened to reach a state where the colour genuinely was selected, not because the wait verified anything. A wait that can pass by coincidence gives no signal at all on the run where the thing it's checking is broken.


### A New Order assertion reported the archived article was accepted, when it was correctly rejected
**(1)** The archived-visibility test needed to confirm New Order refuses an archived article. After clicking Search, it waited for either the error text OR a resolved article, expressing the second half as `document.querySelector('.lookup-banner-success, .combobox-input') !== null` — reasoning that a successfully resolved article shows a colour Combobox. **(2)** The test printed `New Order — rejects archived article: false`, which would have meant the `p.isActive` filter wasn't working. Suspicious rather than conclusive: the filter is three lines of very direct code with its own rule-85 comment, so a failure there would have been surprising. **(3)** Re-ran the exact same steps in a throwaway script that just dumped the page text after a fixed pause, and the page plainly read "No active article found with number PROBE-DEFER-02." — the app was right and the test was wrong. The cause was in the wait predicate: New Order's **party picker** is itself a Combobox, so `.combobox-input` already exists on the page before any article search happens. The `||` was therefore true on the first poll, the wait returned immediately, and the assertion read the page before the search had resolved. **(4)** Re-keyed the wait onto two conditions that are both genuinely false beforehand: the error text (doesn't exist until the search fails) or `input[placeholder="e.g. A101"]`'s `disabled` property (the article input is only disabled once a product actually resolves). **(5)** This is the correct fix rather than "add a longer timeout" or "wait for a different element" because it restores the property the wait is supposed to have — CLAUDE.md's own rule that a `waitFor` predicate must be false in the starting state. The failure mode here is the one that rule exists for and is worth naming precisely: the test didn't crash or hang, it produced a confident, plausible, WRONG answer about application behaviour, and the only reason it got caught was that the answer happened to contradict code that was easy to re-read. A predicate that can pass by coincidence is not a weaker test, it's an unreliable narrator — and a shared component appearing in an unrelated part of the same screen is an easy way to introduce one without noticing.
---

### A jsdom test clicked a submit button and nothing happened — the form's own onSubmit never ran
**(1)** Every earlier test dispatched a `submit` event directly on the `<form>` element to trigger a submission (established from the very first Login screen test, and repeated correctly in every test since). Writing the Manage Users create-account test, tried something new: dispatching a `click` on the submit *button* instead, assuming — as in a real browser — that clicking a `type="submit"` button inside a form triggers the form's submission automatically. **(2)** After the click, nothing happened: no error banner, no new row in the list, the form fields still held their typed values. Silent failure, not a thrown error, which made it look at first like the account-creation code itself might be broken. **(3)** Wrote a small standalone debug script that filled the same fields and logged the DOM state before and after the click — confirming the button was found, was not disabled, and the click did fire — but the form's `handleCreate` never ran (no fetch went out, verified via the same debug script). Swapping only the interaction — dispatching `submit` on the `<form>` instead of `click` on the button — with everything else identical, worked immediately. **(4)** Reverted the test to the pattern already used everywhere else in this project: dispatch `submit` on the form element directly. **(5)** This is the correct fix, not a workaround, because it wasn't a bug to fix in the app at all — jsdom doesn't run a full browser's HTML forms engine, and the implicit "clicking a submit button submits its enclosing form" behavior is exactly the kind of native-browser behavior a DOM-only simulation isn't guaranteed to reproduce. The lesson generalizes: when a jsdom test needs to trigger form submission, dispatch `submit` on the `<form>` itself — it's both more reliable across jsdom's actual feature support and closer to testing the thing that matters (the `onSubmit` handler), rather than an intermediate browser mechanism jsdom doesn't fully implement.

### POST /api/products silently dropped isKids — the column existed, the endpoint never read it
**(1)** Started building the New-article form's Kids toggle, assuming `POST /api/products` already accepted and persisted `isKids` the same way it accepted `category` — a reasonable assumption, since the `Product.isKids` column has existed in the schema since Phase 1 and `PATCH /api/products/:id` already lists it as an editable field. **(2)** Re-reading `createProduct` in full before wiring up the frontend (not after hitting a bug — checked proactively, the same habit established after the earlier `Product.name` and `login()`/`me()` gaps) showed `isKids` was never destructured from the request body, never written to `data`, and never included in `productSelect`'s returned fields — meaning even if a client somehow got it into the create request, it would be silently discarded, and a client couldn't verify it either way since it was never in the response. **(3)** Confirmed by reading the code directly, not by reproducing a failure — the gap was structural and visible on inspection. **(4)** Added `isKids` to `createProduct`'s destructured fields, its `data` object (coerced with `!!isKids` so an omitted field cleanly defaults to `false`, matching the schema default), and to `productSelect` so every read of a Product — list, single, and the response from creating one — actually reflects it. Updated `04_API_SPEC.md`'s documented request/response shapes to match, same as the pattern already established for `Product.name`. **(5)** This is the right fix, not a workaround, because `isKids` isn't a new concept being introduced — it's a column and an editing capability that already existed everywhere except the one endpoint that needed it for a feature being built right now; wiring it up completes something that was already half-built rather than inventing new surface area.

### A jsdom test checked bundle-count too early because handleColorChange became async without the test noticing
**(1)** Wrote the New-article color-staging test following the exact pattern from the earlier Matched-branch test: switch color, wait for `.staged-row` to appear (proof the previous color was finalized), then check state. **(2)** Two bundle-count assertions failed — checking for 2 Bundles (X and Y) found only 1, and checking for 3 (X, Y, Z) found only 2 — consistent with a bundle-creation call not having landed yet, not with the app creating the wrong number of Bundles. **(3)** The Matched-branch `handleColorChange` this test's structure was copied from is fully synchronous; the New-article version built in this same task is not — it awaits `createBundle` before finishing. `setStagedColors` (which produces the `.staged-row` the test was waiting for) fires at the very TOP of the function, before that await, so waiting for the staged row proves the PREVIOUS color was finalized but says nothing about whether the NEW color's Bundle creation has actually completed yet. **(4)** Added a wait for `.stepper-value` to show `'0'` (the new color's reset sets count) before trusting any bundle-count check — that value is only set on the LAST line of `handleColorChange`, after the await, so seeing it is proof the whole async function, Bundle creation included, has genuinely finished. **(5)** Correct fix because it targets the actual invariant that matters (has this specific async operation completed) rather than a fixed delay that would be exactly as fragile as the sleep-based timing bug logged earlier in this project — the general lesson repeating here: whenever a function's synchronicity changes, everything that tests it by watching its FIRST visible side effect (rather than its LAST) needs re-checking, not just its assertions.

### A jsdom test couldn't check a checkbox — two different wrong techniques before finding the one that works
**(1)** Writing the first test in this project to interact with a checkbox, reached for the same technique already proven for text inputs and selects: grab the property descriptor's real setter (bypassing React's tracked setter), assign the new value, then dispatch a `change` event. **(2)** The staged row never showed the "Damaged" badge — but the checkbox itself reported `checked: true` immediately after the interaction, which was misleading: React's controlled `checked={currentDamaged}` prop had NOT actually updated, and the very next re-render (triggered by an unrelated action) silently reverted the DOM back to unchecked, proving the underlying state was never really touched. **(3)** Wrote a standalone debug script to isolate the interaction from the rest of the test, and along the way discovered a SECOND, unrelated bug: the debug script itself had no `try`/`finally`, so an earlier crashed run had left orphaned "Debug CB..." Factory/Location/Color/Product rows in the real database — and the `Location` uniqueness constraint then silently 409'd the debug script's own next fixture-creation call, poisoning `location.id` with `undefined` and producing a confusing, unrelated-looking timeout. Cleaned that up and rebuilt the debug script with proper cleanup and timestamp-unique names before continuing. **(4)** With a clean debug environment, tried dispatching a manually-constructed `Event('click')` instead of `change` — still no effect on React's state. Only calling the checkbox's real `.click()` **method** (not a synthetic dispatched event) worked, confirmed by the checkbox surviving an unrelated re-render still checked. **(5)** This is the correct technique, not a lucky guess: jsdom only runs a form element's native "activation behavior" (the browser-native effect of interacting with it — a checkbox actually toggling, a submit button actually submitting its form) in response to the real DOM methods (`.click()`, `.submit()`) or genuinely trusted user-generated events, not an event object manually constructed and dispatched by test code. `Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(...).set` works for text-input `value` because React's own value-tracking hooks the *setter itself*, independent of native activation behavior — but a checkbox's meaningful state change (and React seeing it) depends on that native toggle actually happening, which only `.click()` triggers. The general lesson, now proven across three different failure modes in this project (buttons, checkboxes, and — implicitly — anything else with native activation behavior): when simulating a real interaction in jsdom, prefer the real DOM method over reconstructing individual events by hand, and verify a state-changing interaction actually stuck by checking it survives an unrelated subsequent re-render, not just by reading `.checked`/`.value` immediately afterward.

### The regression sweep caught three tests still checking for text this task deliberately removed
**(1)** Building the real receipt table meant removing the placeholder line ("N articles added to this receipt so far") that three earlier tasks' tests had been asserting against as their proof that a commit succeeded — a reasonable thing for those tests to check at the time, since it was the only visible evidence a commit had landed. **(2)** After finishing the table and running the full regression sweep (standard practice after every task, regardless of how confident the diff looks), three files threw instead of cleanly failing — `waitFor` timeouts on that exact placeholder text, which no longer exists anywhere in the rendered page. **(3)** Confirmed by reading each failure: all three were waiting on text this task's own diff had deleted, not a sign of anything the new code got wrong. **(4)** Updated each to check for the real table instead (`.receipt-group` count, row count) — and for one of them (the damaged-flag test), used the opportunity to close a real, previously-acknowledged gap: that test could only verify the damaged flag reached the pre-commit staging list, not the actual committed entry, since nothing rendered `receiptItems` at the time it was written. With the real table now in place, it directly asserts the Damaged badge on the committed row. **(5)** Same shape as the earlier `POST /api/products` price-gate task's stale-test fix: a deliberate change in one task breaking an assertion an earlier task's test made about the UI's exact prior shape isn't a regression in the code, but it's real work the regression sweep exists to surface — and it's specifically why the sweep checks *every* prior test file, not just ones that seem related to the current change.

### A jsdom test clicked the wrong "Save receipt" button — the modal's confirm button and the page's trigger button share the same text
**(1)** Wrote the Save Receipt test using the same `findButton(doc, text)` helper already proven across every earlier test — search the whole document for a button whose text matches. **(2)** The test hung waiting for the second confirm modal's own click to take effect; nothing happened after the click that was supposed to submit the receipt. **(3)** `ConfirmModal` overlays the page rather than replacing it — the page's own "Save receipt" button (the one that opens the FIRST modal) stays mounted in the DOM the whole time the modal is open, and its confirm button's label was deliberately chosen to also read "Save receipt," so there were genuinely two buttons with identical text on screen at once. `querySelectorAll('button')` returns them in DOM order, and the page's button — rendered earlier in the JSX than the modals — came first, so `findButton` kept finding and re-clicking the WRONG one (which does nothing new, since the first modal was already showing). **(4)** Added a second helper, `findModalButton`, scoped to `.modal-scrim` specifically, and used it for every button click that's meant to target whichever modal is currently open. **(5)** This is the right fix, not a narrower workaround (like renaming the modal's button), because the ambiguity is a structural fact about how the UI is built — a confirm modal overlaying its own trigger — not a naming mistake to correct away; the modal's label intentionally matches the page button's label since both describe the same action to the user. The general lesson: a text-based button lookup that's reliable everywhere else can silently break the moment two on-screen elements legitimately share a label, which is common with confirm-modal patterns specifically, since the whole point is that the modal's own action button usually restates the triggering action.

### A killed test process orphaned its fixtures, which then silently inflated a LATER run's "exact total" assertions
**(1)** Ran the full regression sweep (all test files in a loop) the same way as every previous task, with a fixed 2-minute shell timeout on the whole batch. **(2)** The batch hit that timeout while the new Live Stock test was mid-run — its process was killed before its `finally` cleanup block could execute, leaving that run's Factories/Products/Colors/Locations/Stock genuinely orphaned in the real (shared) dev database. Running the Live Stock test again immediately afterward, several assertions that check EXACT totals (`Total sets = 11`, `exactly 2 factory sections`) failed with suspiciously doubled numbers (22, 4 sections). **(3)** The doubled numbers were the tell — not a logic bug (which wouldn't double so cleanly), but leftover data from an identically-shaped previous run still present in the database this new run's queries also read from. Confirmed by querying for factories matching this test's naming pattern and finding two full sets of fixtures instead of one. **(4)** Deleted the orphaned rows directly, then restructured the test itself so this class of failure can't recur: nearly every check now runs after first searching for this run's own unique timestamp tag (embedded in every fixture's name), scoping the visible page state down to exactly this test's own data before asserting anything about counts or totals, rather than asserting global totals that implicitly assume the shared database is otherwise empty. **(5)** This is the right fix, not a one-off cleanup, because Live Stock View is structurally different from every other screen tested so far in this project: every earlier test's assertions were already naturally scoped (filtered by a specific `productId`/`bundleId` the test itself created), where this is the first screen whose entire purpose is showing everything, unfiltered, system-wide — meaning naive "exact total" assertions against it were never actually safe against a shared, long-lived dev database, independent of whether a process gets killed. The dedicated tag-search step makes the test correct regardless of what else exists in the database, not just resilient to this one incident.

### `@@check(...)` was specified in the schema doc, but no such thing exists in Prisma
**(1)** Round 11 of `03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md` specified `@@check(qtySets >= 0, name: "stock_qty_sets_non_negative")` on `Stock`, with a note to verify it compiles before relying on it. It looked entirely plausible — it sits alongside `@@unique` and `@@index`, follows their exact naming convention, and CHECK constraints are a completely standard Postgres feature, so a schema language that already exposes unique constraints and indexes exposing check constraints too is a reasonable expectation. **(2)** `prisma validate` rejected it: `P1012 — Error validating: This line is not a valid field or attribute definition`. **(3)** Two details in that error message distinguish "this version doesn't support it yet" from "this has never existed." The error is a *parse* error about the line not being a valid definition at all — not a semantic complaint like "unknown attribute `@@check`" or "requires preview feature X," which is what Prisma emits for a real-but-unavailable feature. So the parser doesn't recognise the token whatsoever; there's no preview flag that would turn it on. Isolated it in a throwaway two-field schema first, so nothing else in the real schema could be confounding the result. **(4)** Wrote the constraint as raw SQL appended to the migration file instead: `ALTER TABLE "Stock" ADD CONSTRAINT "stock_qty_sets_non_negative" CHECK ("qtySets" >= 0);`, and replaced the `@@check` line in both `schema.prisma` and the schema doc with a comment explaining where the constraint actually lives and why it can't be declared inline. **(5)** This is the correct fix rather than dropping the constraint, because the *intent* — a database-level guarantee that stock can never go negative — is fully achievable; only Prisma's declarative syntax for it is missing. The genuine risk with hand-written SQL in a Prisma project is that the migration engine later sees the constraint as "drift" (something in the database that the schema doesn't describe) and generates a migration dropping it. That's not a hypothetical worry, so it was tested rather than assumed: running `prisma migrate diff` against both databases *after* applying returned "This is an empty migration," proving the engine doesn't model CHECK constraints at all and therefore leaves this one permanently alone. The one real cost is invisibility — someone reading `schema.prisma` won't see it — which the comment on the `Stock` model is there to mitigate. And crucially the constraint was verified to actually *work*, not just to exist: a raw SQL `UPDATE` setting `qtySets = -1` (deliberately bypassing every application-layer guard, since that's the only path this constraint exists to catch) was rejected by Postgres naming the constraint, with the row left unchanged.

### A regression sweep failure that was a genuine flake — and why that took a second run to establish rather than assume
**(1)** After the schema migration and sample-tracking removal, ran the full regression sweep as usual. **(2)** `test-inline-create.mjs` failed with `waitFor timed out after 6000ms: sets stepper appears` — the point where picking a color should trigger auto-Bundle-creation and reveal the quantity stepper. **(3)** The tempting read was "this task broke something," but the specifics argued against it: that test touches colors, bundles and inline creation, none of which this task's diff goes near, and it failed on a *timeout* rather than a wrong value or an error response. Ran the file in isolation — 21/21 passed, and the test server's log showed no error at all. Then re-ran the entire sweep in the same order — 452/452, the exact pre-task baseline. **(4)** No code change; recorded as a flake caused by a 6-second `waitFor` losing a race against a slow round-trip to the remote Neon database while the sweep was hammering it. **(5)** Recording this honestly rather than quietly re-running until green matters, because the two possible explanations — "a real regression" and "a timing flake" — demand opposite responses, and a single green run doesn't distinguish them. What actually distinguishes them is evidence: the isolated pass, the clean server log, the total matching the known baseline exactly, and the failure mode being a timeout on a code path unrelated to the diff. Worth noting the flake is a real (if minor) weakness in the test, not purely bad luck: `waitFor` polling is the right approach per this project's own rule, but a 6-second ceiling is tight for a network round-trip to a remote database under load. It's left as-is for now, since raising every timeout to paper over one observed flake would slow the whole suite and blunt its ability to catch genuine hangs.

### Chasing a "race condition" that did not exist, and finding a permanent bug underneath it
**(1)** Two tests failed intermittently (2 of 5 regression sweeps) with the color dropdown reading "All colors staged" while disabled. That state is only reachable when `colorBootstrapMode` is false while the loading flags have settled, so the obvious reading was a race: `setColorBootstrapMode(true)` happens in the fetch's `.then`, `setResolvedColorsLoading(false)` in its `.finally`, and if those two landed in separate renders there'd be a window with bootstrap still false and loading already finished. Intermittent failure plus two state updates in different promise callbacks is a genuinely reasonable case for suspecting an ordering race. **(2)** The hypothesis survived nothing. 60 repeated runs of the exact failing scenario in isolation produced zero failures; sampling the DOM every 25ms never caught the bad state; keeping JSDOM instances alive to amplify contention did nothing. **(3)** What finally worked was abandoning inference and logging the actual ordering — a temporary `RSLOG` recording every state transition and every render's derived values, plus a dump hook on the real test so a failure would print its own timeline. The captured log immediately killed the hypothesis: `.then` and `.finally` flush in the *same* microtask batch, so bootstrap and loading always land in one render — the window being hunted cannot open. The same log showed the real culprit one render earlier: `611.84ms RENDER {lookup:"matched", bootstrap:false, resLoading:false, resN:0, disabled:true}`, then `617.23ms resolvedEffect:START`. The color UI renders the instant the lookup resolves, but `resolvedColorsLoading` starts as `false` and only becomes `true` inside an effect, which runs *after* that render commits. Measuring it across 24 runs: the gap is present in **100%** of lookups, lasting 2-6ms. It was never a race; it is a deterministic bug on every single lookup. The only random part was whether the tests' 100ms poll happened to sample inside it. The dump also corrected a second mistake — the failing check was in step [3] (an article that *has* colors), not step [2] as previously reported, proving the gap wasn't specific to the colorless path. **(4)** Replaced both loading booleans with explicit `'idle' | 'loading' | 'loaded'` statuses and gated the placeholder and `disabled` on `colorListsSettled` (positively: "has everything this article depends on finished") instead of on "nothing is currently in flight." **(5)** This is the correct fix rather than raising the tests' poll timeout or initialising the boolean to `true`, because the defect is a **representation** problem, not a timing one: a boolean can only encode "fetching right now", so "not asked yet" and "asked, answered, empty" collapse into the same `false`, and the UI was forced to guess. Adding the missing state makes the bad render unrepresentable rather than merely unlikely. Initialising the boolean to `true` would have papered over the same hole — it fixes this one screen's first render while leaving "finished and empty" still indistinguishable from "never started" for every later reset. Loosening the tests would have been worse still: they were correctly reporting a real defect a user can see.

### Proving a fix by measurement, not just by the suite turning green
**(1)** After the three-state fix the full sweep passed 508/508 with no test file modified, which is the normal bar for "done" in this project. **(2)** That bar was not sufficient here. The bug had a ~4% chance of being observed per lookup, so a green suite is exactly what a *broken* build looks like most of the time — the suite had passed 3 of 5 sweeps while the bug was present. **(3)** Built a sampler that polls the real shipped bundle (no instrumentation) roughly every millisecond throughout a lookup and records every distinct visible state, then — critically — ran it against a deliberately built **control** bundle whose gate used the old "nothing in flight" semantics with everything else identical. **(4)** The control flagged a premature claim in **20/20** runs; the fixed bundle showed **0/30** across ~562 samples per run, with the sequence collapsing to just `Loading… → correct terminal state`. **(5)** The control is what makes the zero meaningful: without it, "0 bad states" is equally consistent with "the fix works" and "the sampler cannot see a 4ms window", and those are indistinguishable from the output alone. Two sampler flaws surfaced and were fixed only because the control's numbers looked wrong first — a tight `setImmediate` loop starved Node's poll phase so fetches never completed, and the settle-detection accepted "stably showing Loading…" as a terminal state. Both would have produced a falsely clean result on the fixed bundle. The general lesson: when verifying a fix for an intermittent defect, first prove the instrument can detect the defect, or a negative result measures nothing.

### A "hanging" test that failed at the same line every time was two bugs, not a flake — plain typos, found by not assuming
**(1)** Writing the new category-picker test, chained a size-chip click straight into a "Create article" click with zero gap in two places, matching what looked like the same pattern used successfully elsewhere in the suite. Both hung, `waitFor` timing out after 6s. **(2)** The instinct from the immediately preceding investigation (the color-picker race that turned out not to be a race) was to suspect a flake and just retry — but this failed at the *exact same line*, every single run, three runs in a row. A flake is inconsistent by definition; a deterministic failure at a fixed point is a real bug, and treating it as "probably just flaky, run it again" would have been the wrong lesson to take from that earlier investigation, not the right one. **(3)** First bug: comparing the actual passing pattern elsewhere in the suite (`test-receive-new-article.mjs`) showed every existing "click a size, then Create" sequence has a `waitFor` on `.pieces-readout` updating *in between* the two clicks — never a bare double-click. Two synchronous `click()` calls with nothing awaited between them can let the second one's handler run against a still-stale pre-first-click React closure, since nothing forced a render to commit first. Second bug, found only after fixing the first and hitting a *new* failure at a *different* fixed line: the fixed `waitFor` predicate checked for the literal text `"= 1 pieces per set"`, but the component's own pluralization logic (`piece{n===1?'':'s'}`) renders `"= 1 piece per set"` — singular, no `s` — at exactly `n=1`. A plain string mismatch, never going to resolve no matter how long the timeout. **(4)** Added the missing `waitFor(() => pieces-readout includes ...)` between the two clicks (matching the established idiom instead of inventing a new one), and corrected the singular/plural string. **(5)** Both fixes are correct because each was confirmed against a concrete, checkable fact — the DOM's actual rendered text for singular vs. plural, and the codebase's own existing pattern for sequencing these two clicks — not by guessing and re-running until green. The broader lesson, sitting right next to the previous entry about a bug that only *looked* like a race: the same posture (verify against real output, don't assume) cuts both ways. Don't manufacture a timing explanation for a problem that's really a race (previous entry) — and don't dismiss a genuinely deterministic, same-line-every-time failure as "probably a flake" just because the last investigation happened to end that way. Which one you're looking at is answered by running it more than once and checking whether the failure point moves.

### A throwaway manual probe script with no cleanup broke an unrelated test two files later in the sweep
