**(1)** Before writing the real, formal test for the five new deactivate/reactivate endpoints, ran a couple of quick one-off scripts against the running server to sanity-check the new routes actually worked — `probe-deactivate.mjs`, creating a real Factory and Color via the API, deactivating and reactivating them, checking a 404 case. Standard practice in this project before committing to a full test file, and the script itself worked exactly as intended. **(2)** It had no `finally` block, unlike every real test file in this suite — it was written as a quick look, not a lasting fixture, so cleanup wasn't part of it. Deleted the file afterward (`rm probe-deactivate.mjs`), which removed the *script* but did nothing about the *rows it had created* in the real TEST database — a Factory and a Color were left behind, silently. **(3)** The next full regression sweep failed one check, in a completely different, untouched file: `test-inline-create.mjs`'s very first assertion, which depends on the TEST database genuinely having zero Colors at that exact point in the sweep (it's specifically testing the "system has zero colors anywhere" message). Confirmed the cause directly rather than guessing: queried the TEST branch's Color table and found exactly one row, named `Probe C <timestamp>` — unmistakably the leftover from the deleted probe script, not a real regression in anything this task touched. **(4)** Deleted both orphaned rows (the Color and the Factory) directly via Prisma, then re-ran the full sweep twice to confirm — clean both times. **(5)** The general lesson, distinct from the "killed process orphans fixtures" entry earlier in this log (that one was about a crash interrupting a `finally` block that DID exist): a script with no cleanup logic at all is a standing liability the moment it creates anything through a real API against a real (even if non-production) database, regardless of how "throwaway" or "just checking" it felt at the time — the script being deleted doesn't undo what it did. Any script that calls a POST/PATCH endpoint against the shared TEST database, even a two-minute sanity check, needs the same `finally`-block discipline as a real test file, or its own manual cleanup immediately after. The five minutes saved by skipping it cost a full extra sweep-and-diagnose cycle to trace down.

### A waitFor that was already satisfied by the PREVIOUS state, so it asserted against stale data
**(1)** The last check of the new Transfer screen test switches the source location from A to B and confirms the list re-scopes to B's stock. Wrote the wait as `waitFor(() => doc.body.textContent.includes('XferDest') || rowTexts(doc).length > 0)` — reasoning that either the new location's name appearing, or rows existing, meant the refetch had landed. **(2)** It failed, reporting location A's rows (`XferNavy… 8 sets`) where B's were expected. **(3)** The predicate was the bug, and the failure output made it obvious rather than mysterious: it printed exactly the *previous* location's content, not an empty or half-rendered list. `rowTexts(doc).length > 0` was **already true at the moment the wait began** — A's rows were still on screen, because React doesn't blank the list while a new fetch is in flight. So `waitFor` returned on its very first poll, before the request for B had even resolved, and the assertion ran against content that was never going to change within that tick. The `||` made it worse by giving two ways to pass early instead of one. **(4)** Rewrote the predicate to wait for the *new* data specifically — `rowTexts(doc).some((t) => t.includes('XferRed…'))` — and added a second assertion that A's signature row is *gone*, so the check now proves the list re-scoped rather than merely that it contains something. **(5)** This is the correct fix because the original predicate was testing "is there anything here," which was true before, during, and after the thing being waited for — it had no power to distinguish the states it was supposed to separate. The general rule this makes concrete, and which the earlier `'Loading…'`-poll bug was a different face of: a `waitFor` condition must be **false in the starting state**, or it isn't a wait at all, it's a no-op that happens to be followed by an assertion. When waiting for a transition from one populated state to another populated state, key the predicate on something unique to the destination state, never on a generic "is it populated yet" that the origin state already satisfies.

### Two specific, confident-sounding claims in a separately-generated handoff document turned out to be untraceable to any real source
**(1)** A follow-up document from a separate review conversation stated two things as verified fact: that real hand-entered dev data (Factories named Comeco, RDX, and Jyoti Creations) had been wiped by test-cleanup logic, prompting a Neon test-branch isolation fix — and that the Kids-toggle color-picking bug's root cause was "no frontend UI ever existed for creating a Color." Both were specific enough (real-sounding factory names, a plausible-sounding root cause) to be worth building on directly, and the natural next step would have been to reference them as established background in future work. **(2)** Asked to verify both against the actual, current `LEARNING_LOG.md` before treating them as fact. Neither is there. A full search of the log for the factory names, "Neon branch," "TEST_DATABASE_URL," "wiped," and "unscoped delete" found no matching incident. A separate search for "no frontend UI," "never existed for creating," and the actual technical terms involved (`colorBootstrapMode`, `resolvedColorsLoading`) found that the log documents a *different*, fully-diagnosed root cause for the color-picking bug (a boolean loading flag unable to represent "not fetched yet," fixed by a three-state status — logged in detail at three separate points). The "no UI ever existed" claim is also directly contradicted by the live codebase: `ReceiveStock.jsx` has a working `handleCreateColor` function and a "+ Create new color" option that predates this claim. **(3)** Diagnosed by treating the log as the sole source of truth for "what actually happened" and searching it exhaustively (multiple keyword variants per claim, not a single grep) before accepting or rejecting either claim, then cross-checking the codebase itself for the more specific, checkable claim. **(4)** Did not incorporate either claim into any doc or future plan. Reported both as unverified findings rather than silently working around them or quietly "fixing" the log to match the handoff document (which would have meant writing history that didn't happen). **(5)** This is the correct response, not excess caution, because both claims were specific and plausible enough that skepticism wasn't the natural first instinct — a vague claim invites scrutiny, a precise one (real-looking names, a coherent-sounding technical mechanism) tends to invite trust instead, which is exactly backwards when the claim's only support is a document generated in a different, unverifiable conversation. The general lesson, an extension of the doc-drift entries above (never treat a revised doc as authoritative without diffing it against the live source): the same applies to *summaries of prior work* — a generated handoff/summary document is not itself evidence, no matter how specific or confident it reads, and any claim from one that matters enough to act on needs to be traced back to the actual log, code, or doc it claims to summarize before it gets treated as settled fact.

### A prior task's six-file doc update wrote to Desktop root instead of the project folder, and the drift sat unnoticed for two days
**(1)** An earlier task set out to update all seven numbered docs plus `CLAUDE.md` after a round of design review. It should have written every file to `/Users/aadityaarora/Desktop/warehouse files/` (the actual git repo root), but six of the eight writes — `01_PRD.md`, `03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md`, `05_BUSINESS_RULES.md`, `06_ROADMAP.md`, `07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md`, `CLAUDE.md` — instead landed one directory up, at `/Users/aadityaarora/Desktop/` directly, all within the same 8-second window. `02_ARCHITECTURE.md` and `04_API_SPEC.md` were either not part of that batch or didn't drift the same way — no stray copies of those two were ever found. **(2)** Nobody noticed at the time. The canonical files in the project folder kept being read, edited, and reasoned about as if they were current, while a real batch of corrections — mostly legitimate fixes for content that actually was stale in canonical (the Kids sizing rule, sample-tracking removal, several roadmap status updates) — sat one level up, untracked by git, invisible to every subsequent task. **(3)** It surfaced only indirectly: a separate review conversation, apparently working from an upload of the stray `05_BUSINESS_RULES.md`, cited "rule 95" as settled fact. That conversation's own file was internally consistent (verified via grep + md5sum on its end), which is exactly why the contradiction looked like an error in *this* project's investigation rather than what it actually was — two genuinely different files sharing one filename. Tracing it required checking git history (one commit, ending at rule 65 — nowhere near rule 95), searching the whole filesystem (not just the project folder) for the filename and for the rule's exact text, and comparing checksums to prove the two `05_BUSINESS_RULES.md` files were byte-distinct, not copies of each other. **(4)** Took a full snapshot commit of canonical state before touching anything, diffed all six stray files against their canonical counterparts in full, and split every diff into "independently verifiable against an already-existing canonical rule or the live running code" versus "asserts something new with no source outside the stray batch itself." The first category (Kids sizing per rule 50, sample-tracking removal per rule 84, the `qtyReservedForSample` cleanup, several roadmap status corrections matched directly against controller/route files) was merged. The second category — rule 95, cited nowhere except that one 8-second batch write — was not merged on the strength of the stray file alone; it was added separately, after the owner confirmed it directly as a decision being made now, with an inline note dating it and explaining its provenance rather than presenting it as recovered history. **(5)** The general lesson: a task that writes multiple files needs to verify *where* it wrote them, not just *that* the write succeeded — a doc sitting one directory outside the repo will pass every "did the edit apply" check while being invisible to git status, invisible to every future `grep`/`Read` against the project path, and invisible to the person reviewing the diff, since there's no diff to review against a file nobody's looking at. The two-day gap wasn't a reasoning failure in any individual task; it was a blind spot no single task had a reason to go looking for, until an external, unrelated contradiction forced the question.

### The Transfer staged-batching diagnostic test crashed on `toBeInTheDocument` — a matcher that was never actually installed
**(1)** Writing the throwaway rendered-component test for Transfer's new staging flow (same vitest + happy-dom + `@testing-library/react` harness already used to diagnose the earlier Receive Stock staging report), the assertions were written as `expect(screen.getByText(...)).toBeInTheDocument()` — the standard Testing Library idiom, and the same style the earlier Receive Stock diagnostic test's surviving code implicitly resembles. It seemed safe to assume this matcher just came with `@testing-library/react`. **(2)** The test failed immediately, before any real assertion about staging behavior ran: `Error: Invalid Chai property: toBeInTheDocument`. **(3)** `toBeInTheDocument` isn't part of vitest's built-in `expect` (which is Chai-based, not Jest-based) — it's added by a separate package, `@testing-library/jest-dom`, which extends `expect` with DOM-specific matchers. It was never installed in this pass. Checked whether to just add it — `npm view @testing-library/jest-dom engines` returned `{ node: '>=22' }`, and this machine runs Node v20.12.2. Installing it would have violated CLAUDE.md's non-negotiable engines-check rule on the very check that rule exists to catch. **(4)** Rewrote every `toBeInTheDocument()`/`not.toBeInTheDocument()` assertion to use plain, Chai-native equivalents already available in vitest's `expect` without any extra package: `screen.getByText(...)` on its own (it throws if the element isn't found, so calling it *is* the assertion) for the positive case, and `expect(screen.queryByText(...)).toBeNull()` for the negative case. **(5)** This is the correct fix rather than a workaround, because it doesn't need a new dependency at all — `getByText`/`queryByText`'s own throw-or-return-null behavior already expresses "is this present" without a DOM-specific matcher layered on top, and reaching for an incompatible package would have repeated the exact jsdom-on-this-Node-version mistake already logged above, on a rule specifically written after that happened three times.

### The Factory Payables debit/payment diagnostic tests clicked "Confirm amount owed"/"Confirm payment" and neither submit handler ran
**(1)** Writing the throwaway test for the new "Record amount owed" form, the submit step was written the same way as every other button interaction in these tests: `fireEvent.click(screen.getByRole('button', { name: 'Confirm amount owed' }))`. **(2)** Both the debit test and, once written the same way, the payment test timed out in `waitFor(() => expect(createFactoryDebitMock).toHaveBeenCalled())` — the mock was never called, even though the form's fields were filled correctly and nothing else in the assertion trail pointed to bad data. **(3)** This is the exact, already-documented "native activation behavior" gap (see Concepts, below): `fireEvent.click` on a `type="submit"` button dispatches a click event that any click listener will see, but happy-dom doesn't perform the browser's own built-in "and also submit the enclosing form" step from that dispatched event — recognized immediately from the earlier two occurrences of this same failure mode already logged in this file, rather than re-diagnosed from scratch. **(4)** Changed both to `fireEvent.submit(button.closest('form'))` — dispatching the submit event directly on the element that actually owns the native behavior, matching the fix already established for this exact class of bug. **(5)** Correct because it doesn't try to simulate a click "hard enough" to trigger a side effect that dispatched events structurally can't trigger — it targets the real behavior (form submission) at its real owner (the `<form>`) instead of its trigger (the button). The Concepts entry below previously logged this gap as hit "twice" — once as this same submit-button case, on Manage Users' create-account form, and once as the unrelated checkbox-click variant — updated now to a third occurrence of the pattern generally (second occurrence of the submit-button case specifically), since a mistake repeated a third time across unrelated screens is worth a future task recognizing on sight rather than re-diagnosing again.

### `POST /api/bundles` was `requireRole('OWNER')` since this project's SECOND COMMIT — silently breaking every STAFF-initiated new-article receipt for the entire life of the app
**(1)** The original approach, set in commit `fed0af2` ("Build out the Phase 1 API surface" — the very first commit that actually built route files, immediately after the initial scaffold), gated `POST /api/bundles` behind `requireRole('OWNER')`. At the time this may have seemed like a reasonable default — Bundle sits structurally adjacent to Product, and Product's own price-setting path is OWNER-gated, so treating "anything that shapes what an article can be" as owner-level territory wasn't an unreasonable instinct in isolation. **(2)** It was never caught by any test, any code review, or any real usage for the rest of the project's history — not a regression, not something that broke later, just wrong from the start and never exercised by the account type it actually affects. It surfaced only tonight, when a real STAFF login (`ram123`) hit it directly while receiving a genuinely new article and got `"Could not add that color: This action requires the OWNER role"` — the first time in this project's history anyone had walked through Receive Stock's new-article flow as an actual STAFF user rather than as OWNER (who never hits this gate, since OWNER passes every `requireRole('OWNER')` check trivially) or by reading the code and assuming the documented behavior matched. **(3)** Diagnosed by tracing the exact call chain rather than guessing: `ReceiveStock.jsx`'s `handleColorChange` calls `createBundle(lookup.product.id, newColorId)` the moment a color with no existing Bundle is picked — this is not an edge case, it's the mandatory, unconditional first step every single time a brand-new article gets its first color. Confirmed the route's actual gating (`requireRole('OWNER')`, verified by reading `routes/bundles.js` directly, not assumed), then confirmed via `git log --all -- backend/src/routes/bundles.js` that this line has been byte-for-byte unchanged since commit `fed0af2` — the second commit in the whole repository, meaning this was never a working feature that regressed, it never worked for STAFF at all. Then checked every sibling endpoint in the same workflow — `POST /api/products` (`requireAuth` only, with its own comment stating *"staff create new articles during Receive Stock routinely"*), `POST /api/colors` (`requireAuth` only), `POST /api/transactions` (`requireAuth` only) — all three correctly any-role, all three explicitly documenting staff as the expected user. `05_BUSINESS_RULES.md` rule 71 and `04_API_SPEC.md`'s own `POST /api/products` section both explicitly describe staff creating new articles unaided — while `04_API_SPEC.md`'s own `POST /api/bundles` section, two hundred lines later in the *same file*, marked it `👑` (OWNER-only) without ever being reconciled against the rule it directly contradicts. Searched `LEARNING_LOG.md` for any prior record of this flow being tested end-to-end as a real STAFF login (`grep -i "as staff\|ram123\|staff account\|staff login"`) — found nothing relevant to Receive Stock or Bundle creation at all, only an unrelated Home-screen footer-note test. The absence of evidence was itself the evidence: nobody had ever actually run this path as STAFF before tonight. **(4)** Fixed by changing `routes/bundles.js`'s `POST /` handler from `requireAuth, requireRole('OWNER')` to `requireAuth` alone — matching Product/Color/Transaction's gating exactly, character for character in shape. Updated `bundleController.js`'s own header comment (previously asserted "OWNER only (👑)," now stale and actively misleading) and `04_API_SPEC.md` line 201 (`👑` → `🔒`, with an explanatory note reconciling it against rule 71) so the documentation no longer contradicts itself. **(5)** This is the correct fix, not a narrower one, because the actual invariant this project has consistently applied everywhere else — creation-without-price is staff territory, only setting a *real price* requires OWNER+PIN (rule 71 itself) — was never actually violated by Bundle creation in the first place; a Bundle carries no price field at all, it's a bare Product+Color link. The bug was never "Bundle creation needs looser rules than everywhere else," it was that Bundle creation had accidentally never been given the SAME rule as everywhere else. Verified end-to-end with a real STAFF login, not just a unit-level check of the route change in isolation: created a throwaway STAFF account, logged in as it for a genuine token (same pattern the Home role test already established — see Concepts, "Testing a role-conditional UI by creating a real user for each role"), and walked the complete real sequence a brand-new article actually requires — `POST /api/products` (create, pending price) → `POST /api/bundles` (first color) → `POST /api/transactions` (STOCK_IN) — confirming zero `403`s anywhere in that chain, then cleaned up the throwaway account and its created records. This is precisely the scenario that had never been tested before tonight, and precisely the one this fix needed to prove, not just assert.

### Party List shipped visible to STAFF — should have been owner-only from the start, same category as Manage Users/Factory Payables/Article Pricing
**(1)** The original task scoped Party List as a read-only screen backed by `GET /api/parties`, which is genuinely any-role at the API level (04_API_SPEC.md) — that fact got carried one level too far, into the frontend tile and route visibility as well, on the reasoning "the endpoint is any-role, so the screen should be too." `App.jsx`'s route used a bare `<ProtectedRoute>` (no `requireRole`) and `Home.jsx`'s entry used `show: () => true`, both matching Transfer's unconditional pattern. **(2)** Never caught by the automated tests (they only assert the screen renders correctly once mounted, not who should be able to reach it) or by the real STAFF/OWNER end-to-end pass done at the time (it deliberately tested that STAFF *could* reach the screen without a 403, which was true and beside the point) — it surfaced only when the task was reviewed against what the screen actually contains: no staff-facing purpose today, since it isn't wired into New Order (which doesn't exist yet); its only real substance right now is add/archive/GSTIN/address, all owner-only activities, the same category as Manage Users/Factory Payables/Article Pricing. **(3)** Confirmed by reading `App.jsx` directly rather than assuming: all three genuinely owner-only screens use `<ProtectedRoute requireRole="OWNER">`, and `ProtectedRoute.jsx` redirects a role mismatch straight back to `/` (`<Navigate to="/" replace />`) — not a 403 page, a silent bounce home. **(4)** Fixed by changing `/parties`'s route to `<ProtectedRoute requireRole="OWNER">` and `Home.jsx`'s entry to `show: (user) => user.role === 'OWNER'`, matching the other three character-for-character. `GET /api/parties` itself was deliberately left any-role, unchanged — that's correct and will matter once New Order's own Party dropdown needs it. **(5)** This is the right fix, not an overcorrection, because the mistake was conflating two different questions: "which roles is this API endpoint safe to expose to" (any-role, genuinely, forever) and "which roles have a reason to open this screen today" (owner-only, until a staff-facing consumer of Party data actually exists). An endpoint being safely any-role doesn't mean every UI surface built on top of it should default to the same visibility — that decision belongs to what the screen actually lets someone do, not to the permission of the read call underneath it.

### `06_ROADMAP.md` said Factory Payables' frontend, Category archive/reactivate, and Party's endpoints were missing — all three had actually shipped
**(1)** The original approach — implicit, not a deliberate decision — was that each task prompt would update whichever doc it was directly about (`04_API_SPEC.md` for a new endpoint, `05_BUSINESS_RULES.md` for a rule change, this file for its own entries) and that was considered sufficient documentation upkeep. **(2)** Noticed when a task asked to verify the roadmap against real git history "since it's stale relative to real, committed work": three items it flagged as missing or incomplete — Factory Payables' frontend, Category archive/reactivate, Party's `POST`/`GET` — were each checked against `git log` and found already built and committed (`0ede2e0`/`519f0e9`, `e44053f`, `0bdbd0f` respectively). A fourth gap turned up unprompted: Article Pricing, a whole screen with its own UI-brief section, was never added to the roadmap at all. **(3)** Diagnosed by checking each claim directly against `git log --oneline --all -- <file>` rather than trusting the doc's own text, then reading `07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md`/`03_DATABASE_SCHEMA.md` for anything built that the roadmap never mentioned in the first place. The common thread: every one of the individual task prompts that built these features updated the doc *specific* to that feature (API spec, business rules, its own commit) but none of them had "update the roadmap index" as part of their scope — so the index just never got touched after Phase 1.5 work started shipping. **(4)** Updated `06_ROADMAP.md`'s Phase 1.5 section to the real, git-verified state for all four items, and flagged the one thing still genuinely incomplete (Category's frontend archive UI is written but not yet committed) rather than marking it done. **(5)** The actual fix isn't "edit the roadmap once" — that only corrects today's snapshot. The real cause is structural: no task in this project has "keep the index current" as its job, so it will drift again the next time a feature ships via a docs-specific task prompt. Worth treating as a standing note: a task that closes out a roadmap-tracked item (Factory Payables, Category, Party, or any future Phase 1.5+ entity) should update `06_ROADMAP.md`'s status for that item in the same commit, not leave it for a later dedicated audit.

### New Order's low-stock badge E2E check was flaky — a fixed `waitForTimeout(800)` raced a second, independent fetch
**(1)** The original approach: `waitForSelector('button:has-text("...")')` to confirm the colour chips had rendered, then a flat `waitForTimeout(800)` "to let the stock fetch settle" before checking for the low-stock badge. This seemed reasonable because 800ms comfortably covers this app's normal local-network response times, and every other timing-sensitive check elsewhere in this session's E2E scripts had used a similar fixed pad successfully. **(2)** The check (`lowBadgeNearRed === 1`) came back `false` — consistently, across a rerun of the identical script — even though a direct `curl` against the real backend confirmed the stock data itself was correct (Red at exactly the ≤2 threshold, Blue well above it), and a separately-written, more minimal debug script exercising the exact same user flow showed the badge rendering correctly on its very first check. Two scripts, same real app, same real data, different outcomes — the discrepancy was the tell that this was a timing artifact in the test, not a data or rendering bug in the app. **(3)** Diagnosed by adding a raw HTML dump of the colour-chip row at the exact failure point rather than guessing: on one run it showed the badge missing, and — critically — merely adding that extra diagnostic `await` (with no functional effect of its own) was enough to shift timing and make the very same check pass on the next run. That confirmed the two fetches this screen kicks off in parallel on article resolution — `getValidColors` (which the button-existence check was actually waiting on) and `listStock` (which the badge actually depends on) — are independent promises that can settle at slightly different times under real network conditions; the button appearing is not evidence the stock fetch has also landed. **(4)** Fixed by replacing the fixed sleep with `page.waitForSelector('.color-chip-low-badge')` — polling for the actual target state instead of guessing how long it takes to arrive. **(5)** This is the correct fix, not a workaround, because it removes the race at its root rather than papering over it with a longer guess: CLAUDE.md's own testing rule ("polling for anything waiting on a real network response, never a fixed sleep") exists precisely because a fixed delay is either too short (flaky, as here) or wastefully long, and the right fix is always to wait for the real signal, not a longer fake one. Reran the corrected script three consecutive times against the real dev server with zero failures, confirming this wasn't a coincidence.

### Two throwaway verification scripts failed on their own mistakes, not the app's — module resolution and a guessed field name
**(1)** Wrote the pre-migration baseline script into the session scratchpad directory (the standing instruction is to keep temporary files out of the project), and wrote a follow-up audit script using `orderBy: { createdAt: 'asc' }` on `OrderAdjustment` — `createdAt` because every other model in this schema names its timestamp that way, so it was typed from habit rather than looked up. **(2)** The first died on `Cannot find module '@prisma/client'`; the second on `Unknown argument 'createdAt'`. **(3)** Neither needed real diagnosis — both error messages named the cause outright, and Prisma's in particular printed the full list of valid fields with `changedAt` among them. The first is just how Node resolves `require`: it walks up from the *script's own* directory, and the scratchpad has no `node_modules` above it, so a script that imports project dependencies has to live inside the project regardless of where temporary files are supposed to go. The second was a real schema fact — `OrderAdjustment` deliberately names its timestamp `changedAt`, not `createdAt`, because the row records *when a value changed*, which is the whole point of that table. **(4)** Copied the script into `backend/` (deleted afterwards in cleanup) and used `changedAt`. **(5)** Worth logging despite being trivial, for one reason: both failures were in the *verification* code, and a verification script that errors out is the harmless outcome. The dangerous version of the same slip is a check that runs, passes, and was measuring the wrong thing — which is exactly what a guessed field name would have produced had it happened to be a valid-but-different column. Reading the error rather than pattern-matching a fix is what kept these thirty seconds each.

### A round-trip smoke test failed on `Argument 'party' is missing` — which had nothing to do with the relation it named
**(1)** Wrote a smoke test to prove the migrated table could actually hold a real row, building the `create()` call from plain scalar foreign keys (`partyId`, `bundleId`, `locationId`, `userId`) — Prisma's "unchecked create" form, which every other script in this project has used successfully. The price came from `bundle.product.sellingPrice`, taking whichever bundle `findFirst()` returned. **(2)** Prisma rejected it with `Argument 'party' is missing`, listing `create`/`connectOrCreate`/`connect` as the options — i.e. demanding the *relation* form for a field I had deliberately supplied as a scalar. Taken at face value this reads as "this model can't be created from scalar FKs," which would have been a genuine (and alarming) claim about the new schema. **(3)** The tell was in Prisma's own echo of the arguments it received: `priceAtReturn: null`. The fixtures line printed just above showed why — `sellingPrice: 'null'` on the product `findFirst()` happened to return, which is a legitimate state in this app (the "pending price" case, where an article exists before anyone with OWNER role and a PIN has set its prices). So the real sequence was: a `null` landed in a required field → that made the input invalid against `UncheckedCreateInput` → Prisma fell back to matching the *checked* `CreateInput` variant instead → and reported the first thing missing from *that* shape, which was `party`. The error named a symptom of the fallback, not the cause. **(4)** Changed the fixture query to `where: { product: { sellingPrice: { not: null } } }`, which picked article 6044 at ₹675 and made all four assertions pass. **(5)** This is the correct fix rather than switching to `connect:` syntax — which would also have made the error disappear, while leaving the actual defect (a `null` price silently flowing into a required money column) completely untouched and simply relocated to a different error message. The general lesson about Prisma specifically: when `create()` complains about a field you never mentioned, suspect that a *different* field invalidated the input shape and triggered a fallback to the other variant. Read the arguments Prisma echoes back, not just the sentence at the bottom. The lesson about this project: `Product.sellingPrice` is genuinely nullable and `findFirst()` has no reason to avoid those rows, so any script or endpoint reading a price must handle the pending-price case rather than assume a price exists.

### A 404 on the new endpoint that was not a routing bug — a stale dev server owned the port
**(1)** Started the real dev backend for the end-to-end pass with `node src/server.js &`, then confirmed it was up the way this project always has: `curl /api/health` returned `{"status":"ok"}`, so the server was running and the E2E could proceed. **(2)** Every UI step passed until submit, which failed with `Cannot POST /api/returns` (404) — and the staged work stayed on screen with the real backend message shown, which is the designed failure behaviour but obviously not the intended outcome. Nothing had been written: 0 returns, stock unchanged. **(3)** The instinct was to suspect the route registration, since this project has a committed history of exactly that mistake ("Register factory-debits routes — was never actually committed"). Checked it directly instead of assuming — `server.js` had both the `require` and the `app.use`, and the modules all loaded. `lsof -ti :3001` then showed a single node process, PID 42911, started at 13:55 — *before* this task began — while the PID this session had recorded was 46978, which `ps` reported as not running. So the earlier session's dev server had never been stopped, my process died on `EADDRINUSE`, and the health check that "confirmed" the server was up had been answered by the stale one running pre-change code. **(4)** Killed 42911, restarted, and verified the route by its *response code* rather than by the server merely being alive: `POST /api/returns` returning `401` proves the route exists and reached the auth middleware, where `404` would prove it doesn't. Full E2E then passed 24/24. **(5)** This is the correct fix rather than "restart everything and retry" because it names the actual failure in the *verification step*: `/api/health` answers identically from any server on that port, so it confirms "a server is up", never "my server, with my code, is up". A liveness check that can't distinguish those is not evidence about a change you just made. The durable lesson is to verify a new route by hitting the route — an auth rejection is a positive signal — and to check the listening PID against the one you started whenever a brand-new endpoint 404s.

### Three throwaway-test bugs that reported correct behaviour as failures
**(1)** The backend harness asserted `mine.length === 6` for the returns it had created, `returnEntries.length >= 6` for History, and picked its History sample with `find((e) => e.description.includes(f.party.name))` — all written while composing the test top-to-bottom, each looking obviously right in isolation. **(2)** Three failures: "got 5" twice, and a History description assertion that failed while printing a description that was visibly, completely correct. **(3)** Counted the actual successful creates by hand: 2 (multi-line) + 1 (price-spoof) + 1 (non-OTHER-no-note) + 1 (fresh-location) = **5**. The `6` was plain arithmetic error on my part; every rejected request had correctly created nothing, which is what the surrounding assertions were themselves proving. The third was subtler and more instructive: the sampled entry matched on party name, but *all five* returns share that party, so `find` returned whichever came first — the fresh-location line — and the assertion then checked it against the *other* location's name. **(4)** Corrected the count to 5 with a comment showing the arithmetic, and re-pinned the History sample to `includes('3 sets')` — the one line whose article, colour, location and reason are all known exactly. **(5)** The count fixes are correct because the app's behaviour was right and the expectation was wrong — changing the app to produce 6 would have meant creating a row from a request that should have been rejected. The locator fix matters more: an assertion keyed on a value shared by many rows isn't testing what it appears to test, it's testing a row chosen arbitrarily, and it passes or fails on ordering luck. Same family as the `waitFor`-already-satisfied entry above — key an assertion on something that identifies exactly one target, never on something several candidates share.

### The test-cleanup delete order was right for returns and wrong for everything else
**(1)** Cleanup deleted transactions with `where: { partyStockReturn: { partyId: { in: ... } } }` — scoped to return-linked transactions, which is precisely what the returns tests create. **(2)** It crashed on the *next* statement: `Stock` deletion violated `RESTRICT` on `Transaction_stockId_fkey`. The run had already reported 44 passed / 3 failed, so the fixtures were left half-deleted in the test database. **(3)** The error named the constraint and the offending key directly. The cause was that the same harness *also* exercises `POST /api/transactions` (to prove the shared-helper refactor didn't regress `STOCK_IN`/`STOCK_OUT`), and those transactions carry no `partyStockReturnId` — so the returns-scoped filter never matched them, and they kept a `RESTRICT` reference alive on the `Stock` rows being deleted. **(4)** Broadened the filter to every transaction touching the fixture bundles (`{ stock: { bundleId: { in: bundles } } }`), and rewrote cleanup to sweep by **name pattern** rather than only this run's ids, so the previous crashed run's orphans were cleared too. Verified: 0 leftover parties, returns, stock rows and transactions. **(5)** The pattern-sweep is the part that matters. A cleanup keyed only to ids held in memory cannot recover from its own earlier crash, and half-cleaned fixtures silently inflate a later run's counts — the exact failure already logged when a killed test process orphaned its fixtures. Cleanup should be written to be re-runnable and self-healing, not merely correct on the happy path.

### An E2E assertion failed on text that was correct — `innerText()` reports rendered casing, not source casing
**(1)** The dashboard E2E compared the six KPI labels against `['Stock value', 'Sets in stock', ...]` — the exact strings in the JSX — using `allInnerTexts()`. Straightforward, and it matched the component source character for character. **(2)** It failed, reporting `["STOCK VALUE","SETS IN STOCK",...]`. Every other assertion in the run passed, including the colour-role and ordering checks against the same six cards. **(3)** No real diagnosis needed once the failure output was read rather than skimmed: the returned strings were the right words in the wrong case, which points at presentation, not data. `.dash-kpi-label` carries `text-transform: uppercase` — straight from the design bundle, which is exactly what the card labels are supposed to look like. Playwright's `innerText()` returns text **as rendered**, with CSS text transforms applied, unlike `textContent()` which returns the raw DOM text. So the app was right, the design was right, and the assertion was comparing a rendered string to a source string. **(4)** Lower-cased both sides of the comparison, and added a separate assertion that `getComputedStyle(el).textTransform === 'uppercase'` — so the casing is now checked deliberately as a design requirement instead of accidentally as a side effect of a text match. **(5)** This is the right fix rather than switching to `textContent()`: `textContent()` would have made the failure disappear while also making the test blind to whether the label renders uppercase at all, which is a real part of matching the design. The general lesson is that `innerText` and `textContent` answer different questions — "what does the user see" versus "what is in the DOM" — and an assertion should pick the one that matches what it actually intends to guarantee. Same family as the earlier entry about asserting on a value several rows share: the test passed or failed for a reason unrelated to the thing it was named after.

### Record Payment's date defaulted to the wrong day — `toISOString()` reads UTC, not local time
**(1)** The Party Payables "Record payment" form needed a sensible default for its date field, and the obvious one-liner was `new Date().toISOString().slice(0, 10)` — a pattern that looks completely standard for producing a `YYYY-MM-DD` string, matching `<input type="date">`'s own format exactly. **(2)** A real browser test recording a real payment through the actual form showed the entry saved with yesterday's date, not today's — caught only because the test printed the recorded row back and it visibly disagreed with the wall clock, not because anything threw an error. **(3)** Checked the actual local time against `toISOString()`'s output directly: `date` on the machine showed `Fri Aug 21 00:44:19 IST 2026`, but `new Date().toISOString()` printed `2026-08-20T19:14:19.580Z` — still the 20th. `toISOString()` always reports UTC, and IST is UTC+5:30, so for roughly the first 5.5 hours of every IST calendar day, the UTC calendar day is still the previous one. The bug wasn't rare — it fires every single day, for hours, and would have looked completely correct in testing done any other time of day. **(4)** Rewrote the default to build the string from `getFullYear()`/`getMonth()`/`getDate()` — the local-time getters — instead of `toISOString()`. **(5)** This is the correct fix rather than, say, adjusting the string by a fixed offset: the getter-based approach reads whatever the browser's own clock considers "today" directly, so it's correct in any timezone, not just corrected for this one case. The general lesson: `toISOString()` (and anything else with `ISO` in the name) is a UTC-normalized wire format, appropriate for sending a timestamp to a server or comparing two instants — never for asking "what calendar day is it right now" for a human-facing default, which is a local-time question by definition. This app already had the correct pattern elsewhere (`Orders.jsx`'s `currentMonthKey()` uses `getFullYear()`/`getMonth()` for exactly this reason) — the bug was reaching for the more familiar-looking one-liner instead of the pattern already established in the same codebase.

### Adding `productName` to `GET /api/stock` first showed up as blank text, not an error — a stale dev server running the old controller
**(1)** The original approach was straightforward: add `name: true` to the existing `Product` select in `stockController.js`, add `productName` to the flattened response, and render it in both Low Stock pages' accordion header exactly like Pack/Bill/Ship Order already do. Since this backend process was already confirmed running (`lsof -i :3001`) before the edit, no restart seemed necessary — the edit was to a controller file, not to routing or startup config. **(2)** The first real-browser Playwright pass showed every article header as `"6023 — "` — the em dash and the `<span className="muted">` wrapper rendered, but `article.productName` was always empty, on both the staff and the owner dashboard pages, with zero console errors (an empty string is valid JSX, not a crash). **(3)** Checked the running backend process directly rather than assuming the edit was live: `ps aux` showed the listener on port 3001 had a start time from before this task's edit was made. This project's dev backend is started with plain `node src/server.js`, not a file-watcher like `nodemon` — so the process in memory was still running the pre-edit controller, serving the response shape from before `name: true` was added, regardless of what the file on disk now said. **(4)** Killed the stale process and restarted `node src/server.js` fresh, then reran the identical Playwright script with no other change — every article header now showed the real name (`"6023 — Jordan"`, etc.) on both pages. **(5)** This is the correct fix, not a coincidence: a plain `node` process reads its module files once at startup and holds them in memory for the life of the process, so any edit to a `require`d file has zero effect until that process is killed and restarted — unlike the frontend's Vite dev server, which does hot-reload. Matches the earlier-logged "404 on the new endpoint that was not a routing bug" entry in spirit (a liveness check that answers identically regardless of which code is actually running isn't evidence the change took effect) — here the tell was blank data instead of a stale response, but the underlying cause (a long-running plain-`node` process not knowing the file changed) is the same category of mistake, worth remembering as a standing habit whenever backend controller logic is edited mid-session rather than at the start of a fresh test pass.

### A real 500 in the middle of a passing UI test, which turned out to be my own test tooling exhausting the DB connection pool
**(1)** The `qty` feature's STAFF browser test drove the whole Receive Stock flow end to end and reported every assertion correct — 8 steppers rendered, the readout tracking M+1/L+2/XL+1 to "4 pieces per set", the receipt row reading `Black 3 12`, and "Receipt saved." Because the flow completed and every figure was right, the natural read was that the test had passed cleanly. **(2)** It hadn't: the console-error collector printed one entry — `Failed to load resource: the server responded with a status of 500`. A 500 during a task that changes how money is computed is not something to note and move past, so the flow completing successfully made it *more* worth chasing, not less — a write path that half-fails while still appearing to succeed is exactly the dangerous shape. **(3)** The browser only sees the status code, so the cause had to come from the server log, which named it outright: `PrismaClientKnownRequestError ... P2024 ... Timed out fetching a new connection from the connection pool (connection limit: 21)`, thrown from `middleware/auth.js:25` on `prisma.user.findUnique` — the auth middleware, on a `User` lookup. That located it definitively outside this change: nothing in the `qty` work touches `User`, and the change adds no new queries at all, only extra *columns* to selects that were already running. The real cause was my own verification tooling — the baseline, comparison, setup and aggregate scripts each construct their own `PrismaClient`, and against a Neon serverless Postgres those pile up against a shared 21-connection ceiling alongside the dev server's own pool. **(4)** No application fix was warranted, because there was no application defect: the correct response was to identify it as environmental, confirm it was unrelated to the change, and stop opening long-lived clients unnecessarily in throwaway scripts. **(5)** This is the right resolution rather than "retry until the error stops appearing" precisely because the distinction it turns on — *my tooling* exhausted the pool, versus *the feature* leaks connections — is invisible from the symptom and decisive for what to do about it. The two look identical in a browser console. The generalisable habit is the one that resolved it: when a 500 surfaces during a test of your own change, read the server-side stack trace before forming a theory, and check whether the failing query is even in the code you touched — here it was a `User` lookup in auth middleware during a `ProductSize` change, which settles the question immediately. Same family as the earlier "a 404 that was a stale dev server owning the port" entry: the failure was real and worth investigating, but it belonged to the test environment, not to the app.

### Two throwaway verification scripts failed on their own request-shape guesses, not on anything the app did wrong
**(1)** The Receive → Pack → Bill cycle script sent the pack request as `{ lines: [...] }`, and asked the revenue endpoints for a plain `GET /api/locations/revenue` with no query string. Both were written from memory of the general shape rather than checked against the controller. **(2)** The first returned `400 VALIDATION_ERROR: lineItems must be a non-empty array`; the second `400 VALIDATION_ERROR: period must be one of month, six_months, fy, all, or 'custom' with from/to`. **(3)** Neither needed real diagnosis — both endpoints named the exact expected shape in their own error message, and `packOrder`'s first line (`const { lineItems: submittedLines } = req.body`) confirmed it directly. **(4)** Used `lineItems` and appended `?period=all`. **(5)** Logging these despite being trivial, for the same reason the earlier pair of script-bugs entries gives: the harmless outcome is a verification script that *errors*. The dangerous version is a verification script that runs, passes, and was measuring something other than what it claimed — which is exactly what a wrong-but-still-valid request body would have produced. Both of these failed loudly on their first run, which is the good case; the discipline worth keeping is reading the endpoint's own validation code when writing a test against it, rather than reconstructing the body from memory and trusting a green result.

### A `waitFor` that passed instantly because the app renders "Loading…" with a real ellipsis, not three dots
**(1)** The Owner Dashboard Overview screenshot came back showing "Loading...", so I replaced the fixed `waitForTimeout` with a polled predicate — `page.waitForFunction(() => !document.body.innerText.includes('Loading...'))` — which is exactly the correction this project's own testing rule prescribes for anything waiting on a real network response. **(2)** The rerun produced an identical screenshot, still reading "Loading...", and — the actual tell — `waitForFunction` did **not** throw a timeout. A predicate that is genuinely waiting either eventually passes or times out; returning immediately with the page unchanged means it was never false to begin with. **(3)** The cause was a character: the component renders `Loading…` with U+2026, a single ellipsis glyph, while my predicate tested for `'Loading...'`, three ASCII periods. Those never match, so `!includes(...)` was `true` on the very first poll, and the "wait" asserted nothing at all. This is precisely the trap CLAUDE.md already documents — *a `waitFor` predicate must be false in the starting state, or it can pass immediately by coincidence and silently assert against stale data* — and I reintroduced it while in the act of fixing a different timing bug. **(4)** Diagnosed properly instead of guessing at the wait: I instrumented the page's real network traffic, which showed `GET /api/dashboard/overview` returning **200** and every KPI rendering correctly (`STOCK VALUE ₹7.1 L`, `OPEN ORDERS ₹16,200`, `REVENUE ₹1.6 L`). The page was never stuck; both screenshots were taken before the unlock-then-fetch sequence had completed, and neither "wait" had held for it. **(5)** The correct lesson isn't "match the ellipsis" — that would fix this one string and leave the technique just as fragile. It's that a negative predicate (`!includes(someLoadingText)`) is inherently the wrong shape for this job, because every way of being wrong about the loading text makes it pass instantly and silently. Waiting for a *positive* signal that only exists in the finished state — a KPI value being present, a specific selector rendering — cannot fail this way: get the target wrong and it times out loudly instead of lying. Same root as the earlier logged `waitFor`-already-satisfied entry, and the reason the earlier dashboard-lock bug in this same session slipped through too: `waitForSelector('.dash-lock-overlay, body')` matched `body` instantly, so the overlay check ran before the overlay existed, silently skipped unlocking, and left every figure hidden behind the PIN screen.

### The same cleanup-can't-recover-from-its-own-crash mistake, for the third time in this codebase
**(1)** The UI probe script created a throwaway order, ran its checks, then deleted that order and its probe user at the end — cleanup keyed to the ids held in memory from the run that created them, which reads as obviously correct while writing it. **(2)** The first run died early (an unrelated environment problem — the machine's LAN IP had changed, so the frontend couldn't reach the backend and login timed out), leaving an orphaned order and user behind. The second run then completed all its checks and failed on the final line: `User.deleteMany` violated `Order_createdById_fkey`, because the *previous* run's orphaned order still referenced the *previous* run's probe user, and nothing in this run's memory knew either existed. **(3)** No real diagnosis needed — the constraint error named the exact referencing table and key. The cause is structural, not incidental: a cleanup that only knows the ids it just created is blind to anything a previous crashed run left behind, and each subsequent failure adds more orphans. **(4)** Replaced it with a sweep that finds everything by REFERENCE — every user matching `probe_gst*`, then every order whose `createdById` is one of them — and deletes in FK order. That recovered both runs' leftovers in one pass. **(5)** This is worth logging despite the lesson already appearing twice in this file (the killed-test-process entry, and the party-returns entry that added a name-pattern sweep) because I wrote the memory-keyed version *again* anyway. The generalisable form is stronger than "remember to sweep": a cleanup routine should be written so that running it twice, or running it after a crash, converges on the same clean state — which means it must locate its targets by a durable property (a username prefix, a foreign key) rather than by values it happens to be holding. I also added an explicit check that no stock transactions were attributable to the probes before deleting, so "nothing needs reversing" was confirmed rather than assumed.

### Tests failed against code that was correct — an orphaned backend process was serving a stale build
**(1)** After implementing rule 104 I ran the end-to-end role test straight against `http://192.168.1.30:3001`, because `ps` showed a backend running and `/api/health` returned `{"status":"ok"}`. A responding server on the right port, plus a nodemon in the process list, reads as "the dev server is up and watching my files." **(2)** Ten checks failed. The failure detail was the giveaway rather than the failure itself: staff and owner both saw exactly 162 entries, and the sample entry JSON contained no `actorRole` key at all — not a wrong value, an *absent field*. Code I had just written and successfully `require()`-syntax-checked cannot omit a property it unconditionally sets, so the server answering me could not be running that code. **(3)** `lsof -nP -iTCP:3001 -sTCP:LISTEN` named the listener as pid 20740, and `ps -o pid,ppid` showed its **PPID was 1** — orphaned, adopted by init. Nodemon was pid 4909, so 20740 was not its child; nodemon's own restarts were producing children that could never bind the port because the orphan already held it. `ps` also showed the orphan had started at 12:14, hours before my edits. Nothing was supervising it, so no file change would ever restart it. **(4)** Killed pid 20740, confirmed the port was genuinely free, then started a fresh `node src/server.js` and re-checked `/api/health` and the listener pid before re-running anything. All 23 checks then passed. **(5)** This is the correct fix rather than a workaround because the failing component was never the application code — it was that my requests and my edits were pointed at two different processes. Restarting is only correct once that's established; had I "fixed" the code to make the tests pass, I would have been changing correct code to satisfy a stale server. The generalisable lesson is that **"something is listening on the port" is not evidence that *your* code is listening on it** — and the cheap check that distinguishes them is the PPID: a supervised dev server's PID has its supervisor as parent, and a PPID of 1 means nothing will ever reload it. I now treat "a field I unconditionally set is missing from the response" as a deployment/staleness symptom first, not a logic symptom.

### Test script read the transaction id from the wrong nesting level
**(1)** The probe script recorded the created receipt as `staffReceipt.body.id`, matching the shape every other `POST` in this app returns (`/api/parties` and `/api/orders` both return the created entity at the top level), so the same pattern was applied to `/api/transactions` by analogy. **(2)** Four checks failed and the diagnostic print showed `(not present)` for all four receipt lookups — for **both** roles, including the owner who is supposed to see everything. That symmetry is what ruled out the new filter as the cause: a role filter that hid a row from the owner too would be a different bug from the one I was testing, and far more likely to be the test looking up an id that never existed. **(3)** Read `createTransaction` directly: it responds `res.status(201).json(result)` where `result` is `{ transaction, updatedStock }` — the transaction is nested one level down, because this endpoint deliberately returns the post-write stock figure alongside the transaction. So `body.id` was `undefined`, making every lookup key the literal string `RECEIPT:undefined`. **(4)** Changed both reads to `body.transaction.id`. **(5)** Correct because the endpoint's response shape is intentional and documented in its own controller — the transaction alone would not tell a caller the resulting stock level, which is exactly what a Receive Stock screen needs to display next. The test was wrong, not the API, so the fix belonged in the test. The habit worth keeping: **when an assertion fails identically for a privileged and an unprivileged caller, suspect the test's own lookup key before suspecting the permission logic** — a genuine visibility bug is asymmetric by definition.

### Two probe assertions failed against correct code, both because the test looked at the wrong thing
**(1)** Testing the new dashboard page, I asserted "STAFF sees no dashboard sidebar" with `!!document.querySelector('nav')`, and waited for the populated screen with `waitForFunction(() => !bodyText.includes("Select a factory to see what's owed"))`. Both read as obviously right: the sidebar *is* a `<nav>`, and the placeholder text *does* disappear once a factory loads. **(2)** The sidebar assertion failed for a STAFF user who had in fact been correctly redirected, and an earlier screenshot captured a page reading "Loading…" instead of the payables data it was supposed to show. **(3)** For the sidebar: dumping every `<nav>` on the staff page showed exactly one, `<nav class="tile-grid">` — the mobile Home screen's own tile grid. The URL was `/`, `.dash-sidebar` was absent and the page never mentioned Factory Payables, so the gating was right and the selector was wrong. For the screenshot: the placeholder is replaced by `"Loading…"` *before* the data arrives, so the predicate went true at the wrong moment — it described a state the app passes *through*, not the state I wanted. **(4)** Narrowed the selector to `.dash-rail-nav` (the actual sidebar), and re-keyed the wait onto `.stat-hero` — an element that exists only once the payable figures have genuinely rendered. **(5)** These are the correct fixes because in both cases the application behaved properly throughout; the test was asking a question whose answer didn't distinguish the states I cared about. `nav` was true in both the pass and fail cases for reasons unrelated to permissions, and "placeholder gone" was true in both the loading and loaded states. This is the same trap CLAUDE.md's `waitFor` rule already names — a predicate must be false in the starting state *and* stay false until the actual target state — extended one step: it must also be false in every state the app transitions through on the way there. A three-state flow (`empty -> loading -> loaded`) needs a predicate keyed to the third state specifically, never to "not the first."

### The planned multi-article test order never appeared on screen, and the cause was one layer deeper than the test
**(1)** For the order-grouping task I picked a real, already-existing 3-article, 13-line order (SAI, cancelled, PACKED) as the multi-article test case, on the reasoning that testing against real data beats fabricating a throwaway order whenever real data already covers the case. **(2)** After logging in and expanding every SAI row on the Orders page, the order simply wasn't there — not collapsed, not hidden behind pagination, entirely absent from every rendered row. **(3)** I fetched the order directly from Prisma to confirm it genuinely exists (it does, with real line items across three articles) and read `Orders.jsx`'s own bucketing logic: `bucketDateOf(order)` returns `order.cancelledAt` for any cancelled order, and that field feeds `monthKeyOf()` to decide which month bucket the order shows under. I tried to `select: { cancelledAt: true }` in a throwaway query to confirm its value — Prisma itself refused with "Unknown field `cancelledAt` for select statement on model `Order`," which is what surfaced the real cause: the field referenced in `bucketDateOf()` doesn't exist in `schema.prisma` at all. `order.cancelledAt` is always `undefined`, its month bucket never matches any selectable month, and the order is silently unreachable through the UI forever. **(4)** Rather than fixing that (a real bug, but a different one than the task asked about), I created a fresh 3-article order through the actual `/api/orders` POST endpoint — left at `PLACED`, which renders under "Open orders" and never touches `bucketDateOf()` at all — and used that for the grouping test instead. **(5)** This is the correct call because the task's scope was line-item grouping, not the month-bucketing logic that made one particular pre-existing order invisible; fixing an unrelated bug inside a task about a different bug would have been unreviewable scope creep, and the fresh order tests the actual thing being asked about just as validly. Logged here (rather than silently worked around) so the real bug doesn't vanish — a future task can search this entry when someone eventually asks "why can't I find that cancelled order."

### Two test assertions failed against correct grouping code, both from stale assumptions the test carried in, not from reading what the app actually did
**(1)** Testing the new Comeco factory group, I hardcoded the expected article order as `['9134', '9133', '9554', '9557']` — the four articles I remembered Comeco having from an earlier data dump — and asserted the rendered order matched exactly. Separately, testing that mobile's Factory picker still renders a plain `<select>`, I read `select option` text immediately after `waitForSelector('select', ...)` resolved. Both felt like complete, correct checks. **(2)** The Comeco assertion failed with `["9134","6099","9133","9554","9557"]` rendered — a `"6099"` I hadn't listed. The mobile assertion failed with `mobileFactoryOptions` equal to `["Loading…"]` — the picker's own placeholder option, not a real factory list. **(3)** For Comeco: this app's own rule (article numbers are unique per Factory, never globally) meant I couldn't safely reuse "the articles I remember Comeco having" without rechecking — `6099` legitimately exists as a DIFFERENT real article in BOTH RDX and Comeco, and my hardcoded expectation had simply dropped it from memory between writing the data-exploration script and writing the assertion three files later. For mobile: `waitForSelector('select', ...)` only waits for the ELEMENT to exist in the DOM, which it does the instant React mounts the empty picker — it says nothing about whether the async factory fetch inside it has resolved yet, so the check ran against the picker's own `"Loading…"` placeholder state. **(4)** Fixed the Comeco expectation to the article's real five-member list, with a comment explaining why `6099` legitimately appears twice across factories. Fixed the mobile wait to `waitForFunction(() => document.querySelectorAll('select option').length > 1, ...)` — true only once the placeholder has been replaced by real options, false in the state being waited FROM. **(5)** Both are the correct fixes because the application was behaving correctly the whole time in both cases — the first was my own memory of test data going stale between two points in the same session, and the second is the exact three-state-flow trap already logged once this session (`empty -> loading -> loaded` needs a predicate keyed to the third state specifically, never to "the element exists"). Worth restating even though the general lesson was already logged: recognizing the SAME class of mistake on a second, differently-shaped occurrence (a `<select>`'s options populating asynchronously, not a page-level data fetch) is what turns "I made this mistake once" into "I now check for this pattern," rather than re-learning it a third time in some future shape.

### A STAFF History test reported zero entries — real staff-actor history existed, the wait just fired before the fetch resolved
**(1)** Testing that STAFF's rule-104-filtered History still shows the new avatars, I logged in as a fresh probe STAFF account, navigated to `/history`, and waited a fixed `1500`/`2500`ms before reading the page, reasoning that the earlier OWNER pass against the same screen had worked fine with a similar short wait. **(2)** The test reported `STAFF_ROW_COUNT: 0` and, separately, a plain debug fetch of the same page returned page text ending in `"...History Loading…"` — the screen was still showing its loading state, not a genuine empty result. **(3)** Querying the database directly (bypassing the UI) confirmed real STAFF-actor `Transaction`/`Order` rows exist (Ram and Mukesh Kumar Yadav both have receipts), so a true zero-entry result would have been the actual bug — rereading `History.jsx`'s fetch effect showed nothing wrong there, which meant the test's own timing was the suspect. A longer, explicit wait (`waitForFunction(() => !document.body.textContent.includes('Loading…'))`) on the same URL immediately showed the real entries with their actor names. **(4)** Replaced every fixed-delay wait around a `listHistory()` fetch in the test with that same `waitForFunction` predicate — a network-backed fetch, exactly the case CLAUDE.md's own `waitFor` rule already calls out (never a fixed `sleep()` for something waiting on a real response). **(5)** Correct fix because the app was never actually broken — a `1500`ms guess happened to be long enough once (small OWNER dataset load) and too short a second time (STAFF's larger, more heavily filtered query, plus general request variance), which is exactly why CLAUDE.md's rule bans fixed-delay waits against a live backend rather than allowing "a big enough number": there is no number that's provably big enough, only a predicate that's true precisely when the target state is reached.

### The dev backend had silently detached from nodemon — my `historyController.js` edit compiled but never actually ran
**(1)** After adding `actorId` to `historyController.js`, I curled `/api/history` expecting to see the new field, reasoning that this repo's backend runs under `nodemon` (confirmed by `ps aux` showing a `nodemon src/server.js` process), which restarts automatically on a file save. **(2)** The response came back with every field EXCEPT `actorId` — the exact field I'd just added, with no error of any kind. **(3)** `ps -ef` showing parent/child pids revealed the process actually bound to port 3001 had `ppid 1` (an orphan owned by launchd, not by the nodemon process) and had been running for over 5 hours — since well before this edit — while the visible `nodemon` process (start time Tuesday, this session started Wednesday) had no live child at all. Some earlier session had evidently started a plain `node src/server.js` by hand, and nodemon's own supervision had separately died or detached without anyone noticing, since the app kept working fine for every request that didn't depend on today's edit. **(4)** Killed both the orphaned server and the disconnected nodemon supervisor, then started a fresh `npm run dev` in the background — a plain restart of a local dev process, not a destructive action. Re-curled the same endpoint and got `actorId` back correctly. **(5)** Correct fix, and worth stating the general lesson plainly: "there's a process with the expected name running" is not the same claim as "my code change is live in the process actually serving requests." A one-time sanity curl against the SPECIFIC field just added, right after any backend edit and before building a whole test suite on top of it, is a cheap way to catch this class of drift immediately rather than after several confusing test failures blamed on the wrong layer.

### A login helper's fixed 800ms wait passed for months of reuse, then failed right after a backend restart
**(1)** Testing the new per-actor avatar colours, I reused this session's own established `login()` test helper — fill username/password, click submit, `waitForTimeout(800)` — which had worked correctly across every earlier task's tests this session without incident. **(2)** Immediately after, `page.goto('/history')` followed by a wait for "Loading…" to disappear timed out waiting for `.history-row` to appear. **(3)** Dumping the URL after the wait showed `/login`, not `/history` — the login had silently failed to complete in time, so the subsequent navigation to `/history` had been bounced straight back to the login screen by `ProtectedRoute`, and `waitForFunction(() => !body.includes('Loading…'))` was trivially satisfied by the login page's own body text, which never contained "Loading…" in the first place — a passing predicate that was actually watching the wrong page entirely. A direct curl to `/api/auth/login` confirmed the credentials themselves were fine (200, valid token) — the delay was real frontend/backend latency, not a bad password. **(4)** Traced this to the backend restart performed one step earlier in this same task (previous entry): the very first requests after a cold restart (Vite HMR reconnecting, the backend's first JIT-ed request) are genuinely slower than the steady-state latency `800ms` was calibrated against every previous time this helper ran. Fixed by replacing the fixed wait with `waitForFunction(() => !window.location.pathname.includes('/login'))` — true only once the app has actually left the login route. **(5)** Correct fix, and a sharper version of the STAFF-History lesson two entries above: a fixed delay isn't just risky in general, it can be a value that has genuinely worked correctly many times in a row and still be wrong the next time, because "long enough" depends on transient system state (a process that just restarted) that has nothing to do with the code being tested. A `waitFor`-style predicate keyed to the actual target state has no such dependency — it's not "usually enough," it's correct by construction.

### Two Manage Parties test failures, both from the test script's own assumptions rather than the restructured screen
**(1)** Testing the new "Delete" button, I wrote `page.locator('button:has-text("Delete")')` expecting it to match the row action, then re-clicked `.accordion-header.nested:has-text("Probe Delete Traders")` after toggling "Show archived" back on, expecting that to re-expand the row so I could reach "Reactivate." Both felt like straightforward selectors. **(2)** The first threw a Playwright strict-mode violation ("resolved to 2 elements") instead of running at all: the accordion header itself contains the party's name "Probe Delete Traders," and Playwright's `has-text` matched that whole header alongside the actual "Delete" link-button, since neither locator was scoped narrowly enough. The second didn't error but timed out waiting for "Reactivate" to appear. **(3)** For the first, reading the error's own element dump showed exactly what was double-matching — the accordion header wasn't hidden text, it was right there in the match list. For the second, a screenshot taken at that point showed the row collapsed with just its header and badge visible, no expanded body at all — meaning my second click had toggled it CLOSED, not open. Checking `expandedPartyIds` (a plain `Set` of party ids, independent of `showArchived`) confirmed the row was already expanded from clicking "Delete" earlier in the same run, so a second unconditional click flipped it shut. **(4)** Fixed the first by switching to `page.getByRole('button', { name: 'Delete', exact: true })`, which matches only elements whose accessible name is exactly "Delete," not any container merely containing that word. Fixed the second by checking whether "Reactivate" was already visible before clicking to expand, only clicking when it wasn't. **(5)** Both fixes are correct because the app was behaving exactly as designed in both cases — `expandedPartyIds` persisting across the `showArchived` toggle is the intended, desirable behavior (an owner mid-review of one party shouldn't lose their place by flipping a filter), so the second "bug" was actually the test failing to account for state it had itself set up two steps earlier. The generalizable habit, restated for a third distinct shape this session: before asserting on a UI action's outcome, check what state the test itself already put the page into, rather than assuming a fresh natural starting point.

### A test assumed a fully-staged colour would linger at "0 sets" — it correctly vanished instead, per behavior that predates this task
**(1)** Testing Transfer's new multi-select staging, after batch-adding Black/White/Olive Green with Olive Green set to its full available quantity (3 of 3), I asserted the Olive Green chip would still be present afterward, now reading "Olive Green · 0 sets" — treating "showing zero" as the natural way to confirm the cap had worked. **(2)** The assertion failed: Olive Green wasn't in the chip list at all afterward, and the article's own colour-count badge had dropped from "6 colours" to "5 colours". **(3)** Rather than assuming this was a bug the new chip mechanism introduced, I re-read `availableRows`' filter — `sourceStock.filter((r) => remainingForRow(r) > 0 || ...)` — which was NOT code this task touched. It already excluded any row at zero remaining unless it was the row currently being actively configured (previously via `selectedRow`, now also via `checkedColors`). A colour fully consumed by staging has always disappeared from this screen's list, under the exact same rule that made a fully-transferred row vanish from the OLD flat list long before this task existed. **(4)** Corrected the assertion to expect Olive Green's ABSENCE from the chip list (and the colour count dropping to 5), with a comment tracing the behavior back to the pre-existing filter rather than to anything new. **(5)** Correct fix because the app's behavior was right and consistent with its own established rule the whole time — the mistake was designing a test assertion around what felt intuitively obvious ("a capped-out item should show 0") without first checking what this codebase's own existing filter already did in that exact situation. The generalizable habit: when verifying a NEW mechanism (the chip cap) that sits on top of OLD, unmodified logic (`availableRows`), check what the old logic already does with the new mechanism's edge cases before writing an assertion — the old logic's behavior is the actual spec here, not intuition about what "should" happen.

### Retiring a token (Rust) broke a real feature (Mukesh's History badge) because the verification checked the wrong two things, not zero things
**(1)** While replacing Home's 8 tile colours, the task asked me to confirm Rust/Lavender/Olive were truly orphaned before deleting them. I ran two checks: `grep` across the frontend for the derived CSS **class names** (`tile-rust`, `tile-lavender`, `tile-olive`, etc.) to confirm no `tone:` value in `Home.jsx` still produced them, and a second `grep` scoped to `index.css` alone to confirm the `:root` **definitions** were gone after deleting them. Both passed, so I removed all three tokens and reported it as verified-safe. **(2)** It wasn't safe: `utils/avatar.js`'s `ACTOR_BADGE_COLORS` referenced `var(--rust-bg)`/`var(--rust-text)` directly for Mukesh Kumar Yadav's name-badge — a completely different file, on a completely different UI surface (History, not Home tiles), which my two checks never touched. His badge silently rendered with an undefined CSS variable (effectively invisible/broken styling) from that point until this follow-up task caught it. **(3)** The follow-up task's own step 1 asked for exactly the check that was missing the first time: `grep -rn "\-\-rust-" frontend/src/` — a single command, whole-frontend, for the raw **token variable name** itself (`--rust-`), not the class name that happens to be built from it and not scoped to one file. That one command found the real usage immediately. **(4)** No further "fix" was needed for the detection gap itself — the fix was doing the correct grep, which is now what step 1 of this task's own instructions establish as the standard for retiring a token. **(5)** The generalizable lesson: a CSS custom property can be consumed two ways — through a derived class (`.tile-rust`, checkable by grepping the class name) or directly via `var(--token-name)` in an inline style or a completely unrelated file (checkable only by grepping the raw token name itself, everywhere, not just in the file where it was defined). Verifying "is this token orphaned" requires the SECOND grep specifically; checking only the derived class, or only the definition site, leaves exactly this class of cross-feature breakage undetected. Worth remembering for every future token retirement in this codebase, not just this one.

### The Rust->Success fix (previous entry) itself broke, the same day, because its own conflict list was missing a whole category
**(1)** Fixing the Rust collision, I checked Success against every OTHER ACTOR'S colour (Accent/Indigo/Teal) and all 8 Home tile tokens before accepting it — a real, computed check, not a guess — and it cleared all of them with a comfortable 28.8° margin. That felt like the complete conflict set: every other name-badge, plus the one other coloured-tile surface this app has. **(2)** Aadi reported a real, reachable scenario: a STAFF-packed order shows Mukesh's actor badge sitting directly next to a green "Packed" entry-type badge on the same History row, both rendered in the same colour. No error, no crash — just two differently-meaning badges an actual person could no longer tell apart, on a screen that's supposed to be building the OPPOSITE of that. **(3)** The conflict list I'd built for the first fix only covered surfaces that render a colour NEXT TO an actor badge on OTHER screens (Home tiles) and other actor badges on the SAME screen (the other three names) — it never included History's OWN entry-type badges (`TYPE_BADGE_CLASSES`), which render on the exact same row, immediately above the actor badge, every single time.
