**A component library grown from a design brief, not built up front** — `ConfirmModal.jsx` is the first component in this project built directly from `07_UI_DESIGN_BRIEF.md`'s design-token spec (§3.4's "Confirm modal" description) before any screen that used it existed yet, rather than being extracted from one screen's markup after the fact. The brief describes this component's exact shape (centered, dark scrim, ~300px card, two full-width buttons) as something meant to be reused "for every mutation that changes persisted data" across the whole app — so building it as a generic, prop-driven component (title/body/confirmLabel/tone, not "the Manage Users deactivate dialog") from the start means the next screen that needs a destructive-action confirmation (e.g. Receive Stock's eventual "Save receipt") can reuse it directly, rather than every screen growing its own copy that then needs to be reconciled later.

**Route-level role gating vs. component-level conditional rendering, and why this project uses both together** — `ProtectedRoute`'s new `requireRole` prop stops the wrong role from ever rendering `ManageUsers`/`SetPin` at all (a redirect, before the component's own code runs); Home's `isOwner &&` / `isStaff &&` checks conditionally show or hide *pieces* of an already-rendering page. Both are doing the same underlying job — keep a UI element from showing to someone it doesn't apply to — but at different granularities: route gating answers "should this whole screen exist for this user," conditional rendering answers "should this particular button/banner exist on a screen this user is already allowed to see." Neither is a security boundary (that's still 100% server-side, per CLAUDE.md's non-negotiable rule) — both exist purely so a legitimate user's screen matches what they're actually allowed to do, instead of showing controls that would just 403 if used.

**Updating context state from outside the provider that owns it, via an exposed function** — `useAuth.jsx`'s `refreshUser` is called from `SetPin.jsx`, a completely different file, to update the `user` object that lives inside `AuthProvider`. This works because React context isn't just for *reading* shared state — the provider can also expose *functions* that know how to update its own internal state (`setUser` is never given out directly; `refreshUser` wraps it), and any descendant component can call those functions to trigger an update, the same way `login`/`logout` already worked before this task. The alternative — SetPin directly manipulating some global variable, or reaching into another component's state — isn't possible in React at all; state is only ever changed by the component that owns it (or a function that component explicitly hands out), which is what keeps "who can change this and how" traceable to one place (`useAuth.jsx`) instead of scattered across every component that happens to need to.

**Deriving a stable output order from a reference list, instead of recording interaction order** — `selectedSizes` in the New-article form is just a plain array that grows in whatever order the staff happens to tap chips (`M` then `S` then `L`, or any other sequence) — but the `sortOrder` values actually sent to the backend come from filtering a separate, fixed canonical list (`ADULT_SIZE_ORDER` / `KIDS_SIZES`) down to whatever's selected, so the stored order is always S, M, L, XL... regardless of click order. This matters because click order is an accident of how someone happened to interact with the screen, not information worth preserving — nothing about "the customer tapped XL before M" should ever affect how sizes later display in a receipt or a stock table. The general technique — keep the interaction-order list for simple toggle logic, but derive presentation/storage order separately from a canonical reference — avoids needing to maintain sorted-insertion logic in the toggle handler itself.

**A function's synchronicity is part of its contract, not an implementation detail invisible to callers** — `handleColorChange` started fully synchronous (the Matched-branch version, built two tasks ago) and became `async` in this task, once one of its branches needed to `await createBundle`. Every existing caller (`onChange={(e) => handleColorChange(e.target.value)}`) kept working unmodified, since JSX event handlers don't care whether the function they call returns a value or a Promise — but the tests built against the OLD, synchronous version broke, because they'd been written assuming "the moment this function's first visible effect appears, everything it does is finished," which stops being true the instant any part of the function can pause on an `await`. The lesson isn't really about `async` specifically — it's that changing whether a function completes its work in one tick or many is a real, breaking change to anything that observes its side effects, even when nothing about its signature or its callers' code has to change at all.

**Native activation behavior (why some DOM interactions can't be faked with a plain dispatched event)** — certain elements have a built-in browser behavior tied to specific real interactions: clicking a checkbox toggles it, clicking a submit button submits its form, clicking a link navigates. This is called "activation behavior," and it's triggered by genuine user interaction (or the element's own `.click()`/`.submit()` methods) — NOT by constructing an `Event` object and calling `dispatchEvent()` with it, even with the exact same event `type`. A manually dispatched event still fires any listeners attached to that event type (so `addEventListener('click', ...)` DOES run), but the browser's own native "and also do the built-in thing this event normally causes" step is skipped. This project has now hit this gap three times — a submit button's click not submitting its form (Manage Users' create-account form, then again on Factory Payables' Record payment/Record amount owed forms), and a checkbox's click not toggling `.checked` — all fixed the same way: call the real method (`.click()`) or dispatch the event on the actual element that owns the native behavior (`submit` on the `<form>`, not a click on its button), rather than trying to reconstruct what the browser would have done as a side effect.

**Verifying a test interaction actually worked, not just that it appeared to** — checking `checkbox.checked === true` immediately after simulating a check is not proof the app's own state updated; it only proves the DOM node's own property reads `true` at that instant, which can be true either because React genuinely processed the change or because the property was set directly and nothing has re-rendered to correct it yet. The reliable check used here: perform an unrelated action afterward that's guaranteed to trigger a re-render (e.g. clicking the sets stepper), then check the value again — a controlled input syncs back to its real React state on every render, so if the "checked" value survives an unrelated re-render, the state genuinely changed; if it reverts, the interaction never touched React at all. This generalizes past checkboxes: any controlled input can be spot-checked this way when there's reason to distrust whether a simulated interaction really landed.

**`Map` for a client-side join, instead of `.find()` inside a loop** — `productsById = new Map(products.map(p => [p.id, p]))` builds a lookup table once, so resolving each stock row's `product.factoryId`/`product.name` afterward is a single `productsById.get(row.productId)` rather than an `products.find(p => p.id === row.productId)` re-scan of the whole products array for every single stock row. The receipt table's earlier grouping-by-productId (a previous task) used `.find()` inside `.reduce()` instead, which was fine there because it was searching the small, already-being-built *output* array (rarely more than a few groups) — this is different: it's joining two independently-fetched lists (potentially hundreds of stock rows against however many products exist) where the lookup side doesn't change while the loop runs, which is exactly the shape `Map` is for. The general rule of thumb: reach for `Map` when the same collection gets looked up repeatedly by ID inside a loop; `.find()` stays fine for one-off or small-N searches.

**Snapshotting a value into a row, instead of joining to its live source** — `Transaction.costPriceSnapshot` stores a COPY of `Product.costPrice` at the exact moment a STOCK_IN happens, rather than the payable calculation looking up `Product.costPrice` fresh every time it runs. This is a deliberate trade against the normal relational-database instinct (don't duplicate data, just join to the single source of truth) — here, duplicating it on purpose is exactly what's needed, because the single source of truth (`Product.costPrice`) is allowed to change over time, and a past receipt's cost must NOT change retroactively just because the join target did. The general pattern — copy a value into a historical record at the moment that record is created, rather than trusting a live foreign-key join to still mean the same thing later — shows up anywhere "what was true when this happened" matters more than "what's true right now": this project already does the same thing conceptually for session-item snapshotting during Receive Stock (Factory/Location/price captured into a local JS object before commit), but `costPriceSnapshot` is the first place it's a real, persisted database column rather than a client-side object field — the same idea, now with actual permanence behind it.

**Idempotent** — an action is idempotent if doing it once and doing it five times in a row leave the system in exactly the same state. `deactivateFactory` is written this way on purpose: calling it on a Factory that's already `isActive: false` doesn't return an error, it just re-applies `isActive: false` (which was already true) and responds `200` with the same shape a fresh deactivation would. The alternative — rejecting a second deactivate attempt with a `409` or similar — would be a perfectly defensible design, but it turns something that shouldn't matter (a staff member double-tapping a button, a network retry after a dropped response, two people deactivating the same record moments apart) into an error the frontend has to specially handle for no real benefit; the end state is identical either way. This is also why `deactivateUser` and all five of the newer deactivate endpoints re-check `findUnique` before updating rather than trusting the `:id` blindly — the *existence* check still 404s on a genuinely missing record, but once the record is found, flipping `isActive` to a value it might already hold is treated as a no-op success, not a special case. `POST` endpoints in this codebase are deliberately NOT idempotent by the same logic in reverse — calling `POST /api/factories` twice with the same name is supposed to fail the second time (`DUPLICATE_FACTORY`), because unlike a boolean flip, "create a new row" has no natural no-op meaning if one already exists with that identity.

**Atomic multi-write operations (why a Transfer is one endpoint, not two calls)** — a Transfer has to do five things: decrement stock at the source, increment it at the destination, create a `Transfer` record, and create two `Transaction` rows. The tempting shape is to let the frontend do it as two ordinary calls — one `STOCK_OUT` at Delhi, one `STOCK_IN` at Gurgaon — since both endpoints already exist. That is broken in a way that only shows up occasionally, which is the worst kind: if the browser loses connection, the tab closes, or the second request errors after the first succeeded, stock has left Delhi and arrived nowhere. It's simply gone, with a completely valid-looking audit trail for the half that worked.

Wrapping all five writes in `prisma.$transaction` makes the whole group **atomic** — the database either applies every one of them or none, with no observable in-between. Concretely, in `transferController.js`, if the guarded decrement finds insufficient stock and throws, the `Transfer` row created moments earlier on the same line of execution is rolled back too; nothing is left behind. Its own test asserts exactly this (a rejected transfer leaves zero orphaned `Transfer` rows, zero orphaned `Transaction` rows, and both locations' quantities untouched), because "it errored" and "it errored *cleanly*" are different claims and only the second one is worth anything.

This connects to two ideas already in this log. It's the same `$transaction` used since the first `POST /api/transactions` (stock update + Transaction insert together), just with more writes inside it. And it's why the operation gets its own dedicated endpoint rather than being composable from existing ones: atomicity is a property of a single server-side database transaction, so it cannot be reconstructed by a client making two separate HTTP calls, no matter how carefully that client is written. If two writes must not come apart, they need one door — which is also why `POST /api/transactions` permanently refuses `TRANSFER_OUT`/`TRANSFER_IN` (see the decision entry above).

**Stored vs. derived values (and what a "snapshot column" actually is)** — one of the recurring shape-decisions in this schema, and the reason `PartyStockReturn` now stores a per-set price rather than a total.

*Prerequisite: what "derived" means here.* A value is **derived** if it can be recomputed from other values you already have. `3 sets × ₹675` is derived — given `qtySets` and a price, the ₹2,025 total can be produced any time, by anyone, with no extra information. A value is **irreducible** if nothing else on the row can reproduce it.

The naive instinct is to store anything you display, because computing feels like work. The problem isn't cost, it's **divergence**: a stored derived value and its own inputs are two copies of one fact, and the moment one is corrected without the other, the row is silently wrong. It still parses, still renders, still looks well-formed — there is no error state, which is what makes it dangerous. Concretely: the old `valueSnapshot` column stored `qtySets × price`. Correct a miscounted return from 3 sets to 2 and the total stays at the 3-set figure forever, with nothing in the system aware of the contradiction. Storing `priceAtReturn` per set makes that impossible to express — there's only one copy of each fact, and the total is computed at every display site from whatever `qtySets` currently says.

*So why store the price at all, if we're avoiding stored values?* Because the price is **not** derivable — and this is the distinction that matters. `Product.sellingPrice` is a live, editable field; it changes when the owner re-prices an article. Look up "what did this return cost" six months later and `sellingPrice` gives you today's number, not the one that was true at the time. The historical value is genuinely unrecoverable unless it was captured at the moment it was true. That's a **snapshot column**: a deliberate copy, taken because the original *moves*.

The rule this produces: **snapshot the inputs that decay, derive everything downstream of them.** `qtySets` doesn't decay (it's right there on the row), so it isn't snapshotted. `sellingPrice` decays, so it is. The total is downstream of both, so it's computed, never stored.

This isn't a new idea in this project — it's the third instance of the same pattern, which is why the shapes match:
- `OrderLineItem.priceAtOrder` — snapshots `sellingPrice` so a later re-pricing never retroactively changes what a Party was actually charged.
- `Transaction.costPriceSnapshot` — the same move on the factory side (rule 82), so a price change never rewrites what was owed for a past receipt.
- `PartyStockReturn.priceAtReturn` — the party-facing return, deliberately named and shaped to match `priceAtOrder` rather than inventing a fourth convention.

It also explains a decision logged elsewhere that would otherwise look inconsistent: the amount payable to a Factory (rule 81) is **computed** from real `Transaction` rows rather than kept as a running stored balance. Same principle. The individual receipts each carry their own snapshot of the price that was true when they happened; the payable figure is derived from them on read. A stored running total would be a fourth copy of a fact already fully determined by the transaction history — and the first time a correction landed, it would quietly stop matching.

**Reusing a UI pattern vs. reusing a UI component (and why Good Returns did both)** — a distinction that only becomes obvious once you've done one and called it the other.

*Prerequisite: what a React component actually is.* A component is a function that takes an object of inputs (**props**) and returns a description of what should appear on screen. React calls it, compares the description against what's currently displayed, and updates only the differences. Because it's just a function, "reusing" it can mean two quite different things.

**Reusing a pattern** means writing new code that follows the same shape as existing code. Good Returns' party picker is this: the filter chips, the grouped-by-location list, "All" selected by default, the trailing "Other" group, collapsing to a compact chosen-row with a Change link — all of that is written out again in `GoodReturns.jsx`, structurally identical to `NewOrder.jsx`. Nothing is literally shared. Change one and the other doesn't move.

**Reusing a component** means both screens call the *same function*. Good Returns' Location picker is this: `CreatableSelect` was living inside `ReceiveStock.jsx`, so it moved to `components/CreatableSelect.jsx` and both files now import the one definition. Fix a bug in it and both screens are fixed at once.

*Why not always the second one?* Because extraction has a real cost: the component has to become genuinely general, and every caller is then coupled to it. `CreatableSelect` was a clean extraction because it was **already** self-contained — it held only its own `adding`/`newName`/`busy`/`error` state and communicated purely through props, with no reference to anything Receive-Stock-specific. Moving it changed no behaviour. The party picker is the opposite: it's woven into six pieces of screen state (`partyChipFilter`, `selectedPartyId`, `partyPickerOpen`, the parties list, its loading status, its error) and the two screens do different things on selection. Extracting *that* would mean inventing an interface for all six, and the interface would have been guessed rather than known.

The rule of thumb this produces: **extract when the thing is already a closed unit; copy the shape when it isn't yet.** A pattern copied twice is cheap to unify later, once you can see which parts genuinely vary. A component extracted too early gets an interface designed around a single caller, and every later caller either distorts it or works around it.

This connects to something already in this log. The same call was made on the backend in this very task, but landed the other way: `applyStockMovement` **was** extracted, because it was already a closed unit (inputs in, one stock row out, no caller-specific state), and because three separate copies of an upsert-then-guarded-update is exactly the situation where a fix lands in one place and silently misses the other two. The distinction isn't frontend-vs-backend at all — it's whether the thing has a real boundary yet.

**"Presence as state" (and why it beats a value-plus-flag pair)** — the shape Pack Order's redesign landed on, worth naming because it will come up again.

*The problem this solves.* Sometimes a piece of state needs to answer two questions at once: "what is the value" and "has this actually been decided yet." The tempting first move is two variables — a value (defaulted to something reasonable) plus a boolean tracking whether a human has engaged with it. That's exactly what Pack Order had: `packingQty` (defaulted to the ordered quantity) and `touchedLines` (a `Set` of which lines a human had adjusted). It works, but the two variables can drift out of the relationship you intend — nothing in the type system stops `packingQty[id]` from existing while `touchedLines` doesn't mention `id`, even though the code only ever means for one to imply something about the other.

*The fix.* Collapse both questions into ONE piece of state, where **the key existing IS the "has this been decided" fact**, and its value is the decision itself. `confirmed: { [lineId]: qtySetsPacked }` — `id in confirmed` answers "decided or not," `confirmed[id]` answers "decided as what." There is no way to construct "decided with no value" or "a value that doesn't count as decided," because those aren't different fields that could disagree, they're the same fact read two ways.

*How this differs from — and relates to — the `idle`/`loading`/`loaded` rule already in this log.* That rule is about not aliasing "hasn't started" onto a boolean's default (`false` meaning both "not loading" and "found nothing empty"). Presence-as-state is the same underlying discipline — don't let one representation's default coincide with a different real state — applied to a *map* rather than a boolean, and to a value staff assign rather than a fetch outcome. The general principle underneath both: **a "not yet decided" state should be structurally distinct from every decided state, not reachable by coincidence through some other value's default.**

*When NOT to reach for this.* It only fits when "undecided" and "decided as the default value" are actually the same outcome you'd otherwise be tracking with a separate flag. If the two facts are genuinely independent — able to vary on their own axes rather than one gating the other — collapsing them would hide information instead of clarifying it. Good Returns' `reason` field is the counter-example from the same session: reason and quantity aren't a decided/undecided pair, they're two independent facts about one confirmed line, so they stay as separate fields.

**Nested routes, and why the Owner Dashboard needed them** — the first place in this app where one route renders *inside* another.

*What every route in this app looked like before.* `App.jsx` held a flat list: each path mapped to one full-screen component, and every one of those components rendered its own `ScreenHeader` at the top. Navigating from Receive Stock to Live Stock unmounts the entire first screen and mounts the entire second. That's correct for a phone — each screen genuinely is the whole view.

*Why the dashboard can't work that way.* It has a persistent rail and header wrapping a content pane that swaps. If `/dashboard/orders` and `/dashboard/parties` were two flat routes, each would have to render its own copy of the rail, and clicking between them would tear down and rebuild that rail every time — losing scroll position, re-running any state it held, and flickering.

*The mechanism.* A `<Route>` can have children, and the parent renders `<Outlet />` wherever the child's content belongs:

```jsx
<Route path="/dashboard" element={<ProtectedRoute requireRole="OWNER"><DashboardLayout /></ProtectedRoute>}>
  <Route index element={<Overview />} />
  <Route path="orders" element={<ComingSoon title="Orders" … />} />
</Route>
```

`DashboardLayout` renders the rail, the header, and `<Outlet />`. React Router matches the URL against parent *and* child, mounts the parent once, and swaps only whatever `<Outlet />` resolves to. `index` is the child shown when the URL is exactly the parent's path — it's what `/dashboard` (no trailing segment) renders, and it's a distinct concept from a path of `""`.

*Two consequences worth understanding, because they're the actual reason this shape was chosen:*

1. **The auth gate is inherited.** `ProtectedRoute requireRole="OWNER"` sits on the parent, so every child is gated by construction. A future dashboard page added as a child *cannot* accidentally ship ungated — there's no line to forget. Compare the flat list, where each of Manage Users / Factory Payables / Article Pricing repeats its own `requireRole`, and the protection is only as good as whoever adds the next one remembering.
2. **`NavLink` needs `end` on the index route.** `NavLink` marks itself active when the current URL *starts with* its `to`. `/dashboard` is a prefix of `/dashboard/orders`, so without `end`, the Overview link stays highlighted on every child page — two rows lit at once. `end` restricts the match to an exact one. This is the same class of off-by-one as a prefix `startsWith` check where an exact comparison was meant.

This connects to the `ProtectedRoute` note already in this log: it's a UX guard, never a security boundary. Nesting makes it harder to forget on the client, but the real enforcement is still `requireRole('OWNER')` on `GET /api/dashboard/overview` — a STAFF user who edits their way past the router still gets a 403 and an empty shell, which is exactly the intended outcome.

**Additive migration (and why a column default is the whole trick)** — a schema change that only *adds* something, so every row and every line of code that existed before it keeps working untouched. Building on the **Migration** entry above: the risk in any migration is the rows that already exist, because they were written under the old rules and nobody is going to hand-edit them. A column with a `DEFAULT` sidesteps that entirely — Postgres fills the value in for every existing row as part of the `ALTER TABLE`, so there is no "old row" and "new row" distinction afterward. `ProductSize.qty Int @default(1)` is exactly this: the migration is one line (`ALTER TABLE "ProductSize" ADD COLUMN "qty" INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 1`), and all 72 pre-existing rows came out with `qty = 1`.

The reason the default has to be *1* specifically, not 0 or null, is that it has to make the new formula agree with the old one. Pieces-per-set used to be `COUNT(rows)`; it became `SUM(qty)`. Those two are the same number precisely when every `qty` is 1 — so choosing 1 as the default is what makes the change invisible to every article that doesn't repeat a size. A default of 0 would have silently valued the entire existing catalogue at zero pieces per set. This is worth internalising as the general shape: an additive migration is only genuinely safe when the default value makes the new logic reproduce the old logic's answer for existing data, and the way to be sure of that is to measure it (snapshot before, recompute after, compare) rather than to reason it through, because the cost of being wrong is silently incorrect money rather than a visible crash.

Contrast with the non-additive alternative that was rejected here: representing a repeated size as two `ProductSize` rows would have required dropping a `@@unique` constraint — a structural change, not an additive one, with no default value available to make it safe, and it would have removed an existing guard against accidental duplicates as a side effect of permitting deliberate ones.

**Relation filter (filtering a query by a field on a *related* table)** — prerequisite: the ORM and schema entries above, plus the idea of a foreign key (a column on one table holding the id of a row in another — `Transaction.userId` points at a `User`). Ordinarily a `where` filters on columns of the table you're querying: `where: { type: 'STOCK_IN' }` filters Transactions by their own `type` column. A *relation* filter instead asks a question about the row on the other end of a foreign key. In `historyController.js` we now write:

```js
prisma.transaction.findMany({
  where: { type: 'STOCK_IN', user: { role: 'STAFF' } },
})
```

`user` is not a column on `Transaction` — it's the relation defined in `schema.prisma` (`user User @relation(fields: [userId], references: [id])`). Prisma turns `user: { role: 'STAFF' }` into a SQL join against `User` and filters on *its* `role` column. This matters because it means the database never returns the excluded rows at all, rather than us fetching everything and discarding some in JavaScript afterwards — which is both wasteful and, for a permissions rule, the difference between data that was never sent and data that was sent and then hidden. The relation's *name* differs per table here (`createdBy` on Order, `changedBy` on OrderAdjustment, `user` on Transfer/Transaction, `correctedBy` on the two correction tables) even though the question is identical, which is why `actorScope(relationName, viewerRole)` takes the name as a parameter and builds `{ [relationName]: { role: 'STAFF' } }` with a computed key. Connects to: the `select` concept (which *columns* come back) — `where` decides which **rows**, `select` decides which **fields** of them.

**Fail-closed vs fail-open (and why an allowlist beats a denylist for permissions)** — prerequisite: nothing beyond "a filter decides what a user is allowed to see." A permission check can be written two ways that look equivalent and behave identically *while the code is correct*:

```js
entries.filter((e) => e.actorRole === 'STAFF')   // allowlist — show only what's confirmed safe
entries.filter((e) => e.actorRole !== 'OWNER')   // denylist  — show anything not known unsafe
```

They diverge precisely when something unexpected appears. If a future source is added and its query forgets to `select` the actor's `role`, then `e.actorRole` is `undefined`. Under the denylist, `undefined !== 'OWNER'` is `true`, so the entry is **shown to staff** — a permission leak caused by an unrelated omission. Under the allowlist, `undefined === 'STAFF'` is `false`, so it's **hidden** — the same omission produces a missing entry someone will notice and report, rather than a silent disclosure nobody sees. "Fail-closed" names this property: when the system doesn't know, it denies. It's the same instinct behind this project's rule that `cost_price` is never *selected* for a STAFF request rather than selected-then-stripped — the safe default is to withhold and require a positive reason to release, not to release and require a positive reason to withhold. Concrete example in this codebase: the rule 104 backstop filter at the end of `listHistory`, which deliberately re-checks every merged entry even though the seven `where` clauses upstream should already have made it a no-op.

**Props (React) — passing a value *into* a component to change how it behaves** — prerequisite: the component concept (a function that returns UI). A prop is an argument to that function, written as an attribute where the component is used. Until now this project's components mostly took props that were *data* (`<ScreenHeader title="Factory Payables" />`). `inDashboard` is a different, useful shape: a prop that selects between two ways of rendering the *same* content.

```jsx
// Definition — a default value, so callers that don't pass it get the old behaviour unchanged.
export default function FactoryPayables({ inDashboard = false }) {
  const content = ( /* …everything the screen shows… */ );

  if (inDashboard) return content;              // dashboard supplies its own chrome
  return <div className="page"><ScreenHeader … />{content}</div>;  // mobile draws its own
}

// Two call sites, two shells, one component:
<Route path="/factory-payables"           element={<FactoryPayables />} />            {/* mobile */}
<Route path="/dashboard/factory-payables" element={<FactoryPayables inDashboard />} /> {/* dashboard */}
```

Three things worth noticing. **`inDashboard` with no value means `inDashboard={true}`** — JSX shorthand for boolean props, the same shorthand HTML uses for `disabled`. **The `= false` default is load-bearing**: the mobile route passes nothing, so without a default the prop would be `undefined` and the component would still work by luck rather than by design; the default makes "unchanged mobile behaviour" an explicit promise. **Building `content` as a variable first** is what keeps the two branches honest — the alternative (writing the markup twice, once per branch) is two copies that drift apart the first time someone edits one.

The judgement call this encodes: a prop like this is right when the *behaviour* is genuinely identical and only the surrounding frame differs. When two surfaces need genuinely different layouts and interactions, separate components are better — which is why `dashboard/ArticlePricing.jsx` exists as its own file rather than as `<ArticlePricing inDashboard />`. Connects to: the conditional-rendering idea already used throughout this app (`{open && <div>…</div>}`) — this is the same technique applied to a whole component's wrapper instead of one nested element.

**Composite string keys (using `${a}:${b}` as a single Map/Set key instead of nesting)** — prerequisite: the accordion state pattern already logged elsewhere in this file (a `Set` of ids, toggled by add/delete, used for Live Stock's `expandedFactories`, Bill Order's `expandedArticles`, and now the Orders page's new per-order article accordion). That pattern works cleanly when there's only one dimension to identify a section by — one Set of article ids, because only one order's articles are ever open at a time in those screens. The Orders page is different: it shows *many* orders at once, each capable of having its own several expanded articles, and two different orders can easily contain the same `productId` (the same real-world article, ordered by two different parties). A single `Set` of bare `productId`s would conflate them — expanding article 6023 on one order would incorrectly mark article 6023 "open" on every other order too.

The fix used here is a composite key: `` `${orderId}:${productId}` ``, still stored in one flat `Set`, exactly like every other accordion state in this app:

```js
function toggleArticle(orderId, productId) {
  const key = `${orderId}:${productId}`;
  setExpandedArticles((prev) => {
    const next = new Set(prev);
    if (next.has(key)) next.delete(key);
    else next.add(key);
    return next;
  });
}
```

The alternative would be a nested structure — `{ [orderId]: Set<productId> }` — which is a genuinely different shape needing genuinely different update code (you'd have to immutably copy the outer object *and* the inner Set on every toggle, instead of just the one Set here). The composite key avoids that entirely: from the `Set`'s point of view, `"orderA:6023"` and `"orderB:6023"` are just two unrelated strings, so the *existing* single-`Set` toggle logic needs no changes at all — only the string passed into it changes. This is a small, reusable trick worth recognising elsewhere: whenever "the same id can legitimately mean two different things depending on some outer context," concatenating the outer context into the key is often simpler than reaching for a nested data structure.

**Eager vs. deferred writes — "when does this actually hit the database?"** This is a design question every form in this app quietly answers, and Receive Stock had been answering it the expensive way. An **eager** write happens the moment you enter something: type an article number, set its sizes, press "Create article", and a real `Product` row exists a few milliseconds later. A **deferred** write holds everything in the browser's own memory (React state) and sends it only when the person commits the whole thing — here, "Save receipt".

Eager is genuinely simpler to write, which is why it's such a natural first version. Every record has a real database id straight away, so anything downstream that needs one (creating a `Bundle` needs a real `productId`; creating a `Transaction` needs a real `bundleId`) just works. Deferred means inventing placeholder identities and reconciling them later — real extra machinery.

The cost of eager is that **the database ends up recording intentions rather than events.** Someone starts typing a new article, gets interrupted, and closes the app — a `Product`, its `ProductSize` rows, maybe a `Color` and a `Bundle` all persist forever, describing stock that was never received. Nothing marks them as abandoned; they're byte-for-byte identical to real records. They show up in article lookups, in the colour picker, in Live Stock at zero sets. Deferring turns "I was in the middle of typing this" into something that costs nothing when abandoned, which is what it should cost.

The practical rule this project now follows: **write at the moment the user commits, not at the moment the UI has enough information to write.** Those two moments feel similar while building a form and are completely different in use.

**Idempotency, and why the deferred version needs it** — an operation is *idempotent* if doing it twice leaves the same result as doing it once. Deferred creation makes this necessary rather than optional, for a reason that isn't obvious up front: the same record can legitimately be asked for more than once.

Three ways it happens in Receive Stock, all real:
- One receipt has four colour lines for the same brand-new article. That's four lines each saying "this Product needs to exist" — it must be created **once**.
- A save partly fails and the person presses "Save receipt" again. The retry re-runs creation for records that already exist from the first attempt.
- Someone else creates the same article or colour in the gap between the form being filled in and saved.

The pattern used here has two layers. First, a **cache** shared across one save run, keyed on the record's *real identity* (factory + article number; the lowercased colour name) rather than on any id — so repeated lines collapse to one create. Second, every step is **look-up-first or recover-on-409**: try to find it, create only if genuinely absent, and if the server says "already exists" (HTTP 409 Conflict), go read it rather than treating that as failure. The second layer matters because the first only knows about *this* run — it can't know about the previous failed attempt or about another person's work.

One detail worth carrying forward: this app's `POST /api/colors` and `POST /api/bundles` return a 409 **without** the existing row's id. So "recover from a duplicate error" necessarily means making another read request. That isn't universal — plenty of APIs return the conflicting record — but it's a thing to check rather than assume, because assuming it would produce code that looks like it handles the conflict and actually can't.

**`useRef` vs `useState`, and the one case where only a ref works** — both hold a value across renders. The difference: changing state schedules a re-render and the new value is only visible on the *next* one; changing a ref (`ref.current = x`) re-renders nothing and is visible **immediately**.

Normally that makes state the right default — the screen is supposed to update. But there's a specific situation where state cannot work, and Receive Stock hits it exactly. `Combobox` creates a colour like this:

```js
const created = await onCreate(trimmed);   // our handleCreateColor runs
onChange(created.id);                      // ...and this runs in the SAME tick
```

`handleCreateColor` calls `setPendingColors([...prev, color])`, then `onChange` immediately triggers `handleColorChange`, which needs to look that colour's *name* back up. But no re-render has happened in between — `pendingColors` still holds the previous render's array, and the lookup finds nothing, producing a staged row with a blank colour name. So the colour is stored in **both**: a ref for the same-tick read, and state so it renders in the picker list.

Carrying the same data twice looks redundant and is worth a comment explaining why (there is one in the code). The recognisable signal for needing this: *code that writes a value and reads it back before React has had a chance to re-render.* That's usually inside one event handler, or across an `await` that resolves synchronously — and it's a real bug that a passing UI test can easily miss, because the blank value renders as empty space rather than an error.

**Why some actions defer and others commit immediately — "whose authority is this, and how long does it last?"** Receive Stock defers almost everything: typing an article number, naming it, picking sizes, inventing a colour, even pressing "Add to receipt" all write nothing until "Save receipt". Reactivating an archived article follows that rule too — *unless* the person also changes its price, in which case both the price change and the reactivation commit immediately. That looks like an inconsistency and isn't; it falls out of a distinction worth naming.

Deferring is about **intent**. Everything on that screen up to "Save receipt" is someone describing what they're about to do. Intent costs nothing to abandon, and abandoning it should leave no trace — that's the whole reason the deferred-creation work exists.

A PIN is about **authority**, and authority is granted for one specific act at one specific moment. When an owner types their PIN, the server verifies it against `priceEditPinHash`, and that verification authorises *this* price change, now. Holding that in browser state until some later, unrelated button ("Save receipt", which might be several articles and several minutes away) would mean stretching a point-in-time authorisation across an open-ended window — and the PIN would have to be kept in memory that whole time to replay it. This app already answers this question consistently everywhere else: Article Pricing's inline price edit and Factory Payables' payment/debit forms all commit the instant the PIN is confirmed. Receive Stock's price path matches them rather than matching its own neighbours on the same screen.

The practical rule: **defer things that describe intent; commit things that exercise authority.** When the two meet in one interaction, the authority half wins, and the UI's job is to say so out loud — which is why this form carries an explicit "unlike the rest of this screen, this saves straight away" line, and why the confirmation afterwards says the change didn't wait for "Save receipt". A person who has learned that this screen defers everything would otherwise be reasonably surprised.

Worth noting the failure ordering this produces, since it's a real design consideration and not just theory. The price path makes two calls (prices, then reactivate — `PATCH /api/products/:id` cannot flip `isActive`). If the second fails, the article stays archived at its new price, and the receipt line still carries its deferred reactivation intent — so saving the receipt still finishes the job. The immediate path degrades into the deferred one. Choosing that order deliberately is the difference between a partial failure that self-heals and one that leaves an article live at a stale price.

**Pure functions, and why they were worth reaching for even inside a React component — "same input, same output, nothing else involved."** A function is *pure* when its return value depends only on its arguments, and calling it changes nothing else (no state update, no network call, no reading `Date.now()` or `Math.random()`). History's `articleGroupFor(entry)` and `locationGroupFor(entry)` (added for the "Group by" feature) are exactly this: given the same entry object, they always return the same `{ key, label }` pair, and calling them does nothing but compute and return.

This connects to a concept already in this log — [[async / await]] and the general idea of a function's *contract*. An `async` function's contract includes "this might take time and might fail"; a pure function's contract is the opposite and simpler: "this cannot fail, cannot be slow, and cannot depend on anything except what you handed it." `resolvedProduct`, `fetch`, anything touching `prisma` — all impure, all needing `await`, all capable of failing. `articleGroupFor` needs none of that.

Purity is what made a genuinely useful shortcut possible during testing: no real `GOOD_RETURN`/`RECEIPT_CORRECTION`/`TRANSFER_CORRECTION` entries existed in the dev database, so a real-browser test could not exercise the branch of `articleGroupFor` that handles them (the "not order-level, no article field" fallback). Because the function is pure, it doesn't need a rendered page, a logged-in user, or a database at all to test — copying its exact body into a plain Node script and calling it with a hand-written object shaped like `{ type: 'GOOD_RETURN' }` tests the REAL function, not a simulation of it, since a pure function has no hidden dependency on its surroundings that a standalone copy could fail to reproduce. This is a general pattern worth reusing: when a piece of display logic is annoying or slow to reach through the full UI (a rare entry type, an edge case buried behind several form steps), check whether the logic it depends on is pure — if it is, testing it directly is not a lesser substitute for a browser test, it's a more precise one, because it isolates exactly the one thing being verified from everything else that has to go right for a browser test to even reach that code.

The general shape worth remembering: **prefer pure functions for anything that's "just compute a value from what I already have."** They're trivial to test in isolation, safe to call as many times as convenient (React re-running a component function on every render is exactly why this matters — an impure "helper" called during render could double-fire a side effect), and their correctness doesn't depend on timing, network state, or what ran before them.
